By bridging neuropsychology and mechanistic interpretability, we demonstrate that LLMs, functionally analogous to biological brains, rely on a densely entangled architecture that can be systematically mapped through clinically-informed interventions.

Target Regeneration Shrinks the Gains of Draft-Training Objectives in Speculative Decoding

Jungseob Lee, Hogun Seong, Euijun Choi, Sugyeong Eo

cs.CL, cs.LG, https://github.com/js-lee-AI/TargetRegeneration

Speculative decoding accelerates large language models by verifying drafted tokens in parallel. New draft-training objectives are usually credited with the acceptance they add over cross-entropy on externally written responses, yet many pipelines let the target rewrite them. We ask how much of that gain survives. With a vision-language target and a block drafter, we train three objectives on four versions of one corpus: cross-entropy on corpus tokens, token-level distillation from the target, and distillation gated to positions that would survive verification. Distillation and the gate add large gains on external responses, but after greedy regeneration, distillation adds a smaller gain and the gate little or none. The shrinkage persists across data orders, position weights, verifiers, decoding temperatures and, for distillation, initializations and learning rates. Most of it remains at matched supervised length and at each objective's best measured training length. A second vision corpus shows the shrinkage, as does a text-only replication, for distillation also with an autoregressive drafter. Objective gains should therefore be measured against a target-regenerated baseline and across training lengths. Our code is available at https://github.com/js-lee-AI/TargetRegeneration.

Our Own Jev: Typed Decisions in One Forward Pass, from an In-House Judge to Open LoRA Models

Yuchuan Weng, Chi Liu, Xin Chen, Srinivasan Manoharan

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.IR, cs.LG

A typed decision maps evidence, a question and a fixed set of options to a probability for every option, computed in one forward pass, by reading a language model's logits or a small head over its hidden states, instead of generating and parsing text. The commercial system Jev popularised this System~1 interface, and open reproductions have followed. We report two uses of the pattern. \emph{Part I} describes an in-house System~1 judge that triages about 148K agentic coding session records for a training corpus. It replaces a frontier LLM judge that cost on the order of \$1,000 per pass and whose stored labels matched a fresh two-judge consensus on only 26.9\% of the sessions where the two judges agreed; a self-hosted frozen backbone with a rank-16 LoRA adapter and a pointer head answers three typed questions in one forward pass, judges 1,000 sessions in about three minutes of one GPU, about \$0.06 at a reference rate and roughly 1\% of the frontier judge's cost, and keeps every session inside the organisation. \emph{Part II} asks whether a readout that adds no parameters can be competitive and whether training one helps. Applying the public Eikos recipe and corpus of about 25K decisions to three further backbones (1B, 2B and 35B), we read the frozen output layer of a LoRA-adapted decoder. On the 111 public hard items of JevBench our single-seed 35B, developed with those items in view, scores 79.3\%, not significantly different from the commercial reference (73.0\%, unadjusted $p=0.12$) and ahead of five open systems after Holm--Bonferroni correction; our 2B is ahead of all four kev checkpoints on the leaderboard. A single-seed ablation finds no detectable benefit from training the 26 letter rows of the output layer, and a retrain on a later corpus release moves the 2B's hard-tier accuracy by 11.7 points, which shows how little small evaluations resolve.

A Large-Scale Evaluation of Role-Enhanced Zero-Shot Chain-of-Thought Prompting

Ibrahim Khan, Thai Van Nguyen, Yi Xia, Pratch Suntichaikul, Mustafa Can Gursesli, Daniel Fernandez Galeote, Juho Hamari, and Ruck Thawonmas

cs.CL, cs.AI

This paper proposes Role-Enhanced Zero-Shot Chain-of-Thought (RE-ZS-CoT), a prompting framework that systematically integrates domain-specific expert roles to enhance reasoning in large language models (LLMs) without task-specific examples. We assess two versions of our approach. In the first version, static role selection, we evaluate eight predefined expert roles across arithmetic, commonsense, and reading comprehension benchmarks to determine their impact on reasoning performance. In the second version, dynamic role selection, each model autonomously selects the single most suitable role for each benchmark, enabling adaptive task-role alignment without manual specification. Across 20 LLMs and seven benchmarks, RE-ZS-CoT frequently surpasses zero-shot and zero-shot-CoT baselines, achieving performance improvements of up to 44 percentage points. The results reveal a strong correspondence between static and dynamic versions; the benchmarks where static roles yielded improvements were almost always those where dynamic roles also outperformed baselines, though static selection achieved peak performance. Ablation studies further show that unconventional roles degrade performance, underscoring the necessity of task-role alignment. The framework's scalability is validated across model sizes (2b-14b parameters), with smaller models like Granite3.3-8b achieving remarkable proficiency in arithmetic reasoning. RE-ZS-CoT advances zero-shot prompting by unifying role specialization with chain-of-thought reasoning, offering a practical, resource-efficient solution for improving LLM capabilities across diverse domains.

Multi-level Encoder with Global Topic for Task-oriented Dialogue Summarization

Zhuoqi He, Peijie Huang, Yuhong Xu, Youming Peng, Mingzhi Xu, Xinyang Lin

cs.CL

Task-oriented dialogue summarization aims to automatically extract key information to generate domain summaries to improve service efficiency and quality. Task-oriented dialogue is inherently logical and surrounds specific topic. How to effectively capture the dialogue topic and the most salient information becomes one of the major challenges of this task. In this paper, we propose a task-oriented dialogue summarization model utilizing a multi-level encoder with global topic (MLEGT). This model discovers temporal and spatial dependencies through a multi-level encoder, while simultaneously obtaining a simple yet effective global topic in the process of graph construction. It not only captures the crucial details but also breaks the long-range limit and discover the intrinsic structure across the dialogue. Then, a global topic guided pointer mechanism is added into the summrizer to capture ignored but important information, which is helpful to improve the accuracy of the summary. A comprehensive study of two public datasets, including a real-world large-scale Chinese police interrogation dataset and a medical reporting service dataset proves the superiority of our method on several strong baselines.

GRAFT: Restoring the Missing Sampling Interface in Continuous Diffusion Language Models

Haoning Yang, Lili Pan

cs.CL, cs.AI

Continuous diffusion language models generate a whole sequence in parallel, never drawing a single word at any intermediate step; only the final step turns embeddings into text. Models such as ELF now report autoregressive-level quality at a fraction of the sampling steps of discrete-token diffusion models, and this design principle -- no step-wise discretization -- is what their own ablations credit for the gain. We show that this same principle carries a hidden cost: a collapse of the lexical long tail. Measured against held-out human text, ELF's output matches the human rate on common words almost exactly, yet its rarest words appear at only 0.30x the human rate, a gap that does not close from 105M to 652M parameters and that no sampler setting these models expose can reach. A same-size GPT-2, in contrast, spans zero to above the human rate by its decoding rule alone. We trace the difference to what continuous diffusion models lack: with no step that ever draws a discrete word, every step is instead driven by an average over the candidates still in play, and averaging a rare candidate together with the common word competing for its slot lands on the common word. An autoregressive model avoids this because its decoding rule samples one candidate rather than averaging over all of them. We propose GRAFT, a training-free method that grafts this missing choice back onto a frozen model: while the model is still undecided, GRAFT commits the rare words the average would otherwise discard, then regenerates the sequence from scratch with those commitments held in place. Across three ELF scales and on LangFlow, an independent continuous dLLM, GRAFT restores the rare-word share of generated text from 0.42-0.77x the human rate to 0.82-1.10x, a relative gain of 34-109%, and moves the entire word-frequency profile closer to human on every model tested, by 2.7x on ELF-B and 12x on LangFlow.

Why is AI-generated language so hard to understand?

Thomas Hikaru Clark, Matias B. Martin, Kyle Mahowald

cs.CL, cs.AI

There is a widespread sense that AI-generated text has recently become harder to understand. In this study, we investigate a set of psycholinguistic features that are known to correlate with human processing difficulty (e.g., object relative clauses, long syntactic dependencies, low-frequency n-grams). Using a novel corpus of AI-generated text across diverse domains, we find that AI models use these difficult features more often than paraphrases generated by round-trip translation. Compared with human writing, all nine frontier and near-frontier models use more inanimate subjects with animate verbs and more low-frequency n-grams, and most also use longer dependencies, more stacked nouns, and more reduced object relatives in conversation; we also demonstrate substantial variation across model families. We propose and test a hypothesis as to why: while human language is optimized for successful communication, AI-generated text is increasingly optimized for other goals like math, code, and reasoning, in which there is a pressure for \textit{information density} rather than successful communication. We provide a proof-of-concept for this hypothesis by fine-tuning a language model under a length penalty, which leads to a divergence from human-like linguistic behavior. Our findings highlight the axes of misalignment between linguistic processing demands in humans and LLMs, and point toward possible remedies.

Carving Radiology Reports at Their Evidential Joints: Anatomical Region Sets as Generation Units

Jinwei Yang, Junhao Chen, Jianwei Zhao, Zhonghan Xu, Hong Cheng, Ling Wang

cs.CL, cs.CV

Organizing radiology reports into independently generated units offers a way to generate fine-grained clinical content and bind it to visual evidence. However, existing methods typically adopt generation-unit structures not directly specified in natural reports or their annotations, introducing additional modeling compromises. We propose RadCarve, which instead defines generation units directly from the anatomical region sets in existing statement-region correspondences, unifying unit formation with visual evidence selection. RadCarve's planner uses composition-specialized query banks to jointly predict unit existence and region composition from the image, iteratively refining both with regional evidence aggregated according to current membership predictions. The generator independently produces each unit's text conditioned on the joint visual evidence of its predicted region set. Compared with strong baselines, RadCarve improves CXRFEScore by 9.4% in unit generation given region sets and achieves 2.4 times the end-to-end binding F1. It also achieves leading report-generation results on Chest ImaGenome and PadChest-GR, using fewer than one-sixth as many parameters as large-scale baselines.

TrainBridge: Agentic NPU Training Optimization Guided by GPU Compilation Evidence

Yupeng Chen, Kunxiang Zhao, Xingjian Wang, Yue Zhao, Yijing Wu, Ye Liu, Kai Li, Congliang Chen, Ruoyu Sun

cs.CL, cs.AI

We study how to improve training efficiency on Ascend NPUs. In the NPU training programs we study, framework-level compilation optimizations such as operator fusion are only partially applied, leaving fragmented computation and avoidable execution overheads. Profiling-guided tuning can address these inefficiencies, but it often requires costly trial and error. To make training on NPUs more efficient, we propose TrainBridge, an agentic approach for transferring GPU optimization guidance to NPU training. TrainBridge uses the highly optimized execution produced by mature GPU compilation stacks as a task-specific reference for NPU tuning. It extracts optimization intents from the GPU training execution to guide NPU program rewrites and further refines the rewritten program through iterative profiling. Our GPU-to-NPU migration experiments show that TrainBridge achieves an average 1.7x speedup across four workloads covering LLM pretraining, image classification, and vision-language training, and an average speedup of nearly 2.0x across 27 Modded-NanoGPT speedrun records with highly optimized GPU implementations.

LTEM: LLM-Friendly Table-Enhanced Markdown for Table Understanding and Generation

Eunbin Kim, Hyeonseok Kang, Sangkeun Jung

cs.CL, cs.AI

Tables in real-world documents, including scientific papers, financial reports, and statistical summaries, frequently use merged cells, multi-level headers, and sectioned layouts. Yet the lightweight formats LLMs handle best (Markdown, CSV) cannot express these structures, while expressive formats (HTML, LaTeX) impose excessive token cost and parsing complexity. To enable systematic analysis of this trade-off, we introduce a taxonomy decomposing table structure into six atomic features (F1-F6) and corresponding complexity levels (L1-L6), and construct STB (Synthetic Table Benchmark), a controlled benchmark with 1,089 tables systematically distributed across complexity levels. Using this framework, we propose LTEM (LLM-friendly Table-Enhanced Markdown), a formally specified, minimal extension of Markdown that explicitly encodes spans and hierarchical headers. Experiments across five state-of-the-art LLMs show that LTEM achieves accuracy comparable to HTML, offers a favorable accuracy-token trade-off relative to HTML and LaTeX, and produces substantially fewer invalid responses than LaTeX; for table generation, LTEM is comparable to MultiMarkdown though HTML is best. We release our taxonomy, benchmark, and format specification to support future research on LLM-table interaction.

norregel: a preregistered benchmark of dated numeric values in Norwegian law

Eirik Botten Nicolaysen (EcoDeco AS)

cs.CL, 10.5281/zenodo.23422302, (versions, v1, 10.5281/zenodo.23422303,, v1.1, 10.5281/zenodo.23432124,, v1.2, 10.5281/zenodo.23434782,, 2.0, 10.5281/zenodo.23437444,, 2.1, 10.5281/zenodo.23502580)., Items, are, withheld

norregel asks language models for numeric values in Norwegian statutes, regulations and parliamentary decisions (NOK amounts, percentages, multiples of the National Insurance basic amount (G) and age limits), each at a stated date. The 916 items were extracted mechanically from Lovdata's open data, and each carries the dated history of its provision, so an answer can be classed as correct, outdated, wrong or uninterpretable. We report a first and a second preregistered run on the same items. In the first run (five models, listed publicly only after local analysis), H1, that outdated answers are more frequent for values that took effect after a model's cutoff, could not be tested in substance, and H2 and H3 were inconclusive; Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 4.6 answered 46.7% and 40.2% of the 880 primary items correctly. The second run was preregistered and published before any of its calls, and eight models were analysed. Its pooled primary test supported H1 under the preregistered decision rule (d = 17.8 percentage points, 95% interval [7.7, 28.9], p = 0.0001). In the preregistered sensitivity analysis S8, which counts as outdated only the most recent value that differs from the key, d was 3.9 [-3.6, 12.7] (p = 0.1754), an interval that contains 0. H1 was supported in every other preregistered sensitivity analysis, but S8 is the only one that gives both groups one target value, so we do not regard the support for H1 as robust. The share of outdated answers equal to that most recent value (H2) was 56.1% [46.6, 65.0], an interval that contains 0.5, and the pooled test of a lower share correct in Nynorsk than in Bokm{\aa}l (H3) was inconclusive. Several models, among them Haiku 4.5, gave mostly uninterpretable answers. Items and keys are withheld; item IDs, per-answer classes, code and results are on Zenodo.

Understanding Target Probability Reuse in Speculative Decoding

Jungseob Lee, Giyoon Jang, Chailin Nam, Jiwoo Kum, Sihyeon Lee, Kyojun Choo, Jihun Song, Eunseo Song, Chanjun Park

cs.CL, https://github.com/js-lee-AI/TargetRows

Speculative decoding uses a target model to verify a tree of draft tokens in parallel. We study whether the resulting next-token distributions can help select the next tree. For unrestricted target models, we show that guaranteed exact reuse requires a distribution computed at the next round's complete context. We characterize when retaining this distribution requires sacrificing accepted tokens and establish conditions under which this loss persists across decoding rounds. Across six targets, we find that reranking first-token branches with a separately computed distribution at the current context improves expected acceptance over draft ranking, especially with small tree budgets. Drawing the first token from this distribution before allocating the tree further improves expected acceptance over pruning a preselected tree, even at equal mean tree size. In our experiments, both selection rules also decode faster than draft ranking when the separate target pass overlaps standalone drafting. Our findings show that making target probabilities available before tree allocation can improve the efficiency of speculative decoding. Our code is available at https://github.com/js-lee-AI/TargetRows.

C$^2$U in RAG: Compositional Concept Unlearning in Retrieval-Augmented Generation

Yiyang Duan, Fan Wu, Tiantong Wu, Che Wang, Zhiren Gong, Wei Guo, Yang Cao, Wei Yang Bryan Lim

cs.CL

Retrieval-augmented generation can expose unwanted concepts through retrieved evidence even when the generator is moderated. We study query-side concept suppression with a fixed encoder and document index. Compositional Concept Unlearning (C$^2$U) routes a profile-derived direction through an augmented atom bank, adding only a bounded edited-minus-original delta to the raw query. Across five datasets, four encoders, and three seeds, the validation-selected configuration reduces pooled binary target Hit@5 by 4.25 percentage points with non-positive control degradation and target-exposure changes. Under the evaluated query transforms, it suppresses target density without increasing budgeted control-query exposure. Separately, ArXiv four-concept composition improves over an averaged-direction comparator after independent validation gates and first-use test evaluation.

Beyond Binary Labels: Diagnostic Evaluation of LLM Safety with Prompt-Level Rubrics

Hongyu Li, Xinlong Huang, Zhan Yu, Jing Zhao, Zhe Zhao, Juntao Zhong, Xiong Kuang, Rui Yuan, ZhenXiang Yan, Huilin Xu, Haitao Yu, Lilin Wang, Rui Chen, Blaze Chen, Fengzong Lian, Renfen Hu

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CR, https://github.com/Tencent-Hunyuan/DiagSafe

Current LLM safety benchmarks predominantly rely on binary safe or unsafe labels, lacking explanatory depth and suffering from low consistency across automated judges. To address this, we introduce DiagSafe, a diagnostic evaluation framework that moves beyond binary classification by employing prompt-level rubrics. Grounded in globally applicable AI safety policies, each rubric provides explicit criteria along two dimensions: safety, which targets concrete harms with specific exemptions to suppress false positives, and usefulness, which independently evaluates instruction fulfillment. To ensure a robust and realistic evaluation, we construct a bilingual (English-Chinese) benchmark grounded in a comprehensive 84-label safety taxonomy, consisting of two subsets: DiagBase, which covers single- and multi-turn prompts with direct harmful queries and template-based jailbreaks, and DiagAttack, which embeds harmful intents into complex instruction-following tasks to form challenging instruction attacks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DiagSafe significantly enhances the reliability of LLM-as-a-judge evaluation, achieving 95.34% agreement with human annotations -- substantially higher than the 69%-87% achieved by the baselines -- and high inter-judge consistency (Cohen's $\kappa$ = 0.864). Furthermore, applying our framework reveals that leading LLMs differ markedly in their unsafe rates, and each model also exhibits unique vulnerabilities along different dimensions. In addition, we observe that models are particularly susceptible in Chinese and multi-turn contexts. Ultimately, DiagSafe provides a reliable and explainable tool for auditing and optimizing LLM alignment.

MOSAIC: An Omni-Modal Benchmark for Long-Term Memory and Cross-Modal Reasoning in Conversational Agents

Hong Ting Tsang, Boyi Zhang, Haoyu Huang, Jiaxin Bai, Yufei Li, Yisen Gao, Zhongwei Xie, Hongyu Luo, Jeff Z. Pan, and Yangqiu Song

cs.CL, cs.AI

Omni-modal assistants must remember not only what users say, but also what they show and convey. Existing long-term memory benchmarks are mostly text-and-image or report a single end-to-end score, so they cannot locate whether evidence is lost when it is represented, retrieved, delivered, or read. We introduce MOSAIC, a benchmark of 923 questions over persona-grounded histories of 11-24 sessions spanning text, image, video, and audio. MOSAIC constructs targeted spatial, temporal, and prosodic signals rather than decorative media, with the strongest causal validation for spatial information and selector-level validation for annotated prosody, and pairs them with a matched diagnostic protocol that freezes the question, reader, prompt, and budget while intervening on representation, retrieval, and delivery. Matched decomposition shows that representation loss is modality-dependent, residual reading dominates even under gold evidence, and abstention remains weak on these controls: strong answerable scores coexist with failures to refuse. MOSAIC thus turns omni-modal memory evaluation from a leaderboard into a diagnosis of write, retrieve, deliver, and read failures.

Let Agents Design How They Search: Automatic Interface Optimization for Agentic Search

Sangjune An, Jaeyoon Jung, Yumin Choi, Jinheon Baek, Junmo Cho, Sung Ju Hwang, Hae Beom Lee

cs.CL

Agentic search depends on the interface through which a language-model agent accesses and explores a corpus. Existing interface designs are fixed by hand and applied unchanged to every corpus, although effective designs may depend on the corpus. We introduce SearchWeaver, a framework for automatic search interface optimization guided by downstream search performance. A proposer uses execution trajectories and task rewards to jointly revise corpus artifacts, executable tools, and usage instructions. This pro cess optimizes what information is prepared in advance, which operations tools perform, and which decisions remain with the search agent. With GPT-5.4-nano, the optimized interfaces improve answer accuracy from 71.6% to 78.4% on BrowseComp-Plus and average nDCG@10 from 45.7 to 55.0 across four BRIGHT subsets over Direct Corpus Interaction. These findings highlight the value of jointly optimizing corpus artifacts, executable tools, and usage instructions to develop effective search interfaces.

Intergroup Bias Predicts Expressions of Social Judgment and Empathy in the r/AmITheAsshole Reddit Community

Yu Hou, Hal Daum\'e III, Rachel Rudinger

cs.CL, cs.SI

People share and understand others' experiences differently based on their social identities; often, people empathize more when the other person belongs to their ingroup compared to an outgroup. While this intergroup empathy gap is well-studied in social psychology, it remains unclear how it manifests in naturally occurring online social interactions. To this end, we measure how commenters express social judgments, as an indirect outcome of their understanding towards the posters, and empathy in text, based on whether commenters share the poster's gender or are of similar ages. We focus on posts where the poster self-reports these demographics, and analyze 126,716 comments associated with 36,163 posts from 18,111 commenters in r/AmItheAsshole, while controlling for commenter and poster group identities, post features (topic, length, and score), and both commenter- and post-level variance. Our results show that when commenters and posters are ingroup, in either gender or age, commenters are more likely to provide a Not the Asshole verdict and show greater text-based understanding as measured by LLMs. We also observe that older commenters behave differently from younger ones. Furthermore, in a subset of posts where the gender and age of the other party in the conflict are explicitly mentioned, we find that commenter-poster ingroup effects are moderated by the group relationships between the poster and the other party when commenters make social judgments. Our work highlights the importance of intergroup context and social group nuances in understanding online discourse.

Hierarchical Masked Diffusion Language Models

Parul Gupta, Satwik, Avni Aggarwal, Bhavesh Gurnani, Arnab Kumar Mondal, Dinesh Khandelwal, Dinesh Garg, Parag Singla

cs.CL, cs.AI

Masked diffusion language models have emerged as an alternative to autoregressive models, enabling parallel token generation. However, standard masked diffusion language models use a shared noise schedule across positions without explicitly encoding which positions should be resolved first. Many tasks exhibit a natural priority among positions: document titles and section headings can guide the generation of body text, while Sudoku cells determined directly from the initial clues can help resolve more difficult cells. To incorporate this notion of priority (or hierarchy) among token positions, we introduce the Hierarchical Masked Diffusion Language Model (HMDLM), which augments each token position with a hierarchy level available as part of the training data, and requires zero or minimal additional annotations. It factors generation into two passes: the first predicts a hierarchy distribution over masked positions, the second predicts token distributions conditioned on it. Giving each hierarchy level its own noise schedule induces a soft, controllable preference for revealing higher-priority positions earlier while keeping decoding fully parallel. Our formulation is mathematically grounded, and we re-derive the forward and reverse processes and the NELBO for this factorization. Our evaluation shows that for the task of language generation on LM1B, HMDLM reduces perplexity by more than 1.5 points over Anchored Diffusion Language Model (ADLM), a recently proposed strong diffusion baseline; on WikiHow, we see a drop in perplexity of up to 4.4 pts on ADLM and up to 7 pts against MDLM. An LLM judge on WikiHow prefers HMDLM generations (the two variants combined) more than $2\times$ as often as the baseline. On the task of solving Sudoku puzzles requiring multi-step reasoning, it improves solution accuracy by up to $8.6\%$ absolute points over existing diffusion baselines.

Credit Where Credit Is Due: Contrastive Attribution Topology Search for Multi-Agent Systems

Fabian Tritthart, Tom Zehle

cs.CL, cs.MA

An LLM multi-agent system (MAS) is only as good as the agents it chooses to trust: its accuracy and cost hinge on its topology, meaning which agents are included and how they communicate. A single task score, however, does not reveal which agents help or hinder performance. We propose Contrastive Attribution Topology Search (CATS), which searches for compact teams within a fixed supergraph of specialized agents. An LLM attributer compares execution traces across candidate topologies and assigns credit to individual agents. CATS uses these credits to update supergraph edges while progressively pruning weak edges and agents, yielding a small team for deployment. With small open-weight models and matched candidate evaluations, CATS improves mean accuracy on BigCodeBench by 5.3 percentage points over score-only heuristic search while using 54\% fewer deployment tokens. On MBPP, CATS matches the strongest baselines with fewer tokens than the other searches. In a HotpotQA prompt-poisoning stress test, CATS excludes all adversarially prompted agents in all three runs, whereas random search retains some at comparable accuracy. Per-agent credit thus supports both team optimization and the pruning of potentially harmful agents.

Asking What a Director Sees Can Change What Vision-Language Models Choose

Jiaxuan Guo, Xingyu Ren, Kejia Zhang, Jingxin Yang, Youran Sun, Haizhao Yang

cs.CL, cs.CV

Vision-language models are now routinely tested on theory of mind: whether they can work out what another person sees, knows, and means. A common design asks the model to report that perspective and then to act for the person, and reads a correct report beside a wrong action as a failure to use the perspective. That reading rests on a quiet assumption: that asking for the report leaves the action unchanged. We show that it does not. In a rendered Director Task, a partition hides the largest block from a director who asks for "the largest block". Asking first which blocks the director can see raises the share of replies that choose this hidden block by 12 to 88 percentage points on six GPT models and by 17 on Qwen3.8-27B, even though the models mostly report correctly which blocks the director sees. Reasoning traces and prompt rewordings suggest why: the question shifts how the request is read, from the director's view toward the model's own. When the prompt states what the director knows and refers to, fewer than 1% of replies pair a correct report with a hidden-block choice. Any evaluation that elicits a perspective report before the action it scores needs a baseline without the question, and we deliver one: paired prompts that differ only in that question, a design any perspective-taking benchmark can adopt.

OSynth: A Longitudinal, Context-Sensitive Evaluation of Meeting Understanding

Ian Porada, Suraj Tripathi, Chaitanya Animesh, Leda Sari, Chaitanya Narisetty, Austin R. Matthews, Bohan Yu, Ramesh L. Thulasiram, Gregory Sell, Kean Chin, Dmitriy Genzel

cs.CL

Meeting notetakers are increasingly deployed in settings where longitudinal context is available including calendar metadata, prior meetings, emails, slides, and speaker enrollments; however, meeting corpora remain limited in terms of size, domain coverage, and available context. We present OSynth, a large-scale synthetic dataset of simulated organizations comprising 570 hours of meeting audio together with associated artifacts and a latent ground-truth state. In a pairwise comparison, human raters distinguish OSynth meetings from real meeting transcripts with only 56% accuracy based on excerpts of 150-300 words. Across state-of-the-art LLMs, additional context yields significant gains on several entity-sensitive dimensions, demonstrating limitations of contemporary approaches and the potential usefulness of OSynth in developing better-contextualized meeting-understanding systems.

Signals and Queries: Token-Efficient Agentic Context Discovery

Yam Marcovitz

cs.CL

User-facing LLM agents must identify which behavioral policies apply at each turn, but evaluating every policy with an LLM is costly. We introduce Signals and Queries (S&Q), a lightweight first-stage relevance filter developed for Parlant 4.0 that removes at least 30% of candidate policies before downstream evaluation while preserving high recall. For each policy, an LLM generates activating examples and hard negatives offline. An independent logistic classifier trained on their embeddings then predicts relevance using embeddings and vector dot products, with no per-turn LLM calls. This shifts expensive relevance evaluation to a smaller candidate set, while allowing each policy's classifier to be updated independently. Evaluated against 9,144 LLM-judge relevance labels covering 254 $\tau^2$-bench conversation prefixes and 36 airline policies, S&Q achieves higher precision than top-K and cosine-threshold retrieval at every evaluated recall level, with consistent results across two embedding providers. These findings support inexpensive, per-policy classification as a practical first stage for reducing downstream evaluation workload in customer-facing agents.

Seeing the Forest for the Trees: Emergent Agreement from Individual Conversations

Adi Simhi, Yaniv Nikankin, Jonathan Herzig, Idan Szpektor, Yonatan Belinkov, Shay B. Cohen

cs.CL

As LLM agents interact in the wild and shape each other's decisions, understanding how they converge toward agreement becomes important. We study this in multi-turn conversations where two LLMs discuss subjective matters, can agree or disagree, and may change their stances. Using a linear classifier on individual conversation activations, we identify an internal representation of agreement. We find that it varies substantially across turns and conversations and does not reliably track external agreement at the individual conversation level. We therefore move beyond the representation of agreement in individual conversations, by aggregating the agreement representation across conversations. Externally, this aggregate view reveals a simple structure: when pooled across conversations, agreement can be predicted well with a Markov process, with agreement at each turn being a direct function of agreement at the previous turn. Similarly, when internal agreement is aggregated, it couples with the external Markov agreement through a power-law mapping, exposing Markovian dynamics in internal representations. Additionally, we find that after a change of stance, models express agreement externally before their internal representations reflect the same shift. These results reveal a joint structure between internal and external agreement that emerges only when internal features are aggregated across conversations. Code available at https://github.com/technion-cs-nlp/SeeingTheForestForTheTrees.

Policy Central: Detecting Conflicts, Redundancy, and Similarity in Deployed Police Policy

Holly Baker, Michael Kuo, Kyana Pollas

cs.CL, cs.CY

Policy Central is a deployed system that helps police departments find relationships between sections of their written policy: whether two sections conflict, are redundant, are merely similar, or are different. We describe the system and compare two architectures. Retrieve-Judge, a retrieve-then-classify pipeline, runs as a cheap library-wide health signal; Screen-Confirm, a screen-then-confirm cascade, is a selective audit of the documents a reviewer chooses, checking them exhaustively against one another. On a 302-pair benchmark drawn from one department's ~84 real policy documents, given each pair Screen-Confirm reaches 0.795 aggregate recall and 0.885 on Conflict, so current models judge well. Finding the pair is the hard part: Retrieve-Judge's composed recall falls to 0.457, and Screen-Confirm recovers 0.472 (0.615 on Conflict). Today these pairs are found by reading the whole library, nearly 20,000 sections, checked against one another and re-checked after every revision. A scan that returns half of them in minutes, at low cost, and can be re-run on demand changes the economics of policy review even before recall improves.

ChronoSynth: Constraint-Aware Question Synthesis over Temporal Knowledge Graphs

Feng Liang, Weixin Zeng, Runhao Zhao, Mingjun Zhou, Shaoshi Yang, Xiang Zhao

cs.CL, cs.AI

Knowledge graph question generation (KGQG) generates natural-language questions from structured graph evidence and a target answer. When the input graph is temporal, a valid question must also preserve the answer field, time condition, reference event, and temporal order that determine the answer set. Existing KGQG methods and prompting baselines can produce fluent questions that alter these conditions, causing temporal semantic drift. This paper studies temporal KGQG as a constraint-preserving graph-to-question problem and proposes ChronoSynth, a memory-augmented and constraint-aware generation framework. ChronoSynth first constructs a Temporal Intent State to make the intended question semantics explicit. It then retrieves compatible typed question patterns from an indexed pattern memory, adapts them to the input graph, and validates the generated question. Validation feedback guides revisions when a supported inconsistency is detected. Experiments on CRONQUESTIONS and MultiTQ demonstrate state-of-the-art generation performance, with ablations supporting the contributions of its components.

B-CARE: Blind Classwise Assessment of Relative Evidence for Stance Error Ranking

Meysam Sabbaghan, Doina Caragea

cs.CL

Stance classifiers make errors that can be costly to identify under limited human-review budgets. Prioritizing potentially incorrect predictions remains challenging, particularly across heterogeneous base models. We introduce B-CARE (Blind Classwise Assessment of Relative Evidence for Stance Error Ranking), a prediction-blind framework for identifying high-risk stance predictions. Given only a target and text, a fixed LLM verifier assigns ordinal evidence scores to all candidate stance labels without observing the base model's prediction or confidence. B-CARE constructs a comparative risk score by contrasting the strongest alternative-class evidence with the evidence supporting the predicted class. The resulting classwise evidence profile can be reused across multiple base models without training a separate error detector or calibrating risk scores. We evaluate B-CARE on four stance benchmarks and three heterogeneous base LLMs, covering 12 dataset-model settings and 5,206 unique test instances. Evaluation includes error-ranking discrimination (AUROC and AUPRC), selective prediction (E-AURC), and review effectiveness (ErrorCapture at fixed review budgets). B-CARE achieves a mean AUROC of 0.735 and a mean AUPRC of 0.524, capturing 41.06% of errors within the highest-risk 20% of predictions. Although B-CARE does not uniformly outperform strong confidence-based baselines, it provides a reusable, complementary evidence signal for prioritizing potentially incorrect stance predictions across heterogeneous models.

Don't Stop Long-Context Continual Pre-Training Too Early: An Empirical Scaling Law

Ziqing Qiao, Xiaoyue Xu, Sikui Zhang, Yinuo Xu, Bingxiang He, Chaojun Xiao, Xu Han

cs.CL

Long-context continual pre-training (LCCP) is widely used to improve long-context capability, but how its benefits scale with the number of tokens used for LCCP remains unclear. We investigate this question through modeling, monitoring, and validating the benefits of scaling LCCP. First, we establish an empirical additive power law relating long-context performance to model size and the respective token budgets of short-context pre-training and LCCP. Experiments across five model scales and more than one hundred short- and long-context budget configurations show that the fitted law reproduces the observed trends and extrapolates well to held-out model scales. Second, we introduce \textsc{Constant-Last4}, a training and observation protocol that averages the four most recent consecutive checkpoints from a constant-learning-rate run to enable stable monitoring along a single extensible training trajectory. Under this protocol, the same scaling-law form fits well and accurately predicts the LCCP performance trajectory of a 1.22B-parameter model trained on approximately 100B LCCP tokens. Finally, downstream evaluations along this trajectory show continued improvements in long-context performance, with no clear sign of saturation within the evaluated range. These gains persist after both 128K context extension and supervised fine-tuning, with benefits extending to long-context understanding and agentic tasks. Together, these results argue for continuing to scale LCCP rather than stopping early: its gains are stably observable along an extensible trajectory, quantitatively predictable, and transferable to downstream tasks.

Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents

Dongfang Li, Zhuoen Chen, Bonian Jia, Zixuan Liu, Zhengxuan Lu, Can Deng, Yukun Shi, Yuhang Meng, Xin Zhang, Jiahe Huang, Fuhao Li, Junmai Wang, Shunyao Wen, Jingjun Sun, Jiaxu Tian, Jiangling Zhang, Zihan Wang, Liangyou Li, Yitong Yao, Shuangyong Song, Beilun Wang, Pengxu Wei, Hanlin Xu, Chang Liu, Yongxiang Li, Baotian Hu, Xiangyang Ji, Min Zhang

cs.CL, cs.AI

Autonomous agents powered by foundation models can reason, plan, and use tools, but their fixed weights and context limits hinder sustained learning from experience over long-term deployment. While external memory systems attempt to bridge this gap, they mostly act as passive retrieval caches, evaluated primarily by whether they can recall stored facts or past interactions. Whether memory can drive genuine continual learning remains an open question. In this paper, we introduce Memory-Centric Continual Learning (MCCL), arguing that memory should be judged by whether it improves future actions, not merely what it retrieves. MCCL has three main parts. First, a three-axis taxonomy organizes memory by where state is stored (substrates), what form it takes (assets), and how it is managed (policies). This defines a two-loop lifecycle that updates memory state in an inner loop while refining management policies in an outer loop based on task feedback. Second, an evaluation protocol measures retention under interference, adaptation to change, and task transfer to isolate genuine behavioral gains from simple context expansion. Third, a memory scaling framework models how agent performance scales with experience, storage capacity, and compute budgets across non-parametric and parametric memory. Together, these components reframe agent memory from passive lookup to continuous learning, offering a principled route toward self-improving autonomous agents.

Right On Q: Recovering Reasoning under Quantization via Self-Generated Trajectories

Boyuan Zheng, Jing Xiong, Chenwei Wu, Hui Shen, Zesen Zhao, Hei Ting Una Chan, Xueshen Liu, Zhen Zhang, Liyue Shen, Zechun Liu, Z. Morley Mao

cs.CL, cs.LG

Quantization-aware training (QAT) aims to recover reasoning capabilities in low-bit large language models (LLMs). Existing recipes typically supervise the quantized student with the original full-precision model on fixed response sequences. However, the resulting low-bit quantized models still struggle on long-horizon reasoning tasks and often fail to terminate within a token budget that suffices for the full-precision model. We identify two challenges in closing this gap. First, fixed-response supervision leaves a mismatch between training prefixes and those encountered during student generation, and this trajectory divergence grows as responses lengthen. Second, on its own prefixes, the student underpredicts some next-token candidates and overpredicts others, and this distributional misalignment becomes more frequent at high-entropy prefixes. We present OnQ, a low-bit recovery pipeline that combines on-policy distillation and candidate-wise Dynamic KL. Starting from block-wise calibration, the quantized student generates responses and receives full-precision teacher supervision on its own prefixes. Dynamic KL weights forward and reverse KL by each candidate's teacher-student probability ratio to balance correction of under- and overprediction. Across Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B at 2- and 3-bit precision, OnQ achieves the highest nine-benchmark average, with the largest gains on long-horizon reasoning benchmarks. At 2 bits, it outperforms every baseline on every benchmark at every model size, improving the Qwen3-8B average by 10.26 points over the strongest baseline. It also produces shorter responses with fewer truncations and repetitive errors.

ECHO: Emergent Common-Mode Hidden Offsets Drive Vocabulary Collapse in Preference Optimization

Yun Li, Ehsan Javanmardi, Simon Thompson, Jiachen Gong, Qunli Zhang, Zifan Zeng, Shiming Liu, Peng Wang, Zixuan Guo, Manabu Tsukada

cs.CL

Preference optimization can improve every ranking metric while building a hidden, shared representation shift that the relative loss barely sees, one that can eventually destroy generation. We trace this failure to the common-mode offset, a shared directional shift in the model's hidden states that appears across token positions, prompts, and both chosen and rejected responses. Because it moves chosen and rejected representations together, the relative ranking loss barely reacts to its accumulation. As this offset grows, it projects onto the output layer to form a vocabulary well, elevating a single token's probability until it dominates decoding and causes generation collapse. The isolated offset alone reconstructs the vocabulary well, injecting the learned offset into an untreated base model recreates the collapse where norm-matched random directions do not, and masking this common component from the loss prevents the well in our flagship setting. Under the per-token ranking loss we study, the same collapse appears in 14 of 17 base-model lineages and under full-parameter fine-tuning. Moreover, the offset is not merely an artifact of collapse, since it is already present in textbook DPO and SimPO runs that form no well. Textbook DPO keeps it weakly aligned with any single vocabulary direction even with three times our base budget. Trained from base models for that budget, however, full-parameter SimPO drives it into collapse on both Llama-2 and Llama-3.1, and the same kind of offset grows, without a well, from a public SFT checkpoint under the official SimPO recipe. Across 17 UltraFeedback runs, the better a run ranks held-out responses, the less often two public reward models prefer its samples to the base model's. These findings expose a blind spot in standard ranking metrics and introduce a representation-level signal that can warn of generation collapse before it occurs.

Beyond Small Talk: Modeling Disclosure Trajectories in Human-AI Conversation

Keith Kwok, Wanting Ning, Tej Deep Pala, Desmond C. Ong, S. Supraja, Renwen Zhang, Soujanya Poria

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.HC, https://github.com/kwokkeith/Beyond_Small_Talk

Recently, we see a rise in conversational agents as a form of social and emotional support, where disclosure will unfold over time. Yet, current evaluations of these agents typically examine isolated conversational states. In analyses of naturally occurring conversations, disclosure history and current disclosure are confounded which tends to make their effects difficult to isolate. We thus introduce Trajectory-based Disclosure Evaluation (TraDE), an evaluation framework grounded in Social Penetration Theory that utilizes predefined disclosure trajectories and evaluates how preceding disclosure history shapes model behavior under matched current disclosure. Through the use of TraDE, we demonstrate across multiple models and relational behavior benchmarks that responses to disclosures matched on depth can differ depending on the preceding trajectory, particularly in whether models accept or challenge the user's framing. We also observed that these differences track accumulated exposure to deeper disclosure rather than any single moment of vulnerability or the order of disclosures. It also tends to persists for several turns after disclosure returns to shallow levels. For several behaviors, we also demonstrate that models diverge most consistently from human supporters in the Emotional Support Conversation corpus (ESConv) not when users open up but rather when they step back in their disclosure. TraDE thus provides a systematic way to expose history-dependent relational behavior that evaluations of isolated conversational states would otherwise miss.

A KAN-based Non-linear Factorization for Embedding Layer Compression

Oliver Giudice, Anna Russo Russo

cs.CL, (JADT, 2026)

Among existing embedding compression approaches, low-rank factorization provides a simple and mathematically principled solution by approximating the embedding matrix with a reduced-rank decomposition. However, its practical effectiveness is often limited, as the resulting linear bottleneck constrains representational capacity. To overcome these limitations, we propose an enhanced factorization scheme that increases expressive power by introducing learnable non-linear projections within the compressed embedding layer. Our method is inspired by the Kolmogorov Arnold Networks (KAN) paradigm, which replaces fixed linear transformations with learnable univariate functions. Despite promising results in other domains, applications of KANs in NLP remain scarce and have so far been largely limited to final or task specific layers, rather than core representational components such as embedding layers. We evaluate the proposed approach by integrating the compressed embedding layer into the Skip-gram framework and comparing it against a standard low-rank baseline under matched parameter budgets on the Word Analogy and Semantic Similarity benchmarks. We further introduce an effective initialization strategy from a pre-trained, uncompressed embedding layer. The behavior of the model is analyzed both immediately after initialization and after a brief post-initialization refinement phase, considering training with the standard Skip-gram objective as well as variants augmented with distillation from the original embedding layer. Overall, our results show that augmenting low-rank factorization with learnable non-linear projections effectively mitigates the limitations of linear bottlenecks, yielding more faithful compressed embeddings under fixed dimensionality and suggesting a promising direction for embedding layer compression beyond purely linear methods.

DynaWeave: Learning to Adapt through Dynamic Scenario Synthesis

Xuan Yang, Chunxiao Liu, Hongsheng Xin, Hao Xu, Ning Miao

cs.CL

Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use tools to complete tasks in stateful environments. However, many training environments remain largely static apart from changes caused by the agent's own actions, whereas external events introduced by other users, services, or sudden contingencies in real environments can invalidate ongoing plans. To address this gap, we introduce DynaWeave, an automated synthesis framework that transforms existing static tasks into verifiable dynamic scenarios. The framework generates exogenous events relevant to each task, covering changes in task requirements, environment state, and external information. It uses directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) to represent dependencies between events and an execution engine to activate them during agent interaction. A dynamic verifier revises only the evaluation checks affected by events that actually occur and retains all other checks. Using DynaWeave, we collect 2,095 verified interaction trajectories across 36 environments and use them for supervised fine-tuning of Qwen3.5 models. Experiments on Gaia2, CostBench, and ToolMaze show that DynaWeave achieves consistent improvements in overall performance across all three evaluated model sizes, increasing the task completion rate on CostBench by 67.34 percentage points and even exceeding the performance of Gemini-3.1-Pro-Preview on ToolMaze. These results support dynamic scenario synthesis as a practical approach to training agents to adapt to changing environments. Our code is available at https://github.com/Miaow-Lab/DynaWeave.

Omnilingual-MsASR: Benchmarking Multimodal, Multilingual, Long-Form Multi-Speaker ASR of Omni Large Language Models

Yuan Ge, Pei Zhang, Xize Cheng, Lingzhou Mu, Mingyang Wang, Jin Xu, Baosong Yang, Shengxiang Gao, Jingbo Zhu, Zhengtao Yu, Tong Xiao

cs.CL

Omni-modal large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in audio and video understanding. However, existing models struggle with long-form, multi-speaker videos, especially in understanding the entire video and recognizing who says what and when. To characterize the capability limit of current omni LLMs and guide future model development, we introduce Omnilingual-MsASR, a multilingual, multimodal benchmark for long-form Multi-speaker Automatic Speech Recognition (MsASR) covering six languages, recordings up to 30 minutes, and scenarios with up to 20 speakers. Omnilingual-MsASR provides parallel audio and video inputs to evaluate models' cross-modal consistency and their ability to exploit complementary cues across modalities. Extensive evaluations of leading omni-modal LLMs reveal substantial performance bottlenecks, while their tcpWERs frequently exceed 100%. These findings highlight fine-grained temporal localization, speaker disambiguation, and cross-modal synergy as key challenges for future omni-modal foundation models. Our work is available at https://github.com/QwenLM/Omnilingua-Bench/tree/main/Omnilingua-MSpeaker.

OmniSimulator: Aligning Small Language Models for Authentic Heterogeneous Behavior Modeling

Jiawei Chen, Ruoxi Xu, Boxi Cao, Zhuoqun Li, Ruotong Pan, Yunfei Zhang, Zhirui Yang, Jingyan Chen, Tingting Gao, Han Li, Yaojie Lu, Xianpei Han, Le Sun, Xiangyu Wu, Hongyu Lin

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG

Large language models (LLMs) have shown great potential as general-purpose user simulators for interactive systems. Prior studies have demonstrated that training on high-quality data can substantially improve model reasoning and downstream task performance. However, the upper bound of user simulation is still constrained by the model's reasoning paradigm: native chain-of-thought often degenerates into shallow pattern matching and fails to adequately model the behavioral factors underlying real user decisions. In this paper, we propose OmniSimulator, a novel training framework designed to improve the predictive ability of small LLMs for realistic user simulation on short-video platform environments. Specifically, OmniSimulator organizes behavior-oriented reasoning through a structured schema with five dimensions, carefully selects data from real user-behavior scenarios, and introduces a teacher model to generate high-quality reasoning traces under the proposed schema, helping small models learn behavior-relevant reasoning patterns rather than relying on superficial behavioral correlations. Based on this process, we construct 9,881 high-quality training instances from three months of historical interaction trajectories of 200 real users, averaging about 50 distilled examples per user. Experimental results show that OmniSimulator improves the average score of Qwen3-8B by 34.33%, achieves performance competitive with Claude-Sonnet-4.5, and supports smaller-scale, self-hosted deployment. These results suggest that learning structured behavior-oriented reasoning patterns is a promising direction towards scalable and high-fidelity LLM-based user simulation.

TenetBench: A Clause-Anchored Russian Corpus for Evaluating Language Models against Professional Codes of Ethics

Yegor Anichkov, Valery Vinogradov, Andrey Furov, Marina Polukoshko, Sergey Bolovtsov

cs.CL, 1, appended, (9, pages)., Dataset, and, code:, https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22678484

Codes of professional ethics guide and restrict specialists, who increasingly work with assistants built on large language models. Such an assistant can breach the user's code, or help the user breach it, without the user noticing. Evaluations reporting broad categories of harm cannot show how often this happens or which provision is breached. TenetBench pairs 3,433 requests conflicting with clauses of fifteen Russian-language professional codes with benign twins that differ only in the legitimacy of what is asked. An automated pipeline generates the tasks from the text of a code, so the corpus grows with every code added and a provision yields new tasks on every run. It also produced tasks and human-validated verdicts from two English codes of other jurisdictions. In human validation of the frozen pipeline, specialists rated 0.995 of the conflicting tasks plausible, and the model judge agreed with the adjudicated label at Gwet's AC1 of at least 0.894 on 1,860 elements. We evaluated fourteen model and reasoning-mode combinations on whether they produce the conflicting text, decline or warn. Eleven of the fourteen produce conflicting content on most such requests, mostly without a warning, and nearly three warnings in four accompany conflicting content. The models that conform more do so by declining while still serving the legitimate twin, and enabling reasoning halved the refusals of two models. We hope the dataset and pipeline will help evaluate how language models observe professional norms and let diagnostic datasets be built for particular professions and organisations.

A Strong MoE Is All You Need for Process Supervision

Guanzhi Deng, Zengyan Liu, Haibo Wang, Kuan Wu, Shing Yin Wong, Sichun Luo, Zhuoran Wang, Linqi Song

cs.CL, cs.AI

Process reward models (PRMs) provide fine-grained supervision for multi-step reasoning, yet obtaining reliable process labels remains a central bottleneck. Existing approaches typically rely on human annotation, rollout-based estimation, or auxiliary verification. In this work, we explore a different source of process supervision natively exposed by Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models: expert routing. We show that simple routing statistics provide an intrinsic process-confidence signal that is competitive with conventional output-logit confidence. Building on this observation, we construct routing confidence by combining overall routing mass, head routing mass, and within-step routing variation, and introduce RoCo-Rethink, which identifies the least confident reasoning step and selectively regenerates the subsequent trajectory. We further introduce RoCo-PRM, which uses a strong MoE to automatically annotate reasoning trajectories with routing-derived step scores and distills them into a standalone PRM. Across multiple MoE architectures and diverse reasoning tasks, both RoCo-Rethink and RoCo-PRM consistently outperform strong baselines, while RoCo-PRM transfers effectively across both model architectures and tasks. Our findings suggest that expert routing is more than a computational dispatch mechanism: it provides an architecture-native source of process supervision for reasoning.

Keeping Time vs. Understanding Time: Neither Alone Explains Temporal Reasoning in LLMs

Auss Abbood, Fernando Mart\'inez-Plumed, Jos\'e Hern\'andez-Orallo, Nigel Collier

cs.CL, cs.AI

Temporal reasoning is widely assumed to decompose into two modes: temporal arithmetic (date and time calculations), and temporal semantics (understanding temporal and causal relations). This dichotomy shapes how benchmarks are designed, how models are trained, and how progress is measured, yet its empirical validity has never been directly tested. We provide two lines of evidence that challenge it. First, we test whether tasks labelled as arithmetic or semantic by the benchmarks support transferable gains within their purported mode. Finetuning on these subsets yields predominantly dataset-specific gains ($+6.8$ to $+23.1$ percentage points), while transfer to a different dataset in the same mode ranges from $-3.2$ to $+1.1$ percentage points. Across the two benchmark families used in the transfer analysis, this pattern provides little evidence of coherent, transferable temporal abilities. Second, using cognitive-demand-based model evaluation, we examine which capabilities actually drive performance on these benchmarks. We find that quantitative and logical reasoning, the assumed proxies for temporal arithmetic and temporal semantics, are not the primary drivers; knowledge is. Together, these findings show that current temporal reasoning benchmarks do not cleanly realise the arithmetic-semantics distinction and instead largely reward models for remembering how time typically works.

Mitigating Query Shortcuts in Chain-of-Thought with Context-Aware Decoding

Shin'ya Yamaguchi

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG

While Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting improves the reasoning performance of large language models (LLMs), recent studies have shown that final answers do not always depend on the generated rationales. In particular, LLMs may rely on query-specific shortcuts rather than the information provided by their reasoning steps. In this paper, we investigate whether context-aware decoding (CAD), an existing contrastive decoding method, can mitigate such shortcut behavior when applied to CoT reasoning. We study this application as Rationale Contrastive Decoding (RCD), which contrasts the next-token distributions conditioned on the query with and without its rationale, amplifying tokens whose likelihood increases due to the rationale. We provide a theoretical interpretation of this decoding rule via a KL-regularized reward-maximization objective. Experiments across five reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that RCD improves answer accuracy over standard CoT. Furthermore, intervention analyses using ground-truth and random rationales reveal that RCD increases the behavioral dependence of final answers on the rationale content. Our findings suggest that contrastive decoding offers a simple, training-free approach to mitigating query shortcuts.

RCSD: Reinforcement and Continual Learning for Domain-Specific Speculative Decoding

Mingkang Liu, Weiming Meng, Peipei Jiao, Jiawei Zhang, Tianyuan Liu, Junyan Li, Lichen Pang

cs.CL

Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference by employing a compact draft model to propose candidate tokens that are verified in parallel by a target model. Conventional training relies on soft-label distillation, which forces the draft model to mimic the target's full output distribution, including high-entropy regions that degrade acceptance rates and increase early rejection risk. We propose RCSD (Reinforcement and Continual Learning for Speculative Decoding), a training framework that aligns draft model optimization with the system-level objective of maximizing acceptance length. RCSD leverages naturally occurring accepted and rejected sequences during speculative inference to construct implicit preference pairs, with masked loss to avoid penalizing target-aligned tokens. Evaluated across multiple benchmarks, RCSD improves average acceptance length by 35% over HASS and achieves 1.35x faster end-to-end inference than HASS on practical workloads, without compromising generation quality.

PACE: Periodic and Channel-Aligned Encoding of Four-Bit Recurrent States

Patrick Haller, Filipe Laitenberger, Alan Akbik

cs.CL

Linear-attention language models keep their context in a recurrent state of fixed size, where an inference system stores one copy of this state for every sequence it decodes at the same time. Storing the state at lower precision therefore lets more sequences share an accelerator. Existing state quantizers round the state after every generated token and report that four bits lose accuracy unless the bit width is raised or the model is fine-tuned.\par We introduce PACE, a post-hoc four-bit state quantizer. PACE rounds the state only once every few generated tokens, as chunked kernels already do once per chunk during prefill, and keeps the tokens generated in between in a small buffer. Rounding less often reduces the error that accumulates in the state. Additionally, we find that the large entries of the gated-delta state gather in columns, because some channels of the update written at each token remain large across many tokens. PACE therefore shares each quantization scale within one column. It also keeps the heads that retain rounding error longest at eight bits. On four hybrid models from 4B to 35B parameters, PACE with mixed four- and eight-bit heads is competitive with eight-bit DAMP on the three larger models at about two thirds of its memory, PACE with four-bit heads throughout scores higher than Q-Mamba at equal memory, and rounding every few tokens preserves the decoding speedup of the buffer.

A Diagnostic Study of Faithfulness in Diffusion Language Models for Table-to-Text Generation

Zaynab Merimi, Noussayba El Marrakchi, Arthur Satouf

cs.CL, cs.LG, source, faithfulness, in, table-to-text, generation

Diffusion language models generate by iteratively denoising a masked sequence under bidirectional attention, without the strict left-to-right commitment of autoregressive decoding. Whether this difference affects how reliably they remain grounded in structured inputs is largely unexplored. We study this question in table-to-text generation using ToTTo, where highlighted source cells make factual grounding directly testable. We compare the masked diffusion models MDLM and LLaDA with GPT-2 and GPT-Neo under an identical retrieval-based few-shot protocol, together with a QLoRA-adapted LLaDA, and we score reference similarity and source grounding as separate axes. The two pull apart. Within LLaDA, additional demonstrations raise BLEU from 11.3 to 22.0 while NLI entailment against the source table falls from 0.78 to 0.52; QLoRA reaches the best BLEU of the study, 24.15, and grounds no better for it. Manual annotation shows the failure modes differ in kind: the autoregressive baselines tend to wander off the table, while LLaDA-based models more often stay on topic and swap out individual entities. Reference-based metrics alone are insufficient to assess faithfulness in table-to-text generation.

Overview of CHIP 2026 Shared Task 1: Patient-Specific Phenotype Concept Recognition in Full-Text Biomedical Literature

Xuanlyu Zhu, Feng Tian, Yang Zhong, Pengqi Zheng, Xiwen Bi, Ling Luo, Yuanyuan Sun, Jian Wang, Lingfei Lin

cs.CL, cs.AI

Automatic phenotype information extraction plays an important role in rare disease diagnosis, genotype-phenotype analysis, and precision medicine. However, accurately identifying phenotype concepts and determining their associations with specific patients remain challenging, particularly in full-text biomedical articles involving multiple patients and complex clinical descriptions. This paper presents an overview of CHIP 2026 Shared Task 1, which aims to promote the development of methods for patient-specific phenotype concept recognition from full-text biomedical literature. The task consists of two subtasks: full-text phenotype concept recognition and patient-specific phenotype association. For this evaluation, we used 200 annotated full-text biomedical articles from the PatientPheX dataset, containing 506 patients, 15,193 phenotype mentions, and 5,656 patient-phenotype associations. A total of 455 teams registered for the competition, with 189 and 103 valid submissions received on the Phase A and Phase B leaderboards, respectively. The best-performing system achieved an overall score of 0.7389 on the final test set, outperforming the baseline by 8.37 percentage points. The top-performing teams explored diverse approaches combining biomedical language models, large language models (LLMs), and ensemble strategies. The evaluation results highlight both the potential of these approaches and the remaining challenges in patient attribution and phenotype normalization from full-text biomedical literature. The post-evaluation phase remains open at https://tianchi.aliyun.com/competition/entrance/532512.

LLMs as survey respondents: Comparison to simple semantic baselines

Esko Lehtonen, Tommi Buder-Gr\"ondahl

cs.CL

Large language models (LLMs) have been explored as surrogates for human survey respondents. A more promising application is data augmentation, where LLMs predict new responses from partial human data. However, the semantic similarity between questionnaire items is a known source of correlation between answers. It is thus unclear whether LLMs actually infer meaningful relationships between question-response pairs, or use surface-level comparison between questions as a heuristic. To assess this, we evaluated how three LLMs can predict responses to held-out items in two surveys measuring attitudes toward automated or light-electric vehicles, based on varying proportions of known responses. Performance was compared to two baselines, where new responses were derived by maximizing embedding-similarity to a prior response. While LLMs mostly predicted human responses better than baselines, their performance consistently declined with a lower share of known responses, even aligning with the baseline in one survey. Our results show that LLMs' capacities for augmenting replies varied across surveys, and can revert to the same levels as simpler embedding similarity baselines when less data is available.

Effects of Model Scale and Quantization on Social Bias and Abstention in Question Answering

Kuniko Paxton, Ifeoluwa Wuraola, Dhavalkumar Thakker

cs.CL

The deployment of small and quantized language models in resource-constrained settings raises an important fairness question: when a compressed model produces fewer biased responses, does this reflect fairer behavior or greater abstention? We study this problem in Question Answering (QA) using the BBQ benchmark across multiple model sizes and quantization settings from Qwen3, Gemma-3, and SmolLM2. We also examine whether the observed trends remain stable across alternative prompt formulations and when the ``Unknown'' option is removed in a forced-choice setting. Our results show that larger models achieved higher QA accuracy, but bias showed no simple scaling trend and quantization effects varied across configurations. Notably, 20 of the 26 configurations with fewer biased responses also show increased over-abstention, indicating that lower observed bias can partly reflect greater abstention. These findings show that lower observed bias can partly reflect greater abstention, and that fairness evaluation should consider biased responses, abstention, prompt formulation, and answer-option design jointly.

Uni-Mem: Learning a Long-Horizon Agentic Memory Manager with Unified Online Policy Optimization

Zhiqian Xia, Jielong Tang, Ling-An Zeng, Shixiong Kai, Rong Yao, Hao Kong, Xiao Dong, Wei Wang, Mingxuan Yuan, Siyu Xia, Haifeng Xia

cs.CL

Long-term agent memory is shifting from predefined workflows toward learnable policies for memory construction and retrieval. Existing learning-based methods either optimize construction alone or combine retrieval-side online reinforcement learning (RL) with offline construction supervision, limiting co-adaptation between the two stages. In this paper, we explore end-to-end online RL for a unified memory manager (Uni-Mem) that learns both capabilities under a shared policy. However, joint optimization with existing RL algorithms such as GRPO faces confounded group comparisons and sparse feedback over long memory-management trajectories. To this end, we propose Stage-Decoupled GRPO, which alternates construction and retrieval optimization while controlling variation from the other stage within each rollout group, yielding stage-specific learning signals. We further introduce Event-Wise Credit Assignment (ECA), which localizes feedback to responsible events and propagates event-level advantages to their constituent operations. Experiments on LoCoMo and LongMemEval show that Uni-Mem outperforms the strongest RL-based memory, demonstrating the effectiveness of jointly learning memory construction and retrieval via Stage-Decoupled GRPO and ECA.

Scaling Multilingual Pretraining of Pixel Language Models

Ilker Kesen, Jenny Kunz, Kasra Malihi, Desmond Elliott

cs.CL, cs.AI

Pixel language models process text as rendered images instead of a finite vocabulary, which gives them strong cross-lingual transfer abilities. However, not much is known about how these abilities are influenced by larger-scale pretraining. In this paper, we train a family of MPIXEL models along three axes of variation: more languages, more scripts, and larger model capacity under fixed per-language compute. In evaluations on five tasks covering 83 languages, we find that scaling acts locally: each axis improves the added languages and scripts, while increased capacity mitigates the curse of multilinguality from pretraining on more languages. Transfer to unseen scripts improves with multilingual pretraining, but does not further improve with scaling. On typologically distant low-resource languages, our models outperform subword and character-level baselines across many writing systems. We also show that larger pixel models reserve more layers for visual processing, and that cross-lingual alignment weakens as more languages are added. Overall, our findings characterize how multilingual scaling improves pixel language models.

Retrieval Augmented Generation Compositionality

Ido Levy, Segev Shlomov, Leshem Choshen

cs.CL, cs.AI, https://dolev31.github.io/lasagna/, Code:, https://github.com/dolev31/lasagna

Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds large language models in external knowledge. Modern pipelines combine embedding, chunking, retrieval, reranking, query augmentation, context compression, and agentic verification, yet these techniques are typically evaluated one at a time. It remains unclear whether their isolated gains survive composition, whether retrieval quality and answer faithfulness favor the same design choices, and whether conclusions from small corpora hold at large scale. We pre-declare three hypotheses and introduce LASAGNA, an open-source framework for controlled RAG evaluation. We test 450 configurations across eight benchmarks and corpora ranging from 5K to 200K documents. Three findings emerge. First, individually strong techniques from different phases of a RAG pipeline do not necessarily compose: three retrieval techniques predict a +47% combined improvement but deliver only +17% when used together. Second, component gains stack differently for retrieval and faithfulness, and optimizing one can hurt the other. Third, the best composition changes as the corpus grows: the combination that wins on a small corpus may not remain best at large scale. These findings show that gains measured in isolation are not reliable predictors of realistic performance, and that new RAG techniques should be tested with the full surrounding stack. We call on researchers to do so and supply LASAGNA to simplify these experiments.

Accept or Repair: Preserving Passage-Supported Answers in Contrastive Decoding

Jungseob Lee, Seungyoon Lee, Hyeonseok Moon, Seongtae Hong, Suhyune Son, Sugyeong Eo, Minhyuk Kim, Chanjun Park, Jaehyung Seo

cs.CL, https://github.com/js-lee-AI/AoR

Large language models often answer from knowledge learned during training, even when a provided passage contradicts that knowledge. To address this problem, contrastive decoding contrasts the predictions under the full and a modified prompt at every token, but it also alters correct answers that the passage supports. We show that checking answers against the passage removes this trade-off. Based on this observation, we propose Accept-or-Repair (AoR), a training-free procedure. AoR keeps a sampled answer if the passage supports it. Otherwise, it tries up to two replacements with contrastive decoding, returning one as soon as it passes this check. It rejects replacements that repeat the answer generated without the passage when the passage favors another answer. Our experiments show that AoR raises the score over sampling by 12.5 points on counterfactual passages and 2.3 on ordinary ones, surpassing eight contrastive and prompting baselines on both, including when each receives the same rejection rule. Replacing only initial answers that the passage does not support improves the average score of every decoder. These results suggest that models can follow passages that contradict their knowledge while preserving accuracy on passages that agree with it. Code will be released at https://github.com/js-lee-AI/AoR.

Bridging Foundational and Agentic Capabilities of LLMs

Wei Guo, Yuanjian Xu, Jianing Hao, Yongyao Duan, Ran Chen, Zhong Li, Guang Zhang

cs.CL, cs.SE

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as agents, yet it remains unclear how their foundational capabilities translate into agentic capabilities. End-to-end scores leave unresolved whether low benchmark capability or weak deployment drives a failure, limiting actionable diagnosis. We bridge foundational and agentic capabilities through an operation-level measurement framework. We represent each model with a foundational profile derived from capability benchmarks and an operation profile derived from interaction trajectories, and define capability realization gaps as deviations of observed operation performance from predictions based on foundational capabilities. Experiments on fifteen model services in $\tau^2$-Bench show that foundational profiles only partially predict agentic operations. Models with comparable profiles can differ substantially in execution, and these gaps remain consistent across task subsets. Operation profiles add information and substantially improve task-score prediction. The framework locates execution departures at the operation level to inform model development and agent evaluation. We will release our code and analysis artifacts.

ALIAS: Evaluating Performance and Memorization in LLM-Written TLA+ Specifications

Arslan Bisharat, Mujtaba Nazari, Khushboo Bhadauria, Brian Ortiz, Eric Spencer, Beatriz Santos, TaiNing Wang, George K. Thiruvathukal, Konstantin Laufer, Mohammed Abuhamad

cs.CL, cs.SE, https://github.com/LUC-AI4FM/ALIAS

Memorization inflates the benchmark scores of large language models (LLMs), since a model can reproduce answers it saw in training. Researchers test for it with consistent identifier renaming (CIR), which gives every identifier in a task a new name, and treat a lower score on the renamed task as evidence of recall. We test this method on the Temporal Logic of Actions Plus (TLA+) language, whose model checker acts as an oracle by confirming whether a renamed task preserves the exact behaviors of the original. Across 111 TLA+-Bench tasks, renaming lowers the correctness of Claude Opus 4.8 from 28% to 18% and of GPT-5 from 9% to 7%. Most of this decrease comes from how the test grades, which requires exact names that most descriptions never state. Grading by behavior shrinks the decrease to about 3 percentage points for Opus 4.8 and to 0 for GPT-5. In two model families fine-tuned on disjoint halves of the tasks, renaming removes about half of the training gain in likelihood and costs 4.9 percentage points (Qwen) and 1.3 percentage points (Gemma) on unseen tasks. A lower score after renaming therefore mixes memorization, name-based grading, and the cost of new names.

Evolving with Open Worlds: Towards Recursive Self-Improvement for Future Forecasting
