Key Findings for Small UAS for Post-Disaster Damage Assessment from the CURSE'26 Exercise

Thomas Manzini, Stephen Johnson, Priyankari Perali, David Merrick, Robin Murphy

cs.CV

The CLARKE building and road damage assessment AI classifier was incorporated into the Catastrophic Unmanned Remote Sensing Exercise (CURSE) 2026, which trained 23 small uncrewed aerial systems (sUAS) teams from state and local agencies and non-governmental organizations in Florida and Missouri on how to collect imagery for an incident command Remote Sensing Cell. Although CLARKE has been operationally deployed to two hurricanes (Debby and Helene), CURSE'26 presented an opportunity to evaluate the machine learning model with a larger set of never-before-seen orthomosaics. The results show that building and road damage assessment together ran in 3.5 minutes on average, and that building damage assessment achieved a Micro F1 of 0.834 with no manual adjustments to correct spatial misalignment and delivered that performance in both Florida and Missouri, demonstrating good geographic transfer. It also showed that road damage assessment performed poorly due to sensitivity to misalignment between road maps and the images, and to lighting and environmental conditions in an urban setting. The analysis of CURSE'26 presents five other findings of value to the sUAS community: that the use of sUAS and machine learning is now operationally normalized and accepted, sUAS teams in the field can improve classification accuracy by timing their flights, exercises are good operational tests but their value for evaluating learning models is less clear, edge computing is needed to overcome the wireless upload barrier, and the application of sUAS and AI to disaster response has ethical and legal ramifications for pilots and data managers.

JointLight: Human-Centric Video Relighting with Asynchronous RGB-Irradiance Joint Modeling

Henglei Lv, Bailin Deng, Xiaoqiang Liu, Yichen Wang, Haoxian Zhang, Pengfei Wan, Lin Gao

cs.CV

Understanding and modeling light transport in human-centric scenes is a longstanding challenge. While recent progress in diffusion models has boosted research in relighting, precise illumination control still lags, primarily due to the scarcity of diverse, high-quality, precisely labeled video relighting datasets. Existing end-to-end relighting approaches model light transport implicitly through neural networks, which substantially complicates controllability. In contrast, some works resort to path-traced irradiance cues as an explicit blueprint for light transport. However, such constructed irradiance cues are prone to artifacts and hinder generative quality when directly used as control signals. With the key observation that noise corruption removes high-frequency artifacts and preserves low-frequency light transport, we introduce an asynchronous joint-modeling paradigm that facilitates fine-grained illumination control. During two-stage training, we force the model to jointly denoise RGB and ground-truth irradiance frames with separate timesteps scheduling. A domain matching strategy is applied to the second stage to bridge the domain gap between irradiance cues and ground-truth irradiance maps. At inference time, we introduce an asynchronous hijacked generation pipeline that facilitates flexible illumination control. For training data, we develop a multi-illumination high-quality synthetic video dataset with paired irradiance maps using a powerful game engine. Trained exclusively on this dataset, our model exhibits robust generalization to real- world scenarios and outperforms prior approaches in both lighting fidelity and generative quality. The code and dataset will be publicly released.

Open-Vocabulary Models and Label Propagation for Uncertainty-aware Pseudo-Labeling of Object Detection Datasets

Jonathan Klees, Timo Heller, Matthias Rottmann

cs.CV

Object detection models rely on large-scale annotated datasets that require substantial manual annotation effort. The labeling costs further increase when annotation uncertainty is captured through soft labels, as multiple annotations must be collected for each object. We address this challenge by introducing a semi-supervised framework for the uncertainty-aware annotation of object detection datasets. Our approach leverages open-vocabulary object detectors to generate region proposals and semantically meaningful object representations. Based on these representations, we construct an object-level similarity graph and propagate probabilistic label information. Our framework assigns soft labels to previously unlabeled objects, quantifying both aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty. We evaluate our method on four object detection benchmarks with soft-label annotations and demonstrate that high-quality annotations can be obtained with only a small fraction of manually labeled objects. Furthermore, we show that detectors trained on semi-automatically annotated data achieve performance comparable to models trained on fully manually annotated data. We achieve state-of-the-art downstream detection performance for the task of semi-supervised object detection on the COCO dataset, with 40.5% AP virtually matching the performance of the model trained on fully manually constructed data (40.9%) at only 1% of labeling costs. These results highlight the potential of open-vocabulary models and graph-based label propagation for efficient, accurate and uncertainty-aware pseudo-labeling.

Compositionality with Primitives: A Unified View from Euclidean to Hyperbolic Vision-Language Models

Puru Vaish, Meelad Dashti, Christoph Brune, Pascal Mettes, Tatiana Tommasi, Nicola Strisciuglio

cs.CV

Compositional zero-shot learning (CZSL) aims to recognize novel combinations of previously observed objects and attributes. Prior work showed that large-scale vision-language models encode decomposable semantic structures that can be recomposed to enable generalization. The Geodesically Decomposable Embeddings (GDE) framework formalizes this by identifying directions in the tangent space at the Frechet mean of the embedding distribution, which encode primitive visual concepts. Unseen attribute-object compositions can be synthesized through vector addition of these primitives. We revisit the assumption that compositional generalization requires explicit attribute-object pair re-composition. By means of new compositional scoring functions and extensive evaluations, we show that directly using the decomposed primitives suffices for recognizing unseen compositions. In that, we gain insights on how the geometry of the embedding manifold (Euclidean, spherical, or hyperbolic) and dataset structure affect compositional reasoning. Across models (CLIP, DINOv2, MERU, HyCoCLIP), we show that compositional structure consistently emerges as primitive representations, enabling inference on CZSL benchmarks (MIT-States, VAW-CZSL, UT-Zappos50K, Clothing16K) and group robustness on Waterbirds and CelebA. Code: https://github.com/nis-research/Compositionality-with-Primitives.

GuidedSAM-Plume: An Expert-Guided Segmentation Framework for Volcanic Plumes

Taddeo D'Adamo (LIRIS, imagine), Simon Thivet (UNIGE), Catherine Pothier (LIRIS, imagine), Riccardo Simionato (UNIGE), Allan Fries (UNIGE), Jonathan Lemus (UNIGE), Costanza Bonadonna (UNIGE), Laure Tougne Rodet (LIRIS, imagine), Bertrand Kerautret (LIRIS, imagine)

cs.CV, Lyon,, France., pp.364-379

Quantitative analysis of environmental phenomena increasingly relies on image-based measurements. In volcanology, the geometric characterization of volcanic plumes and clouds from RGB images remains hindered by the scarcity of dedicated segmentation solutions and partially handcrafted annotations remain widely prevalent. We present GuidedSAM-Plume, an open-source expert-guided framework tailored for the interactive segmentation of volcanic plumes and clouds. It leverages the zero-shot, spatial prompt-based capabilities of SAM2. The framework was thoroughly validated on a challenging dataset through dedicated segmentation metrics and field-relevant volcanological descriptors. From minimal user inputs, it is able to significantly increase performance compared to fully automatic dedicated solutions while preserving the reliability of derived volcanological parameters. The absence of retraining requirements lowers adoption barriers within the volcanological community, and the framework's demonstrated suitability for complex, diffuse plume-shaped phenomena extends its applicability to broader environmental science domains. The re-annotated dataset, produced under the guidance of expert volcanologists within the proposed framework, ultimately yields a more standardized release. The framework and accompanying dataset are made available at https://github.com/tad-damo/GuidedSAM-Plume.

Early versus Late RGB-Infrared Fusion for Counter-UAV Detection: A Comparative Study

Alberto De Zan, Denis Tavaris, Gian Luca Foresti, Ivan Scagnetto

cs.CV, includes, revisions, requested, by, the, reviewers, during, the, final, review, round, that, could, not, be, incorporated, into, the, published, version, Versus, Late, RGB-Infrared, Fusion, for, Counter-UAV, Detection:, A, Comparative, Study,, IEEE, Access,, vol., 14,, pp., 149702-149712,, 2026

This paper presents a novel strategy for UAV detection through visible (RGB) and infrared (IR) fusion, in order to improve accuracy and precision in harsh environments and weather conditions. In this research, two opposite approaches have been studied and implemented: the first concerns image-level (early) fusion, which merges two different spectra into a single image to which one detector is applied. On the other hand, the second approach, called decision-level (late) fusion, focuses on blending together the output bounding boxes of two different detection pipelines, aiming to create a common result that can be applied to both spectra simultaneously. Both strategies have been extensively tested in a dedicated environment, which included alternating low and high brightness scenes, different backgrounds (sky and ground), distances (5, 25 and 60 m), altitudes and zoom levels (1X, 2X and 4X). These tests allowed for the assessment of actual detection capabilities and system reliability in real-world applications. Furthermore, the two fusion strategies were evaluated and compared over a set of aligned images in order to highlight the better approach of the two. The results indicate that early fusion achieves better raw performance, since it incorporates the thermal information from the beginning of the training process. However, late fusion presents an innovative and useful detection workflow that can be applied in cases of sensor or frame misalignment. Both positive and negative aspects have also been pointed out and discussed, in order to give a more comprehensive overview. Finally, the paper outlines implementation considerations and practical recommendations based on operational priorities. Overall, the findings contribute to the research in RGB-IR fusion modalities, with the goal of enhancing UAV detection capabilities.

Revealing Hidden Failure Modes in Chest X-ray Classification via Spectral Domain Analysis

Samuel Halimi, Loic Themyr, Arnaud Abreu

cs.CV, with, Deep, Learning,, PMLR, 315:2682-2710,, 2026

Deep learning models for chest X-ray anomaly detection remain vulnerable to subtle distributional shifts (e.g., acquisition technique, patient-related factors, and preprocessing). Traditional error analysis often relies on semantic metadata or model embeddings, which can mask low-level signal variations that degrade performance. In this work, we propose a data-centric framework for automated failure mode discovery using spectral analysis. We project images into the frequency domain and extract a compact profile summarizing the distribution of signal energy across frequency bands. By performing unsupervised clustering on these spectral profiles, we demonstrate that model failures are not randomly distributed, but are strongly concentrated within specific spectral clusters. This method effectively isolates "blind spots", enabling the prediction of model reliability and the discovery of performance-degrading data slices without requiring ground-truth failure annotations.

How Do DiTs Denoise? An Aggregator-Predictor View of Attention and MLPs

Gyeonghun Kang, William Xingxu Chen, Xiang Cheng

cs.CV, stat.ML

How do diffusion transformers (DiTs) represent image content and learn rules for reusing it to generalize? We develop an aggregator--predictor view of DiTs as input-adaptive local denoisers, in which context aggregation and patch prediction provide functional roles for interpreting attention and MLPs. We first establish that, for Gaussian Markov random field (GMRF) priors, the global Bayes denoiser can be approximated using only a subset of context patches, with error decaying exponentially in context radius. We further show that patchwise linear denoisers, often invoked to explain diffusion generalization, recover the empirical local denoiser with sufficiently informative aggregation. Within this view, AdaLN-modulated attention naturally parameterizes an input- and noise-dependent aggregator, while the MLP admits a learned key--value representation for tokenwise patch prediction. Our experiments support this view. DiTs trained on real images exhibit noise-dependent denoising contexts resembling those in the analytic GMRF setting, while attention mass exhibits a qualitatively corresponding broadening pattern. In a controlled CelebA64 study, shared-memory patch predictors used in place of DiT MLPs achieve competitive denoising and generative performance. Finally, cross-dataset component transplantation reveals compatibility patterns consistent with the proposed roles of QK in spatial weighting, VO in feature transport, and MLPs in patch prediction. Together, these results provide a component-level account of DiT denoising and motivate architecture-specific fine-tuning and controlled intervention.

GeoMem-VLA: Internalizing 3D Scene Understanding into Temporal Memory for Vision-Language-Action Models

Qinfan Zhang, Yifan Luo, Haoyuan Shi, Guanlin Liu, Han Dong, Haozhe Shan, Yong Dai, Xiangcong Ren, Xiaozhu Ju

cs.CV

Geometric structure is essential for robotic manipulation and understanding the 3D world. Most VLA models rely on 2D representations of current observations, while geometry-aware approaches often require additional 3D information. However, geometry inferred independently from individual observations is often discarded rather than accumulated into a persistent task representation. Our key insight is that action generation needs a compact, task-relevant geometric abstraction that can be continuously updated, rather than a complete, renderable 3D reconstruction at every step. We introduce GeoMem-VLA, a VLA framework that internalizes explicit 3D supervision into dedicated geometric tokens and maintains them as temporal memory for action generation, without directly aligning the VLM's native visual embeddings with the teacher feature space. During training, a frozen geometry foundation model supervises learnable GeoPlan tokens through a geometric flow-matching objective over the current scene point map and future end-effector tracks. These tokens are integrated with the memory of observation representations, where historical information is retrieved, adaptively fused with current observations, and consolidated to retain event frames as the task progresses. At inference, the policy retrieves historical representations and fuses them with the current working representation to predict actions, without invoking the 3D teacher or explicitly reconstructing the scene. After benchmark-specific downstream adaptation, GeoMem-VLA achieves 98.9% average success on LIBERO and 91.10% / 91.24% on RoboTwin's clean/randomized settings. Across five real-world tasks, GeoMem-VLA achieves 78.0% average success. These results suggest that internalizing and remembering 3D scene understanding is a promising approach to robot control.

Stochastic Nonlinearities Improve Uncertainty Estimation

Mohammad Sarhangzadeh, Amirreza M. Shebly, Doruk Oner

cs.CV, pages,, including, supplementary, material

Reliable uncertainty estimation is important across visual recognition tasks, providing information about the confidence and reliability of model predictions. Many uncertainty estimation methods obtain predictive diversity through mechanisms that can suppress intermediate features, perturb the input, or require substantial additional computation. Stochastic activation functions, where network units randomly select between multiple nonlinearities during a forward pass, have previously been used to improve optimization behavior, mitigate limitations of fixed ReLU activations, and increase output diversity. In this work, we reframe stochastic activation switching as a lightweight mechanism for uncertainty estimation. The proposed method samples between nonlinear activation functions, inducing predictive diversity while keeping intermediate feature pathways active. This enables uncertainty estimation from a single trained model without the training cost of ensembles, task-specific uncertainty heads, or architectural redesign. We evaluate the proposed approach across classification, semantic segmentation, and out-of-distribution detection using convolutional and transformer-based architectures on diverse datasets. Across these settings, stochastic activation switching improves uncertainty quality while preserving competitive primary-task performance, showing that stochastic nonlinearities provide an effective and information-preserving source of predictive variation for visual recognition.

Expandable Modality Experts for Medical Vision-Language Learning

DM BAPPY, Eunsu Kim, Hanjoo Lee, Sung Hak Lee, Sangjeong Ahn

cs.CV, figures., Submitted, to, ICLR, 2027

Learning across medical imaging modalities requires making use of images without paired text and accommodating new domains while retaining existing representations. We propose a medical vision-language technique built around an expandable bank of modality experts. Its Multiway Transformer combines shared visual computation with deterministically routed feed-forward experts and a separate text pathway. A modality-aware visual tokenizer supplies targets for masked pretraining on 19.4 million unpaired image rows across seven modalities; contrastive alignment then uses 3.23 million image-text pairs. Additional fundus and optical coherence tomography experts learn from paired data while the original image and text embedding paths remain frozen. With a ViT-Large backbone, the proposed technique achieves the best or tied-best result in 24 of 25 benchmark settings across nine modalities: 10 of 11 zero-shot classification benchmarks, all 10 linear probes, and all four retrieval benchmarks. Mean balanced accuracy is 0.674 for zero-shot classification and 0.856 for linear probing, and mean bidirectional retrieval recall is 20.70%. A separate three-seed sequential experiment with matched adaptation data yields fundus and OCT retrieval gains of 14.95 and 9.31 percentage points, with no measured loss on preceding fundus or pathology tasks. The preservation argument requires unchanged existing computation paths and inference conditions, and feature-identity checks verify retention on evaluated inputs. The proposed technique provides a modular approach to learning and expanding medical vision-language representations.

ViBMR: A Large-Scale Multi-Sequence Brain MRI Dataset and Comprehensive Evaluation for Vietnamese Radiology Report Generation

Dai Lam Le, Minh Khoi Nguyen, Son Dinh Nguyen, Amir Reza Jafari, Viet Ha, Nguyen Son, Reza Farahbakhsh, Noel Crespi, Phi Le Nguyen

cs.CV, Vision, (WACV, 2027)

Automated radiology report generation for multi-sequence brain MRI holds strong promise for alleviating radiologists' workload, yet it remains a demanding problem: brain MRI studies are inherently volumetric, each pulse sequence carries complementary diagnostic information, and annotated data are critically scarce for low-resource clinical languages. Existing brain MRI-report resources are still dominated by English reports, translated clinical text, or datasets adapted from task-specific imaging benchmarks, leaving routine low-resource clinical reporting and heterogeneous multi-sequence MRI protocols insufficiently represented. To bridge this gap, we introduce ViBMR, to the best of our knowledge, the first large-scale multi-sequence brain MRI dataset paired with routine Vietnamese clinical reports, comprising 3,649 patients and 11,875 MRI series across T1, T2, FLAIR, and contrast-enhanced T1 sequences. Building on ViBMR, we establish a comprehensive evaluation setup for brain MRI report generation, covering representative medical vision-language models, multi-sequence inputs, and feature aggregation strategies. The results provide key insights into dataset characteristics and model behavior, while demonstrating the necessity of native low-resource clinical datasets for reliable Vietnamese-origin brain MRI report generation. Our dataset and baseline models will be released for non-commercial research.

Asking a Multimodal LLM Whether Someone Fell: Zero-Shot Screening of Low-Resolution Thermal Video in a Laboratory and an Occupied Home

Marcos Junca (Senecta Inc.), Mauro Junca (Senecta Inc.)

cs.CV, cs.AI, https://senecta.ai/research/thermal-fall-llm-data.zip

Thermal cameras can watch over older people living alone without capturing faces, and multimodal large language models (LLMs) can judge a short video without task-specific training. We evaluate a production multimodal LLM, Gemini 3.8 Flash at temperature 0, as the decision stage of an event-triggered fall screener, used exactly as a deployed prototype uses it: one request per short thermal clip, with its sound track when there is one, and its predecessor Gemini 3.7 Flash on the same requests. On 438 staged falls and 563 non-fall windows that we located by hand in a public 32x24 thermal dataset, the deployed request called 69% of the held-out subjects' falls (67% over all subjects; bed falls excluded) and none of 499 non-fall windows, including people lying on the floor and getting up. Over 76 h of ordinary life in an occupied room, recorded at 160x120 with sound, it called none of 395 clips a fall (95% upper bound 1.2 false alarms per day) and caught 4 of the 9 staged falls the camera could see. One cautionary sentence in the prompt ("a person who goes out of view ... is not a fall") accounted for many of the misses: removing it raised sensitivity on the held-out subjects from 69% to 83% on 3.8 Flash and from 80% to 92% on Gemini 3.7 Flash, and in the home from 4 to 9 of the 9 visible falls, with no false call in ordinary living but at the price of calling deliberate lie-downs a fall. Sensitivity collapsed at 8x6 pixels (34%); identical requests disagreed on 4% of windows; and when the model called a fall, its explanation placed it within a second of the annotated landing 99% of the time. Two conventional detectors trained on the laboratory data caught nearly every held-out laboratory fall but raised 14.2 and 23.9 false alarms per day in the home. We release the frame-level fall annotations of the public dataset and every model answer on it.

CausalAlign: Causal Alignment of Video Generation Models for Consistent Ego-Motion

Kai Zhu, Yintianrun Zhang, Shuwei Jiang, Ruolin Zuo, Yixin Chen, Duomin Wang, Miao Liu, Yin Li, Daquan Zhou, Yiwu Zhong

cs.CV

Diffusion-based video generation models (VGMs) can produce visually plausible videos, yet their camera ego-motion often drifts over long horizons. Camera ego-motion determines how the viewpoint changes between frames and is essential for maintaining consistent geometry and trajectories. To address this problem, we propose CausalAlign, a causal alignment framework with two components. First, we diagnose how precise ego motion is represented, used, and retained within VGMs using linear probes and causal interventions, revealing a rise-and-fall pattern during denoising. Second, we strengthen ego-motion state in VGM by aligning the representations at the identified layers to a 3D foundation model through delta-token space. This space encodes the changes between consecutive frames, allowing the alignment to focus on ego-motion dynamics rather than static scene content. With Wan2.2 fine-tuned on DL3DV and RealEstate10K, CausalAlign can largely improve motion accuracy, geometric consistency, and temporal smoothness, while preserving visual quality. For example, it improves geometric consistency by more than 20% relative to the base model. Moreover, with the fine-tuned VGM, CausalAlign improves the success rate of world action models (WAMs) by 10 points on mobile manipulation tasks of RoboCasa365 benchmark. Overall, CausalAlign improves ego-motion consistency in diffusion-based VGMs and yields noticeable gains in embodied models.

DewHand: Direct Metric World-Space Hand Motion Reconstruction from Egocentric Videos

Naru Suzuki, Satoshi Ikehata, Takehiko Ohkawa, Yoichi Sato, Shuhei Kurita

cs.CV

Recovering metric world-space hand motion from egocentric video supports understanding human interactions and transferring them to embodied agents. Three challenges complicate this task: multi-stage pipelines propagate errors from independently estimated hand and camera motion; monocular observations leave physical scale ambiguous; and occlusion and out-of-view hands admit multiple plausible motion trajectories. We present DewHand, a unified model for direct joint reconstruction of world-space hand and camera motion. To avoid compounding errors from multi-stage estimation, DewHand jointly predicts hand and camera motion in a shared world coordinate system within a single-stage pipeline. To resolve metric-scale ambiguity, it combines scene-level geometric features from VGGT-$\Omega$ with metric-space supervision, while hand-specific features preserve fine-grained articulation. To handle occlusion and out-of-view hands, we formulate reconstruction as conditional motion generation with flow matching. Under the same architecture, this generative formulation outperforms direct regression in both accuracy and temporal smoothness with only two sampling steps. Experiments on HOT3D and ARCTIC demonstrate improved world-space accuracy and temporal consistency over state-of-the-art methods. DewHand reduces rigid-aligned world-space error relative to StableHand from 32.9 to 28.4 mm on HOT3D and from 76.2 to 36.2 mm on ARCTIC. It also recovers camera scale within 10% on two thirds of HOT3D clips, versus about a quarter for SLAM. Moreover, in zero-shot evaluations on HoloAssist, H2O, and TACO, captured with different devices, DewHand achieves the lowest rigid-aligned world-space error (WA-MPJPE) on each dataset.

Training-free Acceleration for Blind Compressed Image Restoration via Activation-Calibrated Singular Value Decomposition

Suhang Yao, Xin Li, Xijun Wang, Yufei Wang, Yeying Jin, and Zhibo Chen

cs.CV, Selected, Topics, in, Circuits, and, Systems

Transformer-based models achieve strong performance on blind compressed image restoration (CIR) under unknown compression settings, but their densely stacked projection operators make deployment costly. We study training-free acceleration for such models through an empirical, operator-level analysis. Specifically, we profile where computation is concentrated, characterize channel-wise activation distributions under compressed inputs, and examine the singular-value decay of representative projection weights. These measurements identify the recurring projections in the attention, feed-forward, convolutional, and prompt-conditioning pathways as effective low-rank replacement targets that preserve the original inference graph. They further establish truncated SVD (TSVD) as a principled training-free baseline for blind CIR and reveal why a uniform low-rank objective is mismatched to the channel-imbalanced feature distributions of compressed images. Accordingly, we formulate Activation-Calibrated Singular Value Decomposition (ASVD) for this restoration setting, deriving low-rank factors entirely offline from pretrained weights and lightweight activation statistics gathered from only a few compressed calibration images. By weighting the decomposition with channel-wise activation magnitudes, our ASVD assigns larger penalties to errors on more active channels, requiring neither retraining nor online calibration and leaving the restoration pipeline intact. When instantiated on PromptCIR at rank 64, ASVD consistently improves PSNR-B over TSVD across four standard benchmarks under identical parameter and MAC budgets. At this operating point, the factorized model cuts parameters by 59% (34.75M to 14.34M) and computation by 16.1% (258.79 to 217.16 GMACs for a 256 x 256 input), enabling acceleration without retraining.

Looking Ahead: Modeling Future Evolution in Autoregressive Video Generation

Ruopeng Gao, Yukun Huang, Jiarui Hu, Shuai Wang, Wenbo Hu, Ying Shan, Limin Wang

cs.CV

Autoregressive video diffusion models generate videos chunk by chunk, yet producing plausible continuations does not guarantee that they capture how scenes evolve. Under teacher forcing, strong temporal redundancy may allow local visual continuity to serve as a shortcut for current-chunk denoising, reducing the pressure to model visual evolution. We introduce Future Chunk Prediction (FCP), an auxiliary objective jointly optimized with teacher-forced denoising that predicts temporally separated chunks without access to the intervening visual context. Drawing supervision directly from training videos, FCP requires no additional pretrained visual encoder and leaves autoregressive inference unchanged. Our method achieves superior physical plausibility over standard teacher-forced autoregressive training with a lower overall training compute budget, highlighting the effectiveness and efficiency of future-chunk supervision for modeling visual evolution. Intriguingly, our visualizations suggest that FCP fosters a more structured representation space, which may be valuable for building autoregressive world models.

Multiclass Boundary Refinement with Neural Cellular Automata for Remote Sensing Land Cover Segmentation

Pascoassis Souza Santos Meira, Gilson Antonio Giraldi, Rafael Duarte Coelho dos Santos

cs.CV

Semantic segmentation of remote sensing imagery often places class boundaries a few pixels away from where they truly lie, and this position error is concentrated in the thin band measured by Boundary IoU (BIoU). We treat boundary refinement as the iterated application of a small learned local rule: the Boundary Refinement Neural Cellular Automaton (BRNCA), that starts from the class probabilities of a frozen, pre-trained segmenter and corrects them cell by cell. We generalise the binary rNCA to the multiclass case by keeping the visible channels on the probability simplex through an Aitchison (log-space) update and by adding free hidden channels. The best configuration improves the mean BIoU over the base segmenter by 2.36 points (from 46.63 to 48.99). The gain is concentrated on the irregular woodland class (+8.5 points BIoU); this suggests that position error is correlated with the fractal dimension of the class boundaries.

ClunoGS: Compact Language 3D Gaussian Splatting via Unified Optimization

Jian Xu, Yi Li, Ying Huang, Siyi Wu, Bingzhe Li, Yuede Ji, Miao Yin

cs.CV

Language 3D Gaussian Splatting enables open-vocabulary scene understanding by associating Gaussian primitives with language features. These semantic attributes increase model storage, while most existing 3DGS compression methods are designed mainly to preserve RGB rendering quality. One straightforward approach is to apply sparsification and quantization sequentially. However, pruning based only on rendering quality can remove Gaussians that have small RGB contributions but are important for semantic prediction. Once removed, their semantic information cannot be recovered by later quantization. We propose ClunoGS, a compact framework for Language 3DGS that combines semantics-aware Gaussian sparsification and vector quantization in a unified constrained objective. Semantic supervision is used during sparsification, and the objective jointly considers rendering quality, semantic accuracy, and compactness. We optimize the unified objective with alternating primal, sparsification, quantization, and dual updates. We evaluate ClunoGS on open-vocabulary localization and segmentation using the LERF and ScanNet-10 benchmarks. Under aggressive compression, our unified, semantics-aware formulation substantially alleviates the degradation of semantic representations caused by sequential compression and semantics-agnostic Gaussian sparsification. Ablations further show that jointly coordinating sparsification and quantization better preserves semantic accuracy than applying them independently.

RealMe: Immersive Volumetric Video from Minimal Captures

Chenxu Zhou, Zhiyuan Yu, Yudong Jin, Yinji Shentu, Yu Zhang, Jiaming Sun, Xiaowei Zhou

cs.CV, SIGGRAPH, Asia, 2026, Conference, Papers., Project, page:, https://zju3dv.github.io/realme

This paper addresses the problem of creating immersive volumetric videos from sparse and low-resolution capture resources. Conventional dense multi-view reconstruction methods can produce high-quality volumetric videos, but their reliance on costly capture setups makes them impractical in real-world scenarios. Recent approaches use generative models to relax capture requirements, but still suffer from limited resolution and 4D inconsistency. To overcome these constraints, we propose RealMe, a unified framework built around a high-resolution video diffusion model that enhances limited captures into high-resolution (2K), 4D-consistent videos for volumetric video reconstruction. Specifically, RealMe constructs auxiliary data from capture videos and encodes them into latents along with layout-aware positional embeddings. The video diffusion model adapts factorized spatio-temporal attention to the multi-view capture layout, denoises these latents across viewpoints and time, and decodes them into enhanced videos to refine the 4D representation. This adaptation helps the model capture temporal dynamics and multi-view relationships, leading to more 4D-consistent generation. To reduce the data gap between generated videos and real captures, we apply a latent frequency loss that provides frequency-domain supervision in latent space. Extensive experiments on the public DNA-Rendering benchmark and our self-captured RealVV dataset demonstrate that RealMe is able to create immersive volumetric videos and outperforms state-of-the-art methods in visual fidelity and 4D consistency.

Plausible but Unimplantable: A Trustworthiness Audit of Shape Completion Foundation Models for Cranial Defect Reconstruction

Marek Wodzinski, Kamil Kwarciak, Daria Hemmerling, Mateusz Daniol

cs.CV

A patient who has lost part of the skull vault needs an implant that fits the defect exactly, and such implants are still drawn by hand in CAD by specialists, which makes each case slow and expensive. The task can be considered as a shape completion problem, so pretrained completion models, and now the first shape completion foundation models, look like a way to remove that manual step. We audited three publicly released pretrained shape completion models, two of them foundation models, applied zero-shot to 239 cranial defects, some carved from public skull datasets and some real post-craniectomy cases. Each output was compared with simply returning the patient's own remaining bone, and tested for whether the predicted material could be placed without intersecting the bone that is still there. On the 210 carved defects none of the three improved on doing nothing, and on the 29 real cases only one did so beyond chance. Yet not one output, from any model in any configuration, was implantable: none managed both to stay clear of the bone the patient still has and to place more material than doing nothing, where a task-specific volumetric model, trained for this task and selected on these cases, was implantable on all but one. The output that most convincingly resembles a skull is no more placeable than the rest, and at these point densities implant thickness cannot be read off the emitted points at all, which is a property of the output representation rather than of any one model. On this evidence the shape completion foundation models we could obtain cannot be trusted and, used as released, are not usable in clinical practice. Substantial research towards trustworthy foundation models for shape completion is still needed.

Attn-Forcing: Learning Full-Context Attention Proxies for Streaming Video Generation

Junyao Gao, Sibo Liu, Jiaxing Li, Yanan Sun, Hanxin Zhu, Weidong Zhang, Jun Zhang, Cairong Zhao

cs.CV

Increasing the attention context length significantly improves streaming video generation quality, while incurring prohibitive memory and computational costs as the context grows. Existing methods attempt to capture this contextual information through attention selection or compression, but remain limited by bounded representations and lack explicit attention-level supervision to retain historical contributions to current queries. To address this, we introduce Attn-Forcing, a novel framework that preserves full-context attention contributions for streaming video generation under bounded memory and computation. We achieve this by introducing attention proxies that continuously approximate the attention responses induced by the historical context. Specifically, we implement the proxies as learnable visual latents and employ a Proxy Update Module to incorporate visual context from historical latents. Moreover, we propose an Attention Matching Loss to explicitly constrain the attention behavior of the learned proxies using full-context attention supervision. Extensive qualitative and quantitative experiments demonstrate that Attn-Forcing consistently improves streaming video generation quality and achieves state-of-the-art performance over existing baseline methods.

ProactEgoMem: Benchmarking Proactive Memory in Streaming Egocentric Video

Ruotong Liao, Meikel Kokowski, Xiaoxiao Sun, Yongqin Xian, Laura Bravo S\'anchez, Shiye Su, Federico Tombari, Serena Yeung-Levy, Volker Tresp

cs.CV

Today's multimodal assistants are typically prompted to interpret the visual evidence presented to them. To offload the cognitive burden of remembering across long horizons, wearable assistants should instead proactively acquire and maintain useful memory as the user's surroundings evolve. Doing so on the user's behalf requires identifying what matters, understanding the scene in real time, and initiating memory maintenance in the background while the user stays focused on their activities. We introduce ProactEgoMem, a benchmark that evaluates proactive memory in streaming egocentric video. Three tasks ground the evaluation in ambient environmental needs: Information Capture (IC) preserves transient yet dense visual information, State Tracking (ST) follows changing object states, and Location Tracking (LT) records object movements. The benchmark defines its protocol at two levels of proactiveness: the Notification Level asks for a single timely notification that satisfies the user's intent, while the deeper Intervention Level asks the agent to maintain memory on the user's behalf through an active start-to-stop tool lifecycle. Parallel ProactEgoMem-Daily and ProactEgoMem-Sci tracks contain 300/100 clips covering daily and scientific use cases, paired with 1000/500 questions in ProactEgoMem-QA, which measures the quality of the proactively acquired memory. Across proprietary, open-source, video-language, and streaming baselines, even the strongest models fail to capture memory both timely and accurately. ProactEgoMem provides a rigorous evaluation foundation for proactive multimodal assistance and for video agents that co-exist with their users.

ALPS: Visual State Encoders for World Models

Yanqing Liu, Letian Zhang, Chengzhi Liu, Zijun Wang, Jieru Mei, Hongliang Fei, Yuyin Zhou, Cihang Xie

cs.CV, page:, https://ucsc-vlaa.github.io/ALPS/, Code:, https://github.com/UCSC-VLAA/ALPS

World models turn visual representations into predictions and actions. We argue that their visual foundation should combine physical-state information, native language alignment, and pixel reconstruction, so that a single representation can support prediction, instruction, and inspection. However, existing encoders typically specialize in only one or two of these capabilities; we therefore introduce ALPS, a pretrained visual state encoder that unifies all three in a single set of patch tokens. Using only still images and text, we pretrain a ViT-B/16 from scratch with language, reconstruction, and spatial correspondence objectives, and then refine it via cross-view self-distillation. In a unified evaluation across fifteen public encoders, ALPS achieves the lowest aggregate LIBERO state-probe error among ViT-B models (matching the 3.5x larger V-JEPA 2-L) while maintaining strong language alignment and accurate pixel reconstruction. Downstream, frozen ALPS tokens reach the highest mean policy success on LIBERO (85.5%) when paired with our native text tower, match or exceed leading encoders on world-model planning across PushT, Wall, and Granular, and allow predicted future tokens to be decoded directly into pixels for visual inspection. We hope this work helps establish visual pretraining as a shared foundation for understanding, prediction, and action in world models.

VoxProg: 3D Spatial Reasoning through Voxel Programming

Zhichao Zhai, Menglei Zhao, Guikun Chen, Zihao Yan, Kanzhi Wu, Wenguan Wang, Jun Xiao

cs.CV

Language models' abilities to interpret tasks and generate programs offer a promising basis for 3D spatial reasoning. Yet following spatial instructions in 3D scenes requires programs to construct geometric references that language leaves implicit. In this work, we introduce VoxProg, which formulates 3D object placement through voxel programming: a language model translates task interpretations into executable programs over local voxel views of scene surfaces. Because these views index surface geometry on regular grids with explicit coordinates and neighborhood relations, an evidence program can isolate task-relevant regions and boundaries directly from scene meshes, and a downstream pose program can measure the resulting geometry to compute a target $SE(3)$ pose. Visual and numerical feedback from execution lets the model inspect intermediate selections and candidate placements to iteratively revise its code. To evaluate open-ended placement across functional use, geometric fit, and contextual arrangements, we introduce the 71-task VoxProg-Bench benchmark along with an evaluation suite pairing perceptual Intent Alignment and 3D Physical Validity. Across VoxProg-Bench and external benchmarks, VoxProg consistently outperforms relation-based, keypoint-based, and mesh-based baselines.

Rethinking Video Autoencoders for Interactive World Models

Adrien Ramanana Rahary, Yutong Bai, Nicolas Dufour, Patrick P\'erez, David Picard

cs.CV

Video world models predict future frames from past frames and actions. They serve as simulators in which agents train, plan or are evaluated, and that humans can control interactively. They generate in the latent space of a video autoencoder, whose compression sets their cost per frame and how long each action waits to take effect. Yet world models often reuse the autoencoders of text-to-video generators, built for offline generation with no agent waiting and, without their training data and code, not retrainable at another compression. We therefore ask how, where and how much a world model should compress. We build CADRE, an open family of causal diffusion representation autoencoders spanning multiple spatio-temporal compressions. Its members differ only in a small bottleneck on frozen features and share a diffusion decoder self-distilled to one or a few steps. We train the same world model on each autoencoder in two domains, manipulation and navigation, with two environments each. CADRE forms most of the latency-quality frontier, especially with a moving camera. Our study also leads to four findings. At strong compression, a diffusion decoder is sharper than perceptual regression. In robot manipulation, temporal compression is more effective in the autoencoder than in the world model. Manipulation and navigation call for opposite compressions: temporal compression costs little when the camera is still, and spatial compression when it moves. Surprisingly, with a still camera, coarser spatial tokens hurt motion far more than image quality. Code, models and a live demo are at https://kyutai.org/blog/2026-10-21-cadre

To Keep or Not to Keep: Learning KV Cache Retention in Disaggregated LLM Serving Systems

Dong Liu, Yanxuan Yu, Eric Jiang, Shu Wang, Ying Nian Wu

cs.LG, cs.DC

Disaggregated \textsc{LLM} serving separates prefill and decode across node pools, placing a network fabric between \textsc{KV}-cache computation and consumption. This breaks a key assumption of classical cache policies: a miss no longer incurs only local recomputation, but also network transfer to the decode node. Likewise, admitting a block to the global cache requires an additional transfer, so poor retention decisions waste both memory and bandwidth. We present \textbf{KVLearn}, a learning-based framework for cost-aware \textsc{KV}-cache retention in disaggregated serving. KVLearn includes: (i) a lightweight Prefix Reuse Predictor (\textsc{PRP}) that estimates reuse probability from structural and temporal prefix features without modifying model weights; (ii) a Cost-Aware Retention Score (\textsc{CARS}) that converts reuse probability into keep/admit decisions using recomputation, transfer, and storage costs; and (iii) an Adaptive Threshold Controller (\textsc{ATC}) that adjusts admission thresholds online from observed hit rates and memory pressure. We integrate KVLearn into a globally disaggregated serving topology and evaluate it on text and multimodal workloads, including large image/video-derived \textsc{KV} blocks with heterogeneous reuse patterns. KVLearn reduces end-to-end time-to-first-token by up to 56% versus No-Cache, 38% versus LRU-Pool, and 33% versus Mooncake-style baselines, while cutting inter-node \textsc{KV} transfer volume by up to 53% versus LRU-Pool. On MM-Session, throughput remains within ${\sim}5%$ of oracle. Code is available at https://github.com/FastLM/KVLearn.

Interaction-Gated Time-Frequency Learning for Parameter-Efficient PPG-Based Heart Rate Estimation

Anh Khoi Pham, Phat Lam, Thien Truong Nguyen Ly, Lam Pham

cs.LG, cs.AI, eess.SP

Photoplethysmography (PPG) enables low-cost and non-invasive heart rate (HR) monitoring but remains sensitive to waveform distortion, physiological variability, and measurement interference. Temporal waveform characteristics and frequency-domain periodicity provide complementary information for HR estimation; however, effectively exploring and integrating these representations within a compact model remains challenging. This paper proposes TF-IG, a parameter-efficient interaction-gated time-frequency learning framework for PPG-based HR estimation. TF-IG employs a dual-branch architecture in which a temporal branch captures pulse morphology via dilated depthwise-separable convolutions, while a frequency branch extracts cardiac periodicity information from bounded log-spectrogram representations. To combine the two domains, we introduce an interaction-gated fusion module that explicitly models feature agreement and discrepancy, while also learning feature-wise weights to balance temporal and spectral information. A parameter-shared regression head further maps the temporal, spectral, and fused representations into the final HR prediction space. Experiments on the benchmark BIDMC PPG and Respiration Dataset using a subject-exclusive training, validation, and test protocol show that TF-IG achieves a mean absolute error (MAE) of 1.227 BPM, a root mean square error (RMSE) of 2.060 BPM, a Pearson correlation coefficient of 0.9934, and 92.28 percent of estimates within 3 BPM, while remaining parameter-efficient with only 84.6K parameters. Ablation results further demonstrate the complementary contributions of the temporal and frequency branches, interaction-gated fusion, and shared regression head. These results indicate that explicitly modeling cross-domain interactions provides an effective accuracy-complexity trade-off for lightweight PPG-based HR estimation.

Reinforcement learning for hardware acceleration and system-level optimization on FPGA and integrated-circuit platforms: A survey

Hamza Kheddar

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.AR, cs.PF, cs.RO, cs.SY, eess.SY

Reinforcement learning (RL) enables adaptive and autonomous decision-making in embedded, edge, and heterogeneous computing systems. Field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) and integrated circuits (ICs) offer low latency, energy efficiency, and architectural flexibility for RL deployment, while introducing challenges in resource constraints, implementation complexity, and hardware-algorithm co-design. This review surveys more than 100 studies on RL implementations across FPGA and IC platforms, emphasizing architectures, implementation strategies, performance evaluation, and applications. The literature is organized by RL family, including model-based Markov decision processes, classical value-based methods, deep RL, actor-critic, policy-gradient, and multi-agent approaches. We further classify studies by abstraction level, hardware realization strategy, and application domain, examining how RL algorithms map onto reconfigurable and fixed hardware. Unlike surveys restricted to particular algorithms or platforms, this work provides a unified analysis of FPGA and IC implementations, covering acceleration, reconfigurable architectures, energy-aware control, and system-level optimization. Comparative assessments consider resource utilization, latency, throughput, power consumption, and reported performance gains. Finally, we identify recurring architectural patterns, practical limitations, open challenges, and research directions for efficient RL-driven hardware systems. The survey offers a consolidated reference for researchers developing and evaluating RL solutions on specialized hardware.

Scalable Stress-Constrained Topology Optimization: A Super-resolution and Simultaneous Approach Based on Machine Learning

Xiangyu Sun, Shirin Hosseinmardi, Ramin Bostanabad

cs.LG

Large-scale topology optimization (TO) remains computationally demanding, especially for stress-constrained problems. To address this challenge, we propose the simultaneous neural topology optimization (SNTO) framework where we represent state, adjoint, and design variables with custom neural networks (NNs) whose parameters are jointly optimized within a single optimization loop that leverages continuous adjoint analysis and automatic differentiation. This optimization minimizes a multi-component loss function that combines the design objective, an aggregation of the local stress constraints, and the potential energy functionals. Our NNs have a unique architecture that accelerates convergence while enabling the networks to capture sharp gradients and boundaries which are critical to stress-constrained TO. To achieve scalability to millions of degrees of freedom (DoF), we develop a hardware-aware training scheme in which the design domain is partitioned into subdomains that are distributed across multiple GPUs. To control peak GPU memory usage, we further slice each subdomain into smaller regions whose loss gradients are evaluated sequentially and accumulated to update the NN parameters. Unlike domain decomposition approaches, our partitioning naturally ensures field continuity across the subdomains and does not require the introduction of additional terms into the loss function. For validation, we first evaluate the proposed framework on various 2D stress-constrained benchmarks and compare our results to a conventional finite element-based approach, showing that SNTO produces super-resolution designs with sharper boundaries, smaller gray areas, and lower volume fractions. We then consider large-scale 3D problems with up to eight million collocation points and distribute the training over up to eight GPUs.

SimHack: Predicting the onset of detectable reward hacking before RL training

Junwei Deng, Alex Bie, Natalia Ponomareva

cs.LG

When training LLMs to code with reinforcement learning, reward hacking is often detectable but difficult to prevent, since (a) environment hardening is increasingly difficult as training environments grow more complex; and (b) training-time interventions incentivize models to obfuscate hacking behavior to bypass detection. We instead study whether we can predict when detectable reward hacking becomes dominant before executing an RL run, enabling us to select lower-risk recipes in advance. We find that the initial hacking rate alone does not determine onset, which varies substantially across training recipes and repeated runs. We thus formulate this problem as predicting the distribution of the reward hacking onset step under a target training recipe. We introduce SimHack, a simulator that learns reward hacking training dynamics from a handful of smaller-scale reference RL runs and generates counterfactual simulation runs of unseen target recipes without costly retraining. Across unseen target recipes, SimHack predicts the median onset to a relative error of 11.3% and achieves across-recipe rank correlation of 0.85, while generalizing to differences in model scale, curriculum, number of rollouts, and learning rate between reference and target recipes. As an application, we show that SimHack can guide training decisions by screening candidate RL curricula, discovering curricula over the same set of scenarios that train for 96.6% longer without hacking.

STAR-XPC: Set Transformer for Estimating X-ray Transmission in Polymer Composites

Can Erer, H. Birkan Yilmaz, Nebahat Aral

cs.LG, cs.AI

Evaluating the energy transmission ratio of an X-ray beam through a polymer composite is a challenging task in radiation shielding studies. The reference solution with Monte Carlo transport simulation is highly accurate but computationally expensive: each of our reference simulations is capped at $4\times10^9$ photon histories and a 20,000 s (over 5 hours) time limit, while closed-form analytical methods fail to represent such complex scenarios. We present an artificial neural network model that maps the unordered multi-set of molecular constituents, their mass fractions, and four bulk physical descriptors directly to the transmission ratio $I(t)/I_0$ in 1-3 milliseconds. The model is a Set Transformer, which makes it permutation invariant by construction and accepts mixtures of different sizes up to 11 constituents within a fixed-size padded set. Each constituent is embedded with a chemistry-pretrained transformer language model, ChemBERTa. In our proposed method, training data are produced by an automated PENELOPE/penEasy pipeline using the radiation source at 30, 80, 100, and 120 kV tube voltages over polyethylene-based shielding composite slabs of thicknesses spanning 0.15-2.5 mm. After preprocessing, the dataset contains $n = 4697$ simulated configurations on which a leakage-free, jointly stratified 10-fold cross-validation is performed, attaining mean squared error MSE = $(1.12 \pm 0.56)\times10^{-4}$, normalized-MSE percentage NMSE% = 0.0294% $\pm$ 0.0147%, and $R^2 = 0.9988$. The corresponding per-sample percentage errors give an alpha-trimmed ($\alpha = 0.10$) absolute percentage error (APE) of 1.66% and a median APE of 1.15%. Our model's inference is several orders of magnitude faster than the underlying Monte Carlo simulation (sub-millisecond per query vs. several hours for the reference run) with effective accuracy in predicting $I(t)/I_0$.

Big Claims, Small Margins: Do Reported GraphLLM Gains Survive Simple Baselines?

Michal Podstawski

cs.LG

LLM-based methods for node classification on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) routinely report state-of-the-art accuracy. We ask how these results compare with inexpensive baselines run on the same splits. On the splits of three recent studies, we tune four baseline representatives with validation-only selection and compare them with the highest mean that each paper reports for an LLM-based method in the matched setting. Correct & Smooth on TF-IDF features trails this reference by a median of 2.3 percentage points, and an MLP on frozen e5-large-v2 embeddings by 2.8 points. Correct & Smooth is within 3 points of the reference or better on 15 of 24 tasks, but the reference is significantly higher on 12 of 22 testable tasks, so several reported gains remain substantial. Reading released code next to the papers, we also document evaluation risks: selection of hyperparameters or checkpoints on the test set, labels of test-pool nodes used in training, baseline numbers reused without disclosure, and a text-label misalignment in a released Cora loader. Reports of the same method on the same split also differ by up to 12.5 points across papers whose configurations are not documented to match.

Offline Apprenticeship Learning for Human-Centric Environments

Md Mirajul Islam

cs.LG, cs.AI, (https://www.lib.ncsu.edu/resolver/1840.20/46996)

Effective decision-making in human-centric environments such as Intelligent Tutoring Systems (ITSs) and clinical healthcare requires policies that are adaptive, data-efficient, and ethically deployable. Apprenticeship Learning (AL) infers policies directly from expert demonstrations by recovering the underlying reward structure, enabling strictly offline learning without environment interaction. This dissertation develops a progression of offline AL frameworks organized around two challenges: reward modeling and demonstration quality. For reward modeling, EM-EDM clusters demonstrations by latent reward functions and induces cluster-specific policies, and THEMES partitions trajectories into temporally coherent sub-trajectories guided by a hierarchically learned reward regulator; across one educational and three clinical datasets, THEMES outperforms flat and hierarchical AL baselines. For demonstration quality, HALIDE integrates continuous quality signals into hierarchical policy induction, enabling principled reuse of mixed-quality data under distribution shift. CCHAL further attributes quality to individual decisions via Bayesian credit assignment, dual-arm criticality scoring that contrasts expert and non-expert behavior, and a Bradley-Terry contrastive IRL objective over failure trajectories, outperforming baselines including HALIDE on three clinical EHR datasets and an ITS dataset. Finally, a real-world classroom evaluation of StuEDM, an EDM-induced policy that preserves student agency and intervenes only on disagreement, shows significant learning gains over an Expert baseline and a higher pretest-adjusted posttest score than a CQL policy trained on a far larger corpus, while overriding students less than half as often. Together, this work establishes offline AL as a practical, data-efficient, and ethically viable paradigm for human-centric environments.

ReAPO: Retry-Aware Policy Optimization for Efficient Exploration in LLM Reasoning

Nghi Dao, Calder Morton-Ferguson, Noah Golowich, and Adam Block

cs.LG, cs.AI, stat.ML

RLVR improves language-model reasoning, but commonly relies on independent stochastic rollouts for exploration, which can repeatedly sample similar failed strategies. Prior work has seen success with approaches that guide exploration by introducing dependence into the sampling process by including incorrect solutions in the context for subsequent attempts. Still, these methods introduce the additional question of how to assign credit to each attempt. We propose to view the credit assignment through the lens of Stochastic Shortest Paths, a framework in classical reinforcement learning that seeks to minimize the expected cumulative cost to reach a terminal state (in our case, a verified successful response). We show that, under suitable simplifying assumptions, this perspective naturally leads to a simple modification to GRPO, which we call Retry-Aware Policy Optimization (ReAPO). ReAPO allocates additional generation to failed trajectories, allowing successful retries to be reinforced without rewarding preceding failures, and assigns credit in a theoretically principled way to each attempt. Across Qwen3 and OLMo-2 models from 0.6B to 4B and eight mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks, ReAPO improves first-attempt performance over GRPO as well as natural recent baseline algorithms, while additional ablations demonstrate the utility of both serial sampling and ReAPO's credit assignment. On Qwen3-0.6B, ReAPO also outperforms GRPO with twice the group size or twice the training steps, reaching their peak accuracy with 39% and 31% fewer generated tokens, respectively. Taken together, our results suggest that ReAPO is a simple yet effective approach for improving LM reasoning performance through improved exploration and retry-aware credit assignment.

Motion Equivariant Convolutional LSTM: When Motion Becomes a Gauge Symmetry

Vahid Hosseinzadeh, T. Anderson Keller, Michael Puthawala

cs.LG, cs.CV

Objects move, cameras pan, and weather drifts with the wind: much of what changes in sequential data is motion. Motion changes where things are, not what they are, and is therefore a symmetry of the data. Yet standard recurrent networks are not equivariant to it: their memory stays put while the input moves on. Flow equivariant recurrent networks first brought this symmetry into sequence models, for motions of constant velocity, or flows, by enumerating a bank of velocities. We extend this idea in three directions: from simple recurrent networks to convolutional LSTMs, from flows to motions whose velocity changes in time, and from enumerating velocities to estimating them. Treating a sequence as a field over time, we show that motion becomes a gauge symmetry: a global symmetry made local in time. Built on this view, our motion equivariant convolutional LSTM (MELSTM) uses its velocity model as the gauge field and is provably equivariant to every motion. Across future-frame prediction, classification of digits visible only through their motion, and action recognition under camera shake, MELSTM outperforms a convolutional LSTM and its flow equivariant counterpart with the same number of parameters: it is more accurate, generalizes to longer horizons and to unseen motions, and carries a fraction of the flow equivariant model's state.

Building a Nested LADDER for LLM Unstructured Post-Training Pruning

Nicol\'as Mauricio Cuadrado \'Avila, Ivo Gollini Navarrete, Martin Tak\'a\v{c}, Samuel Horv\'ath

cs.LG

Non-uniform allocation can improve post-training pruning of large language models, yet quality can still degrade sharply at high sparsity. To control degradation as sparsity increases, we introduce LADDER (Layer-Wise Allocation via Damage-Driven Evaluation and Refinement), which allocates sparsity incrementally. At each step, it evaluates pruning an additional fraction of weights from each eligible layer and commits the layer update that causes the least end-to-end degradation per removed parameter. This process produces non-uniform sparsity profiles and exactly nested masks across multiple budgets. We evaluate LADDER on five OPT, Llama, and Qwen models across 10-90% sparsity, using SparseGPT as its candidate-generation backend and comparing against uniform and adaptive allocation baselines. Results reveal a clear operating-regime trade-off: LADDER is comparable or slightly weaker at benign sparsity but becomes substantially more robust from 70% onward. This high-sparsity advantage largely persists after posterior distillation across four of five models and translates into improved retention of aggregate zero-shot accuracy.

Second Thoughts: Exploring and Planning through Comparative Imagination in World Models

Hieu Tran, Hong Yu

cs.LG, cs.AI

World models let agents compare imagined outcomes before acting, but an agent must still decide which alternatives to explore and when a proposed plan should replace its learned policy. We introduce SDI (Sibling Disagreement in Imagination), which connects these decisions through a shared actor-based reference. SDI compares action sequences imagined from the same model state, using repeated rollouts of the actor's own sequence to measure variation under fixed behavior. This reference scales both the spread across candidate returns, which provides an exploration signal, and a proposed plan's predicted advantage, which governs action acceptance. The agent executes a planned action when its advantage exceeds the reference variability by a fixed factor; otherwise, it follows the actor. SDI reuses the base agent's world model, actor, and critic without requiring an additional disagreement ensemble. Exploration and planning guide training data collection, while evaluation uses the learned actor alone. Across Meta-World, Adroit, and DM-Control, SDI achieves a higher observed task-average score than every compared baseline in each suite and matches or exceeds the strongest baseline's performance on 15 of 18 tasks. Component ablations and robustness analyses further examine the roles of exploration, planning exposure, and action acceptance. Paired evaluations from identical simulator states show that the gate improves both recovery from actor failures and preservation of successful behavior relative to ungated planning, while substantially reducing return degradation. Together, these results support comparing imagined alternatives against a shared actor reference as a unified approach to exploring and planning with world models.

Extreme Low-State Quantized Transformer for Electricity-Load Forecasting

Muhammad Sabbir Alam, Harindra Sandun Mavikumbure, Victor Cobilean, Milos Manic, and Jayasimha Atulasimha

cs.LG

Aggressive low-state weight quantization can substantially reduce neural-network representational flexibility, making predictive performance difficult to preserve when only a few discrete weight states are available. We investigate this regime for Transformer-based electricity-load forecasting using 3-, 5-, 7-, and 16-state weight quantization across feed-forward-only and attention-plus-feed-forward quantization. Two tasks predict either the next hour or the next 24 hours from the preceding 48 hours across two co-primary electricity-consumption datasets. Direct post-training quantization (PTQ) increased mean normalized root-mean-square error (NRMSE) relative to full precision by 6.25 and 7.04 percentage points in Datasets A and B, whereas quantization-aware fine-tuning (QAT-FT) recovered approximately 90.2% and 94.5% of these penalties. Quantization-aware training from random initialization (Scratch-QAT) also produced competitive low-state solutions, with particularly favorable behavior at three states. We further introduce accumulated-update thresholding, which accumulates optimizer-proposed weight changes before commitment. Relative to paired Scratch-QAT controls, this reduced committed shadow-weight updates by approximately 99.7% and discrete state transitions by approximately 77--80%, while mean NRMSE changed by only +0.042 and +0.197 percentage points. These results show that useful Transformer forecasting can be retained under highly discrete weight representations while strongly suppressing target-state activity, supporting a prospective route toward compact, low-activity edge forecasting using multilevel synaptic devices.

Measure First, Then Allocate: Calibrated Rollout Budgets for MCP Agents in Reinforcement Learning

Hieu Tran, Zonghai Yao, Sunjae Kwon, Hong Yu

cs.LG, cs.CL

Training tool-using language models requires costly interaction, yet identical rewards within a rollout group provide no group-relative learning signal. We use reward disagreement to guide harness evaluation and the allocation of rollouts for policy training in Model Context Protocol (MCP) environments. We first introduce Discord, which tests harness edits using exact paired inference and decision-preserving early stopping: only disagreeing paired outcomes favor one harness over another. This insight motivates prospective allocation in BRACE (Bank-calibrated Rollout Allocation under Correlated Episodes). BRACE combines historical task evidence, uncertainty in task success rates, and a correlated reward model to forecast mixed-reward groups before allocating generation budget. Controlled harness experiments favor Discord, while live MCP experiments expose a retention-cost trade-off. At three Qwen3-VL model scales, BRACE exceeds the strongest plotted baseline peaks with fewer new training rollouts; historical-bank collection is counted separately. It also leads the compared methods on BFCL and NESTFUL without further training. Ablations probe the bank, estimator, and allocation score; prospective evaluation checks forecast calibration. These results support forecasting reward contrast before generation.

ArcFlow: Training Flow Policies for Multiple Solver Budgets

Hieu Tran, Hong Yu

cs.LG, cs.AI

Flow-matching policies adjust inference cost through the number of solver steps, but reinforcement learning at a single fixed solver budget can hide degradation at cheaper deployment budgets and even erase useful one-step behavior present in a pretrained policy. We introduce Action-Ratio-Clip Flow (ArcFlow), a deployment-aware algorithm for training one flow policy across a specified range of solver budgets. ArcFlow combines action-aligned supervision, budget exposure, and worst-budget checkpoint selection. For reconstruction objectives, its bridge aggregates temporal probes into one surrogate ratio and one PPO-style clip per action, with a boundary residual exactly equal to deployed one-step reconstruction error. Cycling exposes the shared field to the deployment range, and selection optimizes its performance floor. For native velocity objectives, multi-budget endpoint supervision applies the same principle without replacing the native update. Both realizations preserve the architecture and deployment-time cost. Across DM Control, manipulation, and locomotion, ArcFlow attains the highest mean one-step return among five flow-policy baselines on 25 of 30 tasks under each method's reported training settings. Component studies identify complementary improvements from boundary supervision, the fixed-grid bridge, budget exposure, and deployment selection. These results establish deployment-budget-aware training as an effective route to strong low-step control with one policy serving multiple inference costs.

CALLIOPE: Private Insights into AI Use, Without Embeddings

Edith Cohen, Vadym Doroshenko, Badih Ghazi, Charlie Harrison, Pritish Kamath, Alexander Knop, Ravi Kumar, Ethan Leeman, Pasin Manurangsi, Mikhail Pravilov, Adam Sealfon, Yurii Sushko, Marika Swanberg, Chiyuan Zhang

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CR

We introduce CALLIOPE, a top-down framework for discovering topic hierarchies in large corpora of unstructured data (e.g., LLM conversations), while supporting differential privacy (DP). An Explicit Feedback Contract for Iterative ML-Agent Harnesses

Kehui Yao, Chuanbo Hu, Jiao Chen, Ramin Giahi, Reza Yousefi Maragheh, Lalitesh Morishetti, Ziwei Hong, Yiyun Su, Jianpeng Xu, Evren Korpeoglu, Kannan Achan

cs.LG

Iterative ML agents develop models by editing code, running experiments, reading a validation score, and choosing what to try next. In current harnesses this loop is served by an exact validation score returned after every attempt, which leaves the validation split open to adaptive selection. In this work, we introduce an explicit feedback contract for ML-agent harnesses, which specifies what an agent may observe, when iteration should stop, and when the test split may be touched. The contract releases a validation score only when a submission clears a noise threshold computed from the paired standard error, so no margin has to be tuned. We ran experiments on click-through-rate prediction, learning to rank, and a control task with no signal to find. The control is the sharpest case, because the true gain there is zero by construction: an agent reading exact scores ended by reporting a gain of 0.0157, while under the contract it reported 0.0033. On both real tasks the contract narrows the validation-test gap, and test performance is slightly higher rather than lower. The largest difference is in when the agents stop. Exact feedback used its entire budget of thirty submissions on two of the three tasks, including the one where nothing can be learned, while the contract halted after four to twelve. A run that exhausts its budget is usually read as diligence. It is better read as a symptom: when the feedback channel is unbounded, noise keeps supplying improvements to chase.

Towards Realistic Models of Change: A Causal Perspective on Decision-Making and Dynamical Systems

Isacco Beretta

cs.LG, cs.AI, Intelligence),, 2026., 175, pages

While statistical learning has made remarkable progress in recent decades, driven by large datasets and data-driven protocols that have transformed scientific practice, it still faces a key limitation: it struggles to generalize beyond the data it has seen, often failing under distribution shift. Causality offers a promising solution: causal models focus on the underlying mechanisms of change rather than surface-level associations, enabling robust reasoning under external interventions. This allows systems not only to learn from data, but also to reason about hypothetical scenarios, adapt to new environments, and inform better decisions. This thesis explores how these approaches can inform each other, in two parts. The first part examines algorithmic recourse, that is, identifying which changes an individual can make to overturn an unfavorable decision. We propose an alternative perspective to the established framework, arguing for a more realistic cost function, a reformulation of the problem in causal terms, and the inclusion of a temporal dimension in policy evaluation. The second part focuses on dynamical systems, extending the notions of causal interventions and effects to discrete and continuous stochastic processes. We show that the equilibria of these processes, and how they change following an intervention, can be linked to Structural Causal Models (SCMs), viewed as compressed representations of the underlying temporal dynamics. We argue for the relevance of such temporal causal models, since in real-world scenarios actions take time to produce their effects. The common goal of both parts is practical: the utility, robustness, and trustworthiness of these models depend critically on their ability to realistically represent the underlying phenomena.
