Approximate Solomonoff Induction: On the Robustness of Learned Universal Predictors

Qian Li, Shang-Hua Teng

cs.LG, stat.ML

Why large language models trained via log-loss minimization over massive and diverse datasets exhibit broad capabilities and generalizable skills is not yet fully understood. Grau-Moya et al. (ICML 2024) offer a conceptual explanation through the lens of meta-learning: if sequence-generating environments $\nu$ are drawn from a prior $w_{\nu}$, minimizing population log-loss recovers the corresponding Bayesian mixture predictor $\xi=\sum_\nu w_\nu\nu$. In the idealized limit in which $w$ is a universal prior, the optimal predictor corresponds to Solomonoff universal induction. Practical machine learning algorithms, however, generally produce only \emph{approximately} optimal predictors. This raises a natural question: \emph{Does the prediction performance remain robust under approximation}? In this paper, we first provide a tight bound on the worst-case effect of approximation on prediction error. We show any predictor $\rho$ satisfying $D_{KL}(\xi \|\rho)\leq \eta$ retains bounded cumulative-expected-squared prediction error. However, while the Bayesian mixture predictor $\xi$ has error $O(\log(1/w_\mu))$ in an environment $\mu$, we establish a tight $\Theta(\eta/w_\mu)$ bound on the excess error of $\rho$ relative to $\xi$. Thus, approximation can substantially degrade performance in environments assigned small prior probability. In sharp contrast, when environments are averaged according to prior $w$, we prove that the excess prediction error is only $O(\eta)$. Furthermore, we establish a distribution-shift theorem for dominated shifts from a training prior $w$ to a test prior $w'$, interpolating our average-case and worst-case guarantees. Finally, we extend our robustness analysis to a finite-sampling setting relevant to learning universal predictors with neural networks and Transformers, where $D_{KL}(\xi\|\rho)$ could be unbounded. [For the full abstract, see the paper.]

ExpEvolve: Structuring and Reusing Experience for Open-Ended Discovery

Yongqiang Chen, Chenxi Liu, Deyu Zou, Zhenhao Chen, Tongliang Liu, Bo Han, Kun Zhang

cs.LG, cs.CL, stat.ML

Evolutionary coding agents such as AlphaEvolve are one of the notable successes in using Large Language Models (LLMs) to build AI Scientists. These agents tackle open-ended scientific problems by iteratively improving and evolving programs, leveraging the prior knowledge and reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Despite their success, mutations over programs are mostly low-level upon surface heuristics, without a persistent unit of control. In contrast, science compounds through ideas. In this work, we propose ExpEvolve, which injects idea-level extraction and control into evolution: it discovers and leverages the high-level factors from programs that explain scoring differences among different programs. We characterize theoretical conditions on when discovery with idea factors improves sample efficiency. We also build a practical framework to show that the extracted high-level idea factors are helpful for guiding exploitation from experience and exploration when discovery comes to a plateau. Across 11 open-ended mathematical tasks, ExpEvolve significantly improves the final performance and sample efficiency up to 5.0x. The improvements also transfer to 4 other LLMs and to 6 machine learning tasks from MLS-Bench. Analysis shows that ExpEvolve can discover idea-level factors to provide better exploration and exploitation for evolutionary discovery.

Interpretable Cutting Plane Selection for Mixed-Integer Linear Programming Using Genetic Programming

Gwen maudet, Mathis Da Cunha, Gr\'egoire Danoy

cs.LG, math.OC

Cutting planes (cuts) remove fractional solutions from mixed-integer linear programs (MILPs) without excluding any feasible integer solutions, making them a key ingredient in modern MILP solvers. Current state-of-the-art solvers rely on fine-tuned handcrafted cut strategies, which, despite their effectiveness, remain inherently static and can be suboptimal. More recently, learning-based approaches have been proposed to design adaptive cut strategies; however, most of these methods are based on neural networks, offering limited interpretability and providing no guarantees of convergence. In this paper, we propose \emph{Genetic Programming and Statistics-based Cut Selection} (GPSCS), the first learning-based framework that explicitly generalizes handcrafted cut selection heuristics into a fully interpretable policy. First, a genetic programming model evolves a symbolic scoring function that ranks candidate cuts using features derived from the cuts and the solving context. Second, a lightweight statistics-based mechanism adaptively determines the number of top-ranked cuts to add at each separation round by detecting significant score dropouts. We evaluate GPSCS within the SCIP solver on seven classes of synthetic and real-world benchmark instances, with a strong emphasis on generalization: evaluation instances are on average 41 times harder than those used for training. Experimental results show that GPSCS improves average solving time by $6\%$ compared to SCIP's default cut selection strategy and achieves a two-fold speedup over both a recent neural-network-based approach and a no-cut baseline, demonstrating strong robustness and generalization across heterogeneous MILP families.

YingLan: A Multimodal Ocean Foundation Model for Unified Representation Learning from Heterogeneous Observations

Sen Jia, Siqi Wei, Junyu Wang, Zijun Lv, Pan Liu, Meng Xu, Xiyou Fu, Yuxi Sun, Qiang Liu, Shuyu Zhang, Lin Mu

cs.LG, cs.AI

Multimodal ocean modeling remains challenging due to substantial heterogeneity in data structures, physical semantics, and spatiotemporal characteristics across satellite imagery, gridded oceanographic fields, and buoy observations. Existing approaches largely focus on task-specific ocean modeling, while learning transferable representations from heterogeneous observations remains an open problem. To address this challenge, we propose YingLan, a multimodal ocean foundation model that integrates physics-attribute-conditioned dynamic encoding, Spatiotemporal Increment Coupling (STIC), and ocean-state evolution-aware pretraining. Specifically, the dynamic encoding mechanism generates adaptive input projections conditioned on physical semantics and observation characteristics, enabling flexible representation of heterogeneous ocean variables. STIC models multiscale spatiotemporal dependencies through cross-scale interactions of latent temporal increments. The pretraining strategy further combines masked observation reconstruction with temporal evolution objectives to facilitate the learning of coherent ocean-state representations across different prediction horizons. YingLan is implemented using MindSpore and trained on Huawei Ascend 910B NPUs, demonstrating its compatibility with domestic AI computing infrastructure. In a seven-day sea surface salinity (SSS) forecasting task conducted in the Shenzhen coastal waters, YingLan achieves an average RMSE of 0.687508 PSU, corresponding to relative error reductions of 0.58%, 5.31%, and 12.42% compared with LangYa, XiHe, and GloNet2, respectively. These results provide initial evidence of the model's effectiveness in regional ocean forecasting, while broader cross-task and cross-regional evaluations remain necessary to assess its generalization capabilities.

DISRL: Dual-Scale Incremental Semi-Supervised Reinforcement Learning for Dynamic Community Tracking

Tianming Zhang, Jinxu Wang, Yixuan Zhang, Zhengyi Yang, Chenyu Hou, Bin Cao, Jing Fan

cs.LG

Dynamic local community detection tracks the evolving community of designated seed nodes across graph snapshots. However, it faces two critical challenges: temporal supervision uncertainty, where sparse and outdated labels fail to provide seed-specific guidance under topology changes, and unbounded error cascades, where sequential policy transfer accumulates exploration errors over long horizons. To address these challenges, we propose DISRL, a Dual-scale Incremental Semi-supervised Reinforcement Learning framework. DISRL introduces a supervision construction module that dynamically generates seed-specific guidance from sparse known communities, and a dual-scale community exploration module that stabilizes policy evolution by decoupling short-term adaptation within temporal windows from long-term parameter consolidation across windows. Furthermore, we construct a dynamic graph benchmark with diverse community evolution scenarios. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DISRL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines under various dynamic settings.

BrainCodes: A Lightweight EEG Foundation Model over Discrete Neural Codes

Kleanthis Avramidis, Ivan Tashev, Dimitra Emmanouilidou

cs.LG, cs.NE

Recent electroencephalography (EEG) foundation models increasingly emphasize model scale, potentially limiting their deployment on resource-constrained devices and in everyday environments. We argue that transferable EEG features can instead be learned with a compact architecture and introduce BrainCodes, a lightweight foundation model pretrained through self-supervised learning on more than 800 hours of recordings from over 1,000 participants. Our architecture combines a quantization-based neural tokenizer with a spatiotemporal encoder that supports arbitrary electrode configurations. The pretraining objective explicitly encourages representations that capture spatial connectivity through channel positional encoding, and sensitivity to narrowband neural oscillations through an auxiliary training objective. Our model matches or surpasses the state of the art across diverse neurophysiological monitoring tasks, including sleep staging, attention assessment, and cognitive load estimation. BrainCodes transfers effectively to low-resource wearable devices while using up to 97% fewer trainable parameters than comparable architectures. Beyond predictive accuracy, our evaluation framework characterizes the geometry and invariances of learned EEG representations across measurable spatial, temporal, and spectral signal properties.

Reader--Writer Coupling in Transformer MLPs

Giyeong Oh, Sunghee Ahn, Youngjae Yu

cs.LG, cs.CL

A transformer MLP gives each hidden channel two roles: a reader that decides when the channel responds, and a writer that decides what it adds back to the residual stream. Read as a key--value memory, the layer can be measured one factor set at a time: the pairing of readers to writers is carried only by their shared index, invisible to any quantity computed from one factor set alone. Yet training couples the two factors through their joint update, so we ask what reading them together shows. The block supplies the joint instrument: the MLP writes back into the very stream it reads, so the exact Jacobian of its update is square, and its agreement with its own transpose resolves into reader--writer terms whose inner products give each channel, read as a couple, a signed pairing coordinate. Ranked by this coordinate, each block we measure has a small core of strongly coupled channels; where initialization gives either sign equally, the core is nearly all opposed: each writer points against the direction its reader responds to. This opposed core recurs from the smallest models trained from scratch to released checkpoints; its sign is in place within a few percent of training and holds while the coupling grows. Read as couples, most channels return their contribution against the reader that fired it, and the readers of later blocks take it up; few show in the logits. Training holds the couples in place: flipping the opposed writers moves the output toward common tokens, and a writer permuted onto another reader regrows to fit that reader while the reader stays.

Benchmarking and Error Analysis of Deep Search and Deep Research LLM Agents

Zixuan Yu, Zhenheng Tang, Xiaowen Chu, Bo Han

cs.LG, (FAGEN),, Seoul,, South, Korea,, 2026

As Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve into autonomous Deep Search and Deep Research Agents capable of executing long-horizon, multi-step tasks, evaluating their reliability becomes increasingly critical. However, existing evaluation paradigms predominantly focus on final task outcomes, leaving a significant gap in understanding the intermediate execution traces, where agents are highly susceptible to various errors. To address this critical gap, we introduce a comprehensive methodology and analytical framework designed specifically to diagnose errors in intermediate workflows. Building upon this framework, we establish a structured taxonomy that categorizes these vulnerabilities into two primary dimensions: System-Level Errors and Task-Level Errors. Through a systematic examination of step-by-step execution traces and agent-environment interactions, we reveal how these underlying errors affect overall performance. Finally, through empirical experiments and systematic analysis of the identified error types, we provide actionable design guidelines, aiming to enhance the robustness, reasoning capabilities, and reliability of Deep Search and Deep Research agents.

Data Schedules and Learning Rate Schedules Should Be Designed Together

Haodong Wen, Kairong Luo, Tingkai Yan, Wenguang Chen, Kaifeng Lyu

cs.LG, cs.CL

Mid-training (MT) is a training phase in LLM training pipelines that continues pretraining (PT) on smaller datasets of higher value. Taking the PT and MT phases as a whole, training usually starts with only PT data and then transitions completely to MT data. In this work, we view this complete transition from PT to MT data as a special case of a broader class of data mixing schedules that specify how the proportions of PT and MT tokens in each batch change over training. Under fixed total training compute and MT token budgets, we ask: Is this complete transition to MT data optimal for minimizing final MT loss? We show that the answer depends on the learning rate (LR) schedule: one data schedule may outperform another under one LR schedule, but changing only the LR schedule can reverse the outcome. Specifically, when the LR decays throughout continued training, introducing MT data earlier and mixing it with PT data lowers the final MT loss compared with placing all MT data at the end of training, but this result is reversed if we use constant LR. We therefore study how to jointly choose when to introduce MT data and when to decay the LR through ablation studies, and find that it is better to decay the LR only after MT data enters training. Finally, we develop a theoretical model that explains why the optimal data schedule for a fixed MT token budget depends on the LR schedule.

GaP-MoE : Explicit Expert Specialization in Mixture-of-Experts via Orthogonal Gradient Accumulation and Projection

Donghyun Jeong, Yoon-Yeong Kim

cs.LG, stat.ML

Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (SMoE) models improve scalability by routing input tokens to only a subset of expert networks, but they often suffer from representation collapse, in which multiple experts converge toward similar functions and lose their intended diversity. Prior work has addressed this limitation either by improving router architectures or by introducing auxiliary loss terms that implicitly penalize similarity among experts. However, router-based approaches remain vulnerable to routing collapses, and loss-based approaches can conflict with the task's original objective during optimization. In this work, we propose GaP-MoE (Gradient Accumulation and Projection in MoE), which induces expert specialization by directly manipulating expert output gradients rather than modifying the loss function. At each training step, GaP-MoE stochastically selects one of the $TopK$ routed experts as a leader based on normalized routing scores, and projects the gradients of the remaining follower experts onto the orthogonal complement of the subspace spanned by the leader's recent gradients. This removes directional overlap between expert gradients without altering the task loss, allowing specialization to emerge as a direct consequence of the optimization dynamics rather than an externally imposed constraint. We evaluate GaP-MoE in both fine-tuning settings (BERT-based, on GLUE) and from-scratch pretraining settings (GPT-2-based, on WikiText-103, enwik8, and text8), and further confirm its compatibility as a drop-in module for existing SMoE architectures. Experimental results show that GaP-MoE achieves performance comparable to or better than standard SMoE.

Compositional neural networks for multimodal X-ray and neutron scattering inversion

Valentin Munteanu, Alexander Hinderhofer, Dmitry Lapkin, Vladimir Starostin, Frank Schreiber

cs.LG, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, physics.app-ph

X-ray and neutron scattering are indispensable for structural characterization across condensed-matter physics and materials science. Yet, because scattering experiments generally record intensities without phase information, distinct structures can yield similar scattering patterns, rendering the corresponding inverse problems non-unique. This ambiguity can be reduced by combining complementary measurements, but their integration into machine-learning frameworks remains challenging. Here we introduce a modular framework for probabilistic multi-measurement analysis based on prior-amortized flow matching. Single-observation posterior estimators are trained separately for individual observation types or physical probes and combined only at inference time through a compositional sampler. This late fusion approach allows pretrained single-observation models to be reused without dedicated joint retraining. As a case in point, we consider reflectometry, which provides depth-resolved, nanometer-scale information about thin films and interfaces. Our single-observation demonstrations span X-ray, time-of-flight neutron and polarized neutron reflectometry. In separate co-refinement studies, task-matched estimators enable homogeneous composition across multiple neutron contrasts and heterogeneous multimodal composition of X-ray and neutron measurements. Together, these results establish compositional flow matching as a modular approach to uncertainty-aware inversion and co-refinement, with potential applicability to other scattering problems in which multiple observations constrain a common physical structure while retaining measurement-specific parameters.

Cascading Bandits with Noisy Observations: Algorithms and Regret Bounds

Yashaswini Kaluguri, Nikhil Karamchandani

cs.LG, stat.ML

We propose a novel stochastic cascading bandit setup where probing an ordered shortlist of $K$ out of $L$ arms incurs position-dependent costs, and the learner observes only noisy feedback regarding each arm's true output. We identify a sub-class of noise models for which the optimal shortlist can be found by sorting arms according to a per-arm score: such a score exists for both selection and ordering when the channel has no false positives, and for ordering alone (but not selection) when measurements are costless; however in general neither selection nor ordering is separable once noise and cost are both present. For the case where both selection and ordering scores exist, we provide a UCB-based algorithm with $O((L-K)\log T)$ instance-dependent regret over a time horizon $T$, which matches an algorithm-agnostic lower bound (up to constant factors). For the more challenging scenario where scores don't exist, we develop a new algorithmic template, DP-UCB, that embeds optimism directly inside an exact dynamic program, yielding $O\big(LK\log T\big)$ regret. Finally, we validate our theoretical results through extensive numerical evaluations and simulations over both synthetic and real-world datasets.

Parallelizable Probabilistic Latent Reasoning for Diverse Reasoning Trajectories

Seokhyun Lee, Seongjun Lee, Changhee Lee

cs.LG, cs.AI

Language models relying on verbalized Chain-of-Thought (CoT) are constrained to token-by-token generation in the language space, where many tokens serve textual coherence rather than core reasoning. This entangles genuine differences in logical trajectories with superficial variations in verbalization. Moving reasoning into a compact, continuous latent space offers a promising alternative for efficiently representing and exploring diverse reasoning trajectories. However, current latent models remain largely deterministic, capturing only a single reasoning trajectory rather than modeling a distribution over multiple valid alternatives. While stochastic approaches parameterize distributions over latent reasoning steps, they frequently suffer from unstable optimization and limited empirical performance. Furthermore, their reliance on autoregressive latent prediction during training precludes effective parallelization, leading to prohibitively long training times. To overcome these bottlenecks, we propose Probabilistic Latent Reasoning Models (PLRM), a parallelizable variational framework for probabilistic latent reasoning. PLRM adopts a JEPA-style objective in which the posterior infers latent distributions in parallel from observed reasoning trajectories, while the prior learns to predict the subsequent latent distribution via teacher forcing. To model heterogeneous reasoning processes, we introduce a discrete strategy variable together with a mixture-based prior, allowing the model to preserve the Gaussian-mixture structure induced by multiple distinct reasoning trajectories rather than collapsing them into a single Gaussian. Across diverse benchmarks, PLRM achieves performance competitive with deterministic latent reasoning models on single-trajectory datasets while enabling efficient training and consistently outperforming competing stochastic baselines on multi-trajectory datasets.

Nominate-then-Adjudicate: LLM-Assisted One-Pass and Per-Instance MIP Solver Tuning

Yifan Zhao, Qianyang Zhao, Xinyu Guan, Kai Wei, and Yuming Deng

cs.LG, BigData, 2026

Mixed-integer programming solvers expose hundreds or even thousands of parameters whose best setting varies per instance, yet per-instance search is prohibitively expensive. We frame this as configuration for heterogeneous Big Data optimization workloads and propose a nominate-then-adjudicate pipeline built around two design choices. First, a fixed portfolio is sampled once and reused across instances, removing per-instance configuration search at inference; on the evaluated families, the resulting shortlist retains substantial oracle headroom, making selection within it the empirical focus. Second, the fixed 20-feature representation can map structurally different instances to the same vector, preventing a feature-only selector from distinguishing them even when they prefer different configurations. The foundation model receives no task-specific weight updates, and selection is one-pass: one model call and no solver calls at inference. The study represents structural Variety through distinct formulation families and decision Value through solver-performance outcomes; the one-call, zero-solve protocol specifies the decision interface and leaves end-to-end latency unmeasured. Among comparisons exceeding a 20% practical-difference threshold, the complete adjudicator records 538 wins and 185 losses against the top nominee, while its win rate against the default rises from 84.9% to 90.2%. Against per-instance configuration baselines under a matched one-pass protocol it leads the cluster-level methods and the best-performing evaluated per-instance baseline, ISAC+.

Progressive Permutation Alignment with Temperature-Controlled Spectral Sharpening

Yuta Horioka, Naoya Chiba, Yuki Uranishi

cs.LG, cs.CV, https://uranishi-lab.github.io/progressive-permutation-alignment/

In implicit neural representations (INRs), 3D signals are encoded directly in network weights, making stable weight-space operations essential. However, permutation symmetry causes independently trained yet functionally equivalent models to appear distant in weight space, often inducing large loss barriers under linear interpolation. Existing neuron alignment methods either commit early to discrete matches and become trapped in inconsistent local optima or rely on computationally expensive gradient optimization over continuous relaxations, both of which are unreliable in challenging cases such as NeRF. We adopt progressive alignment, a layer-wise strategy that follows inter-layer dependencies from input to output. At each layer, a correlation matrix is estimated and aligned with a discrete anchor obtained by the Hungarian algorithm, after which temperature-controlled spectral annealing gradually sharpens the correlation structure toward a permutation matrix. This update is deterministic and lightweight, avoiding the inner-loop gradient optimization required by continuous relaxation methods. A final refinement stage further optimizes the network layer-by-layer via weight matching, starting from the aligned solution. Experiments on MLPs, CNNs, and Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) show that the proposed method reliably recovers neuron correspondences and achieves stable alignment across architectures. In practical experiments aligning independently trained NeRF models, our method reduces the loss barriers under weight interpolation compared with existing approaches.

The Predictive--Optimization Gap in GuanDan Self-Play

Qicheng Zou, Biao Yuan, Siyu Song, Wentao Liu, Xiaokun Wang

cs.LG, stat.ML

Outcome-predictive signals are often treated as promising optimization targets in reinforcement learning, but predictive validity and optimization utility are different claims. We expose this distinction in GuanDan, a multi-round imperfect-information game in which the implication of a round outcome depends on the public match state. Held-out analysis shows that state--gain interactions provide incremental predictive value beyond a constant signed-gain effect, establishing that match progress is predictively meaningful. We then use changes in a frozen match-win predictor, $\Delta\Phi$, as a controlled round-level supervision intervention in a matched DanZero-style Monte Carlo self-play protocol. The intervention can succeed in an individual lineage: a selected progress-augmented checkpoint substantially outperforms its matched Baseline on a locked evaluation. Yet the method-level result reverses this impression: none of five preregistered replication seeds is positive, the pooled ten-seed interval includes zero, and across-iteration effects repeatedly change sign without a common late-stage advantage. These results identify a predictive--optimization gap: under the studied learner, a signal can describe outcome-relevant progress while failing to provide a reliably beneficial optimization direction.

Alternative Strategies for Active Learning with Bayesian Bradley Terry Model

Saman Jayasinghe, Sean Walton, Alma Rahat, Andy Gray, Chris Wheadon

cs.LG, stat.OT

Comparative judgement uses pairwise comparisons to rank items, but recovering accurate rankings from a limited number of judgements depends on both the inference method and the pairs selected. We developed a Bayesian Bradley-Terry model (BBTM) that uses Polya-Gamma data augmentation for efficient Gibbs sampling and quantifies uncertainty in latent abilities and ranks. We propose three active learning strategies based on the knowledge gradient (KG) principle. We define our acquisition functions using Kendall's tau distance, posterior rank entropy, and posterior separation. Approximate one-step updates to posterior moments reduce the computational cost of evaluating candidate pairs. We also introduce Posterior Separation Reliability (PSR), which measures the spread of posterior mean abilities relative to posterior uncertainty. It provides an internal indicator of ranking reliability without requiring a reference ranking. In synthetic experiments covering different item counts, comparison budgets, and noise levels, BBTM generally recovers ranking more accurately than preference-based Bayesian comparative judgement (PBCJ). At low to moderate noise levels, the pairwise entropy pair selection strategy performs best in ranking recovery. However, at the highest noise levels, the KG strategy based on posterior separation produces better point rankings. PSR increases alongside improvements in ranking accuracy as comparisons accumulate. Robustness experiments identify the proportion of informative outcomes needed to meet specified accuracy targets within a fixed comparison budget. In a moderate noise setting, replacing 30\% of outcomes with random choices has little effect on ranking performance. These findings demonstrate the value of Bayesian inference and active learning pair selection for recovering ranks from limited, noisy paired comparisons.

Generalization Bounds via Vector-Valued $L_p$ Correlation $V_\alpha$

Mohammad Hossein Momeni Hamaneh, Mohammad Mahdi Mojahedian, Mohammad Hossein Yassaee

cs.LG, cs.IT, math.IT

Understanding generalization of randomized learning algorithms remains a central challenge. Mutual-information and total-variation bounds provide complementary ways to quantify data--hypothesis dependence, but they can behave quite differently in high-dimensional regimes. We study a one-parameter family of dependence measures, the vector-valued $L_p$-norm correlation $V_\alpha$, and derive generalization bounds whose dependence terms interpolate between total variation (TV) at $\alpha=1$ and variance and $\chi^2$ related behavior at $\alpha=2$. For $\alpha>2$, the H\"older-conjugate loss moment is below two, yielding heavy-tailed specializations that can remain finite even when the loss variance is infinite. A particularly interpretable specialization occurs at $\alpha=2$, where the bound can be expressed in terms of the variability of the algorithm's output density across datasets, $\int \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_S[p(w\mid S)]}~ dw$. We show that the entire $V_\alpha$ family is invariant under smooth bijective reparameterizations of the hypothesis space. We also introduce an adaptive density stability (ADS) condition that implies explicit control of $V_2$, and we verify it for Gibbs-type posteriors under bounded losses (yielding rates that depend on the inverse temperature and sample size). To address scaling, we extend the framework to a conditional formulation using supersamples, analogous in spirit to conditional mutual information (CMI), and discuss evaluations in the output space as a practical surrogate for estimating dependence. Simulations on Bayesian linear regression illustrate how tuning $\alpha$ can change the tightness of the resulting bounds across regimes. Additional supersample experiments on noisy MNIST and on a pretrained ResNet-50 for CIFAR-10 show similar behavior of the projected loss-space bounds beyond the original clean-MNIST setting.

BIRD-RL: Scaling Agentic Reinforcement Learning over Stateful Data-Centric Environments

Ge Qu, Jinyang Li, Xiaohan Xu, Xiaolong Li, Bowen Qin, Nan Huo, Ziwei Tang, Chenhao Ma, Reynold Cheng

cs.LG, cs.CL

While agentic reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for training small language models (SLMs), existing frameworks are predominantly tool-centric and struggle with data-centric environments. Specifically, applying tool-centric frameworks to stateful tasks like SQL debugging introduces severe spatial inefficiencies since proper environment isolation typically requires a separate database container per rollout. Naively reusing databases without tracking evolving states across multi-turn interactions causes trajectory contamination and cross-trajectory interference, which severely destabilizes policy updates. To overcome these bottlenecks, we introduce BIRD-RL, a novel four-stage agentic RL curriculum that alternately enhances foundational agent reasoning and trajectory-adaptive on-policy exploration to circumvent the instability inherently associated with direct end-to-end learning. Crucially, to support this curriculum, BIRD-RL is driven by a Trajectory-scoped Persistent Execution Infrastructure, which enables safe database replica reuse while preserving trajectory isolation. This design improves spatial RL efficiency by 12.8x, successfully enabling scalable agentic RL for complex data-centric tasks without needs of Kubernettes. Empirical evaluations on the BIRD-CRITIC-SQLite benchmark demonstrate that our 7B and 14B models achieve Success Rates of 44.40% and 48.00%, respectively, showing competitive performance against strong proprietary model-based agents such as Claude-Opus-4.6 Agent (48.20%). Furthermore, BIRD-RL successfully trains unified SLMs capable of both advanced SQL debugging and generation, highlighting its broader potential for multi-objective optimization across heterogeneous, real-world data-centric tasks.

One Act, Many Masks: Effect-Marginal Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon Agents

Shaojin Ma, Ruitao Wang, Pengyi Li, Jie Yang, Liangyu Chen, Gangyi Zhao, Hongyao Tang, Yan Zheng, and Jianye Hao

cs.LG, stat.ML

Recent advances in multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) have substantially improved the performance of LLM agents on complex, long-horizon interactive tasks. However, despite progress in fine-grained credit assignment, policy optimization still applies these learning signals through response-specific probability ratios. Different textual responses can induce the same executable effect, yet credit differences arising from subsequent outcomes remain tied to their individual textual realizations. We identify this mismatch as credit-update misalignment. Our analysis reveals that credit-text pairing directly shapes policy-update directions, even when the policy context and executable effect are held fixed. Motivated by this finding, we propose Effect-Marginal Policy Optimization (EMPO), which aligns policy optimization with execution-level equivalence through an effect-level surrogate that couples updates across alternative textual realizations. EMPO preserves the original credit signals, integrates with both trajectory-level and step-level credit estimators, and requires no additional critics or rollouts. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Sokoban show that EMPO outperforms strong baselines in both task performance and learning efficiency.

How Far Can Foundation Models Be Distilled for Efficient Jet Tagging?

Jay Agarwal, Siddharth Khare, Dhruv Kumar

cs.LG, hep-ex, hep-ph, physics.data-an, https://github.com/JayAgarwal25/distilling-jet-taggers

Jet tagging is one of the most important applications of machine learning at the Large Hadron Collider. Recent models build in more physics knowledge or rely on large-scale pretraining, but the experiments place strict limits on their inference cost. Knowledge distillation can transfer their performance to small students that are cheap to run. We ask which properties of a teacher help the student, how small a student can get and how close it can come to the state of the art. On a jet tagging benchmark, we distil six teachers that differ in architecture, symmetry and pretraining, including two foundation models, into five small students. Every teacher improves every student, and the gain depends mainly on the strength of the teacher. We then shrink our two strongest distilled students, Transformer and L-GATr-slim, in width and in depth. With 20 thousand parameters, a distilled L-GATr-slim outperforms every published jet tagger trained from scratch at a fraction of the inference cost of every teacher. Even with two thousand parameters, it matches ParT, a model with a thousand times as many parameters. We also study what the students learn from their teachers. Our benchmark of inference cost shows that students of the same size can differ by almost an order of magnitude in speed. We release the code and the weights of all trained models.

Asymmetry Tunneling Beyond Adaptive Equivariance Relaxation

Eunho Jeong, Joon Jang, Hyeonjin Kim

cs.LG

Equivariant neural networks encode prescribed symmetries as powerful inductive biases, yet real-world data may exhibit symmetry-breaking structure that these priors cannot fully capture. Prior work has largely addressed this challenge through model-side equivariance relaxation, seeking an appropriate level of equivariance for the data. This raises a complementary question: must equivariance relaxation be adaptively controlled? To investigate this question, we introduce a residual path alongside a strictly equivariant model and deliberately restrict their interface to examine the transmission of task-relevant structure. Across five benchmarks, we observe both residual collapse and survival despite these restrictions; we call the latter phenomenon asymmetry tunneling. We further characterize this phenomenon through four complementary diagnostics. Our results suggest that adaptive control of equivariance relaxation is not a prerequisite for capturing potential symmetry-breaking structure.

Density-Level Backward Error for Low-NFE Generative Flows

Ewsey Obzherin, Valentin Khrulkov

cs.LG, cs.NA, math.NA, stat.ML

Low-NFE generative ODE sampling is constrained not only by solver order, but also by how a fixed budget of network evaluations is distributed along the flow. We develop a density-level backward-error framework that identifies the component of a solver's modified-equation defect that changes the evolving marginal, while excluding solenoidal trajectory distortion that is invisible in Wasserstein tangent geometry. The resulting residual induces a solver-order-aware compute-allocation rule and, through gauge freedom, an objective for selecting numerically favorable velocity representatives of the same density path. This separates our approach from schedule search driven by trajectory or endpoint surrogates and from higher-order solver design. Exact Gaussian and mixture models establish the mechanism, including cases where curvature-based allocation fails and continuum-optimal transport is not discretization-optimal. On a pretrained CIFAR-10 EDM probability-flow ODE, density-aware allocation consistently improves fixed-update Euler sampling in the low-NFE regime. Matched-seed convergence to a dense Euler reference confirms that the method accelerates approximation of the same high-NFE flow rather than changing the asymptotic sampler.

Generative Modeling via Nonlinear Schr\"odinger Bridges and Branching Diffusions

Mathias Grau, Pierre Henry-Labord\`ere

cs.LG, cs.AI, stat.ML

We propose a new generative modeling framework grounded in forward-backward partial differential equations (PDEs). These equations arise naturally in mean-field game (MFG) theory, and are closely related to nonlinear Schr\"odinger systems. Our main contribution is to reinterpret this connection within the setting of generative modeling and to develop an efficient numerical methodology for its practical implementation. Specifically, we derive and characterize a nonlinear extension of the classical Schr\"odinger bridge problem through a coupled system of PDEs, yielding a model we call SBNL (Schr\"odinger Bridge NonLinear). To compute its solution, we design a simulation algorithm based on branching diffusions and the probabilistic representation of semilinear PDEs. The resulting method is embarrassingly parallel, scalable to high dimensions, and applicable to a broad class of nonlinear interaction functionals. We validate the approach through numerical experiments, including recovery of the classical Schr\"odinger bridge, nonlinear extensions with convex penalties (Gross-Pitaevskii) as well as potential interaction terms, and high-dimensional generative tasks such as image synthesis on the MNIST and FFHQ human face dataset. Furthermore, the same framework embeds score-based generative modeling, diffusion models, and tractable gaussian Schr\"odinger bridges.

Rethinking Agentic Model Orchestration for Time Series Forecasting

Tong Guan, Tianxiang Zhan, Shirui Pan, Ming Jin

cs.LG, cs.AI, Temporal, Systems, (FMTS)

Time series foundation models (TSFMs) perform unevenly across forecasting tasks. Recent work uses agentic model orchestration (AMO) to select or combine TSFMs, often outperforming individual forecasters. Yet these gains may reflect stronger or more complementary candidates rather than better orchestration. To disentangle these effects, we introduce FairAMO, a controlled evaluation protocol with frozen candidate forecasts. Experiments on GIFT-Eval show that AMO improves accuracy, but its gains are bounded by the candidate TSFMs. The best orchestrators reduce MASE by less than 6% relative to the best single TSFM across all tasks. These gains depend on complementarity (different candidates excel on different tasks) rather than the number of candidates. Adding members yields diminishing returns, and a redundant member can erase these gains. The value of historical evidence also saturates quickly. A few recent windows capture all gains, and further history adds none. These findings separate system-level forecasting gains from the contribution of orchestration itself.

Shortcut-Resistant CAM Distillation for Long-Tailed Recognition

Wenhai Wan, Teng Zhang, Shao-Yuan Li, Xinrui Wang, Qiang-Sheng Hua, Songcan Chen

cs.LG, Learning,, PMLR, 306:124764-124798,, 2026

Real-world datasets often follow a long-tailed distribution, making generalization to tail classes difficult. We revisit this problem through the lens of shortcut learning, where models prefer the easiest predictive cues (e.g., background or textures) over object-centric semantics, especially under scarce and biased supervision. We find that this tendency is amplified for tail classes: limited examples often share similar contexts, making non-semantic signals highly correlated and thus tempting shortcuts, whereas head classes with diverse appearances and environments encourage more stable object-focused representations. Motivated by this observation, we propose Shortcut-Resistant CAM Distillation (SRCD), a plug-and-play framework that transfers object-focused explanations from head to tail classes. SRCD operates in the Class Activation Map (CAM) space, where a CAM provides a class-specific spatial evidence map for a prediction. SRCD aggregates CAMs from a small set of head-class candidates into a shortcut-resistant teacher using an energy-model weighting based on coherence and concentration, and distills it to the tail-class CAM. We provide a theoretical analysis that quantifies shortcut reliance as shortcut-region evidence mass in CAM space and shows that SRCD suppresses tail shortcuts. Extensive experiments on long-tailed benchmarks consistently improve strong baselines. The code is available at https://github.com/Haifeng3/SRCD.

BRIDGE: An Acceptance Gate to Block False Improvements in Model Updates, with a Retrospective Clinical Evaluation

Dwi Sunaryono

cs.LG, stat.ML, https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23523985

Models are updated repeatedly, and an update that looks better on validation data but not on new data is a false improvement that can misguide clinical decisions. The naive rule accepts every positive validation gain and is optimistic when the same data choose and judge the update. BRIDGE is a model-agnostic acceptance gate that decides each update from predictions and the available labels, with fixed settings. Each fold of the data judges the update chosen on the other folds (cross-fitting) to reduce selection bias; acceptance requires the gain's lower bootstrap bound to exceed a margin. The four settings are (A) 11 new datasets, (B) synthetic tasks, (C) 12 retrospective clinical tasks, and (D) 39 supporting datasets. The false-improvement rate falls from 19.1% (naive) to 1.1% (BRIDGE) in (A), 20.7% to 1.7% (B), 20.0% to 0% (C), and 12.5% to 0.5% (D); every 95% confidence interval of the reduction over datasets or tasks excludes zero (not over the clinical sources). An equal-size random draw from the naive acceptances contains more false improvements in (A)-(E) (post hoc, p<0.001). On the OpenML collection (E) of 14 new datasets, the false-improvement rate falls from 25.6% to 1.0%. BRIDGE removes 97% to 100% of the false improvements that the naive rule accepts, and its mean gain per deployed update is 1.8 to 3.1 times the naive rule's. The 95% upper bound on the false-improvement rate is below 5.5%: formal in (B) for independent tasks (3.56%), and conditional on the real data tested (0.33% to 5.40%).

Geometric knowledge spaces, Attention pairing and Architectures

Shang Xu

cs.LG, math.AG, math.GN

In this paper, we propose a geometric approach to deriving attention mechanisms and exploring potential new transformer architectures. We begin by formalizing the notions of knowledge worlds and attention pairings, then demonstrate how this framework naturally leads to the architectures of standard attention, causal attention, linear attention, tensor attention, and recurrent neural networks. Finally, we introduce several new geometric spaces that may inspire novel neural network architectures.

Are Joint-Embedding Predictive Objectives Effective for Longitudinal EHRs?

Ashvin Gupta, Brendan Delaney, Alessandra Russo

cs.LG, cs.AI

Masked language modelling (MLM) is the dominant pretraining objective for transformer encoders applied to longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs). However, reconstructing individual events may overemphasise noisy or weakly informative observations and does not explicitly encourage learning higher-level patient states. Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) instead learn by predicting representations in latent space and have shown strong performance in a variety of domains. In this paper, we investigate to what extent JEPA can be adapted to EHR sequence representation. Our empirical investigation using MIMIC-IV shows that although all three of our JEPA formulations train stably without representational collapse, they consistently underperform standard MLM. We find that target size, not target heterogeneity, increases prediction error, yet reducing the target size does not recover downstream performance. This suggests that stable latent prediction alone is insufficient for competitive EHR representation learning and highlights the challenge of defining clinically meaningful latent targets for EHR.

Phase Transitions in Learning Driven by Extremal Data

Ethan Levien, Trevor GrandPre

cs.LG, cond-mat.stat-mech, physics.data-an

We study an extremal regression problem, an example of multiple instance learning (MIL), where the predictors are given as multisets, called bags. The bag elements (called instances) possess hidden labels which are pooled to produce an observed bag-level label. Max pooling is common in MIL applications, but gives rise to vanishing gradients. To handle this, modern MIL pipelines approximate the max with a softmax, treating the sharpness as a learnable parameter, denoted $\lambda$. When the max pooling model is the true data generating process, learning will be successful when $\lambda$ is increased during gradient descent, thereby driving the dynamics into regions of parameter space with vanishing gradients. To understand the conditions under which gradient descent breaks down, we study a minimal model and derive a low dimensional projection of the gradient flow in the joint limit of large bags $n$ and high dimension $d$ with $\alpha=\ln n/d$ fixed. The flow has two distinct phase transitions: an intrinsic freezing transition related to the random energy model of disordered systems, and a retrieval transition occurring in modern Hopfield networks. Numerical simulation confirms both the phase diagram and the convergence of the finite-size training gradient to its predicted asymptotic form.

Block Diffusion for Longitudinal EHR: Accuracy, Efficiency, and Representation Trade-offs

Ashvin Gupta, Joshua Placidi, Brendan Delaney, Alessandra Russo

cs.LG, cs.AI

Autoregressive (AR) models have proved effective for zero-shot prediction from longitudinal electronic health records, but impose strictly sequential generation on trajectories containing temporally clustered and interdependent clinical events. We investigate block-based masked diffusion (BD) as an alternative on MIMIC-IV under matched tokenisation, context length, model capacity, and evaluation conditions. Across block sizes, BD exhibits a clear quality--efficiency trade-off, with small-block configurations retaining much of the predictive performance of AR while enabling substantially faster inference. A representative $B{=}8$ configuration achieves approximately $1.43\times$ faster evaluation. Although generative rollout remains below AR in most settings, frozen linear probes on BD and AR representations achieve near-identical performance across clinical prediction tasks, indicating that BD learns comparably informative clinical representations. Increasing the denoising budget yields diminishing returns, while alternative decoding strategies provide limited gains. Overall, our results show that block diffusion is a viable and computationally attractive approach to longitudinal EHR modelling, with further improvements in iterative decoding offering a promising route toward closing the remaining generative performance gap.

Stochastic Pruning of the DPO Computation Graph: 1.4x Faster Training and Lower Peak Memory

Yubing Wu, Wei Chen, Junmei Yang, Delu Zeng, Tiewen Pan

cs.LG, cs.AI

Direct preference optimization (DPO) incurs training costs in output scoring, sequence execution, and backpropagation, so reducing supervision alone does not necessarily save computation. We introduce a unified framework for stochastic pruning of the DPO computation graph that independently samples response positions for scoring, response prefixes for execution, and preference branches for backpropagation. By making these selections before the corresponding operations, the framework omits computation at each stage of a training step. Our analysis characterizes the objective changes induced by position and sequence pruning and uses inverse-probability weighting to obtain a conditionally unbiased single-branch gradient estimator for the sampled objective. Controlled measurements show that position and sequence pruning primarily reduce peak memory, with most output-memory savings attributable to an implementation that retains full response supervision. Branch pruning yields a $1.426\times$ step-time speedup over a concatenated DPO baseline and reduces training time by about 30\% relative to stock DPO across two model families, with slightly lower mean accuracy and benchmark-dependent changes in open-ended scores. These findings relate the resource benefits of stochastic graph pruning to the computation omitted and the resulting changes in the training objective.

WRDO: Weighted Refusal Direction Optimization in Aligned Language Models

Grigoriy Evseev, Vladimir Solodkin, Dmitry Bylinkin, Daniil Medyakov, Vladimir Aletov, Aleksandr Beznosikov

cs.LG, cs.CL

Aligned large language models are trained to comply with benign requests while refusing harmful ones. However, recent work shows that this refusal behavior is fragile and can often be disrupted by simple activation- or weight-space interventions. In particular, refusal has been shown to be largely mediated by a single residual-stream direction, typically computed as a difference-of-means vector between harmful and harmless prompts. However, this standard construction aggregates heterogeneous harmful prompts into one uniform average, potentially conflating distinct refusal-related phenomena. In this work, we study whether refusal interventions can be strengthened by learning how to combine prompt-specific refusal directions. We propose Weighted Refusal Direction Optimization (WRDO), a procedure that constructs a composite refusal direction from a bank of per-prompt directions using learned aggregation weights. This approach allows different harmful prompts to contribute unequally to the final intervention direction. We evaluate WRDO against prior single-direction methods on three open-weight language models using three guard judges and HarmBench as an external evaluator. We further measure the effect of the intervention on general model capability through standard utility benchmarks. Finally, we investigate a defense-oriented setting in which the same optimization machinery is used in reverse to restore refusal behavior in an abliterated model checkpoint. Our results show that weighted aggregation provides a more effective and flexible mechanism for manipulating refusal representations.

SOAR: Social and Opinion-Aware Recommendations

Giannis Christoforidis, Apostolos N. Papadopoulos

cs.LG, cs.IR

One of the enduring challenges in online platforms is recommendation. Both communities and advertisers face difficulties in analyzing complex user behavior, identifying habits and evolving preferences, and providing timely recommendations that are relevant to user interests. In particular, point of interest recommendation poses the additional challenge of identifying the factors that influence user behavior, capturing user tendencies, and generating accurate, personalized recommendations for each individual. In this work, we propose SOAR, a Social and Opinion Aware Recommendation model that incorporates both the user's friendship network and textual reviews to assess whether a location is suitable for a specific user, with a particular emphasis on the sentiment of shared reviews. Experimental results in real-world datasets demonstrate that incorporating reviews into the recommendation process improves the precision of the recommendation.

Implicit Feature Reweighting for Label-Free Graph Generalization

Tianjun Yao, Chen Xi, Xinyang Li, Lixian Zhong

cs.LG, graph-level, OOD, generalization, without, class, labels

Graph-level out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization is critical for deploying graph neural networks in real-world applications where distribution shifts are inevitable. Existing approaches typically require class labels during representation learning to decide which substructures are predictive, limiting their applicability. We propose SCARCE, a label-free representation-learning framework that yields distribution-shift-robust graph representations from an environment partition of the training graphs alone, whether that partition comes from dataset metadata or, when none is available, from clustering a self-supervised embedding. Our method operates in two stages: (1) a variational graph autoencoder is warm-started for graph reconstruction; and (2) an adversarial confusion mechanism forces the encoder to produce representations from which a discriminator cannot distinguish environments, while preserving graph-reconstruction fidelity. We provide theoretical analysis showing that environment confusion contracts environment-discriminative directions in the representation space, which, under regularized downstream classifiers, is equivalent to imposing a stronger directional penalty on environment-associated features. This feature-reweighting mechanism reduces reliance on spurious correlations without erasing information, offering a label-free, continuous relaxation of last-layer reweighting methods. Experiments on a controlled synthetic datasets and the DrugOOD datasets show that SCARCE achieves state-of-the-art OOD generalization among label-free methods, using no class labels during representation learning.

Private Online Prediction from Experts with Small Losses

Bo Li, Peng Ye

cs.LG, stat.ML

We consider online prediction from experts, a fundamental problem in machine learning, under differential privacy constraints. Existing private algorithms achieve near-optimal regret $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{T})$, where $T$ is the time horizon. However, this bound becomes suboptimal when $L^\star$, the cumulative loss of the best expert, is significantly less than $T$. In this work, we present the first differentially private algorithm with regret $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{L^\star})$, offering a substantial improvement in the low-loss regime. Our regret bound matches the non-private lower bound up to poly-logarithmic factors for constant privacy parameters, demonstrating that privacy incurs only a small cost in this setting. Our result assumes that the input sequence is oblivious, i.e., it is fixed in advance and independent of the algorithm's outputs. We also prove that an $\tilde{\Omega}(\sqrt{T})$ regret is inevitable for adaptively generated inputs, even when $L^\star = 0$.

The Reflexivity Threshold: A Phase Transition for Multi-Agent Performative Prediction

Ahmed Mahrous, Roberto Di Pietro

cs.LG, cs.AI, Code:, https://github.com/AhmedMahrous00/reflexivity-threshold

Many learning systems are performative: once deployed, their predictions change the data distribution on which future models are trained. In multi-agent settings, this feedback is routed through a network of interacting learners, so stability depends not only on the strength of performativity but also on who affects whom. We study repeated retraining in multi-agent performative prediction and identify a spectral threshold that separates stable learning from worst-case learning obstruction. Prior work often collapses cross-agent feedback into a scalar contraction constant, missing this network structure. We instead introduce the reflexivity matrix $\Gamma$, which records how strongly one agent's deployment can move another agent's best response, defined from per-agent gradient sensitivities and curvature parameters. The threshold occurs when the spectral radius of this matrix crosses one. Below the threshold, repeated retraining converges geometrically to a unique performatively stable equilibrium, with cumulative excess loss bounded uniformly in the time horizon. Above the threshold, we construct noisy supercritical instances in which every local-information learner incurs expected cumulative excess loss growing linearly for horizons up to order $1/(\rho(\Gamma)-1)$. Together, these results characterize the threshold behavior through upper and lower bounds and recover the single-agent scalar threshold from prior work as a special case.

Fast Bayesian Optimization Through Linear Models

Colin Doumont, Henry Moss, Philipp Hennig

cs.LG, https://github.com/colmont/fast-bo

Bayesian optimization (BO) is typically used only for problems where the underlying function is deemed "expensive" (e.g. in time or money), with the speed of the BO algorithm being of little importance. In the real world, however, problems span a continuous range of values, going from cheap to expensive. In this paper, we present BO algorithms through the lens of a speed-performance Pareto frontier: we compare BO against faster algorithms like CMA-ES, and highlight how certain regions of the Pareto frontier are currently empty. As a solution, we propose a new set of fast BO methods that exploit the linear structure in existing BO algorithms, for both continuous and combinatorial inputs (from ~10 to 6000+ dimensions). Our methods achieve performance similar to state-of-the-art BO, while being more than $10^2\times$ to $10^3\times$ faster, opening the field of BO to an entirely new class of problems.

Fragment-Level Graph Encoding for Interpretable Molecular Property Prediction

Rafael Lopes Almeida (1), Ina P\"ohner (1) ((1) School of Pharmacy, University of Eastern Finland, Kuopio, Finland)

cs.LG, stat.ML, Proceedings, of, the, 8th, Northern, Lights, Deep, Learning, Conference, (NLDL),, 2027., For, associated, code,, see, https://github.com/4D-Lab/frame

Graph neural networks (GNNs) are among the standard tools for molecular property prediction, but the atom-level explanations of their predictions are hard for medicinal chemists to act on. We propose a change of input representation rather than a new model: molecules are encoded as fragment-level graphs from BRICS decomposition, where each node is a synthetically meaningful fragment whose features are the element-wise sum of its atoms' features. Because only the representation changes, any message-passing GNN and any post-hoc attribution method produces explanations at the resolution of functional groups instead of single atoms. Across four architectures, four prediction tasks, and two explainers under scaffold splits, fragment-level encoding matches atom-level test accuracy on roughly five-fold smaller graphs. Integrated-Gradients attributions recover known pharmacophores above chance on two enzyme targets, namely BACE at 1.5 times the chance rate, and PTR1 at 3.2 times. A like-for-like comparison against an aggregated atom-level baseline shows that post-hoc aggregation is not a stable substitute: its BACE hit rate range both exceeds and falls below the fragment model's hit rate depending on two arbitrary aggregation choices, whereas fragment-level training yields a single convention-free attribution at no predictive cost. The code is available at: https://github.com/4D-Lab/frame

A Transfer Learning-Driven Methodology for Efficient and Sustainable Indoor Localization Using VLC

Masood Jan, Wafa Njima, Xun Zhang, Alexander Artemenko

cs.LG, eess.SP, January, 2026

The rapid expansion of the Internet of Things enables seamless collaboration among connected devices, making indoor localization a critical component of industrial automation. However, conventional localization techniques struggle in dynamic environments. These methods depend on extensive, frequent data collection and environment-specific calibration, limiting scalability, interoperability, and effective use of prior research while imposing significant energy penalties that hinder practical deployment. To address these challenges, we propose an energy-efficient transfer learning (TL) based approach for visible light communication based indoor localization. Our method leverages TL to tackle environmental variability, including lighting fluctuations and physical obstacles that typically degrade localization performance, while simultaneously reducing computational overhead and energy consumption compared to conventional approaches. We also introduce a novel model efficiency (ME) metric, designed to integrate localization accuracy, energy efficiency, data efficiency, and transfer gain into a single evaluative measure for comprehensive optimization. Evaluations on a real-world dataset collected from a BOSCH factory demonstrate that our model achieves a 47% improvement in localization accuracy compared to a conventional model that does not utilize TL, and achieves up to 67.5% ME with TL models, compared to only 10.4% in conventional models under high-noise conditions. These results underscore the potential of our approach to deliver a highly efficient and scalable indoor localization system suitable for energy-constrained industrial applications.

Local and Global Recursive Ridge Regression for Online Residual Correction of Frozen Multivariate Forecasters under Label Delay

Takumi Fujimoto, Hiroaki Nishi

cs.LG

A frozen multi-horizon forecaster can be corrected online from its own residuals. However, under label delay the target of a block arrives only after the whole horizon has passed, and the corrector's retained state grows with the number of channels. Aiming to combine a small retained state with a large error reduction, we propose DRiC (DCT Ridge Corrector), which fits a ridge regression of the leading discrete cosine transform (DCT) coefficients of the next residual block on those of the frozen forecast and of the latest input block, with sufficient statistics updated recursively. Its local variant, DRiC-L, keeps one such regression per channel; its global variant, DRiC-G, pools all channels into one regression, whose statistics do not depend on the channel count. A guard tracks a running scale of the frozen forecast's residual for each channel; it skips updates on blocks far outside that scale and clips the issued correction. We evaluate on seven datasets (four ETT series, Weather with its missing-value codes filled, Electricity, and Traffic) with DLinear, PatchTST, and iTransformer backbones, against 10 naive, gradient-trained, and closed-form methods. DRiC-L with eight DCT coefficients (DRiC-L8) reduces the mean squared error (MSE) by 14.55% on average over 126 conditions, against 12.98% for ELF, the most accurate comparator, while retaining 15-17 times less state, although ELF is more accurate on Traffic. DRiC-G retains 14.6 kB on the 321-channel Electricity data and 18.9 kB on the 862-channel Traffic data and is the most accurate method at or below its state on both. However, 5 to 7 of the 10 comparators, all with at least 10 times its state, are more accurate there. On the unfilled Weather file, DRiC-L8 without the guard has on average 8.0 times the frozen forecast's MSE, whereas with the guard it reduces the MSE by 7.94%. Code: https://github.com/keiotakmin/dric.

Improved Training of Strong Stochastic Flow Maps

Zander W. Blasingame, Antonio Franca, Raul Minan, Christian Koke, Niklas Rindtorff, Alexander Tong

cs.LG, cs.AI, math.DS

Stochastic flow maps promise few-step sampling of stochastic differential equations (SDEs) by learning to directly jump between times along the solution path, yet training these models to learn this solution map remains difficult. The primary obstacle is the Brownian path itself: it is an infinite-dimensional object which needs to be passed to the neural network and is nowhere differentiable, which makes the implementation of derivative-based losses that power the state-of-the-art deterministic flow maps just out of reach. We identify two core design principles which address these issues: a) represent the Brownian path with a basis which carries higher-order local information, and b) train with an objective that forces the network to use the full representation of the path, not just the first order. To address this, we leverage the shifted Legendre polynomial expansion with a Wong-Zakai styled objective, unlocking derivative-based losses. Finally, we propose a recipe for fine-tuning large pre-trained flow models into strong stochastic flow maps. We ablate through this design space on several toy problems and use our findings to transform a large flow model into a strong stochastic flow map on ImageNet, achieving roughly a three-fold improvement in FD-DINOv2 over SSFM at four NFE.

Reliability-Aware Probabilistic Fusion for Multimodal Learning with Incomplete Evidence

Patitapaban Palo, Abhisek Tiwari, Mattia Carletti, Anshul Thakur, Xiao Gu, and David A. Clifton

cs.LG

Multimodal inputs may be missing or degraded, raising distinct questions of evidence availability and trust. We introduce a reliability-aware Gaussian Product-of-Experts (PoE) in which an input-dependent reliability weight tempers each modality's latent precision, while missing experts are omitted. We characterize how reliability and availability affect posterior information and expert influence. Across two clinical multimodal tasks, the full framework remains competitive with complete inputs and achieves its clearest mean-AUROC advantage under modality absence, particularly on HAIM-MIMIC-MM. Ablations show a substantial gain from missing-aware training and an additional HAIM improvement from reliability weighting under matched PoE, missing-aware, and consistency training. Unseen test-time corruptions are used diagnostically and reveal modality-dependent reliability-weight and latent-uncertainty responses, including failure under severe degradation. Thus, reliability weighting provides an explicit evidence-control mechanism, while learning dependable quality signals remains an open challenge.

Cross-Area Transfer Learning for VLC-based Indoor Localization with a Transfer Efficiency Score

Masood Jan, Wafa Njima, Xun Zhang, Alexander Artemenko

cs.LG, eess.SP, 1, August, 2026

Indoor localization systems are critical for Industry-4.0 applications. Visible Light Communication (VLC)-based systems offer advantages such as immunity to electromagnetic interference, high accuracy, and energy efficiency, but their performance is hindered by environmental variability across deployment areas. This paper presents a transfer learning (TL) framework for VLC-based indoor localization that enhances cross-area robustness while reducing data requirements. Using real-world measurements from four distinct areas of a Bosch manufacturing facility, we show that fine-tuning a deep neural network (DNN) pre-trained on source area data with only 40% of target-area measurements recovers 97% of full-data performance, thereby reducing the amount of target data needed. We further introduce the Transfer Efficiency Score (TES), a composite metric that identifies the most effective source models for transfer without exhaustive evaluation. Guided by TES, the framework selects the best source model, yielding localization errors as low as 40.9 cm with success rates above 86%. Validation on an unseen area confirms its generalization capability, with a 3.28 cm mean error achieved using the TES-selected source model. Overall, the proposed framework provides a scalable and data-efficient pathway for industrial VLC localization, substantially reducing deployment costs and computational burden while maintaining high accuracy in diverse and dynamic environments.

Probabilistic Machine Learning for Short-Term Atmospheric Seeing Forecasting

Mary-Joe Medlej, Rahul Srinivasan, Simon Prunet, Aziz Ziad, Christophe Giordano

cs.LG, astro-ph.IM, Telescopes, +, Instrumentation,, Copehagen,, July, 2026

Accurate short-term forecasting of atmospheric seeing is of significant interest for astronomical observations and free-space optical communication systems, where rapid variations in optical turbulence can affect system performance and operational planning. In this work, we benchmark machine learning models for predicting atmospheric seeing up to two hours in advance, with a particular focus on probabilistic forecasting and uncertainty quantification. We compare two probabilistic approaches, Gaussian Processes (GPs) and a normalizing-flow- based model (FloTS), against deterministic recurrent neural network (RNN) and long short-term memory (LSTM) baselines. All models are trained and evaluated on seeing measurements from the Maunakea summit. We assess the models using both deterministic metrics, including the root mean square error and the Pearson correlation coefficient, and probabilistic metrics that evaluate the quality and calibration of predictive uncertainty estimates. The results show that deep learning approaches outperform standard recurrent networks for short-term forecasting, with LSTM and FloTS achieving the best overall predictive accuracy. While Gaussian Processes provide a useful probabilistic baseline, their predictive distributions are limited by the Gaussian assumption. FloTS combines competitive forecasting accuracy with flexible uncertainty quantification and produces the most informative probabilistic forecasts.

SPARE: Sparsity Allocation via Routing Entropy for Efficient MoEs

Yifan Yang, Hanling Yi, Dongquan Yang, Feng Lin, Rong Xiao, Aijun Li, Xiaotian Yu

cs.LG, cs.CL

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures scale model capacity efficiently, yet their substantial memory footprint poses a major barrier to deployment. Expert pruning reduces this footprint, but most methods focus on which experts to remove and prune every layer equally. We propose SPARE (Sparsity Allocation via Routing Entropy), a training-free, selector-agnostic method that decides how many experts to remove from each layer using routing entropy, the entropy of tokens' normalized Top-K gate weights on calibration data. When a token's gate weight is spread evenly, removing one active expert discards little of it, so SPARE prunes layers with more diffuse routing more aggressively. A perturbation bound under random pruning formalizes this intuition, and controlled interventions with a fixed selector link concentrated routing to larger output changes. With the same selector and budget, SPARE improves average accuracy over uniform allocation on Mixtral, Qwen3, and DeepSeek models, especially at high pruning ratios. At 50% expert pruning, it raises the eight-task average of Qwen3-30B-A3B from 57.2% to 63.8%, and the math, code, and science average of DeepSeek-R1 from 80.1 to 82.8.

Leveraging Time-Series Foundation Models for Financial Crime Detection

Danae Natalie Savvidi, H. \c{C}a\u{g}r{\i} Bilgi, Kubilay Atasu, Arie van Deursen

cs.LG, Conference, on, AI, in, Finance, (ICAIF, '26),, Milan,, Italy

Financial crime detection requires identifying illicit activity within large transaction networks, where suspicious behaviour is rare and unfolds over time. Existing graph-based methods capture relationships among accounts but typically underuse the temporal behaviour encoded in account transaction histories. We introduce Temporal-Context Edge Enrichment (TCEE), a framework that addresses this gap by summarising each account's transaction history with a frozen time-series foundation model (TSFM), and enriching each transaction's representation with the relevant temporal context from these summaries. These enriched representations can be scored directly for edge classification, or fed into a graph neural network (GNN) as edge features to capture broader structural information. Across six AMLWorld datasets, TCEE improves PR-AUC over the strongest evaluated baseline on five by a relative 10.8% on average, while training 6.0x faster end-to-end. Feeding TCEE's representations into a GNN raises PR-AUC a further 4.8% on the four smaller datasets. On an Ethereum phishing dataset, we show that TSFM-generated account representations fed into a GNN are also effective for node classification, improving PR-AUC by 14.5% over the strongest baseline. As TSFMs continue to advance, our results suggest they can strengthen financial crime detection by capturing temporal signals that complement graph-based approaches.

Characterizing Oblivious Common-Reward Multi-Player Bandits: Universal Stationarity, Coordination Traps, and Certified Global Rates

Mingxiao Li, Wenhai Lai, Hongyu Wang

cs.LG

This paper presents a theoretical analysis of the oblivious common-reward multi-player multi-armed bandit (OC-MPMAB), a minimal multi-player bandit model in which multiple players select arms simultaneously and observe only a shared reward. Crucially, the players are fully oblivious: they do not observe the actions of other players and do not even know whether any other players exist. The contributions of this work are threefold. First, we prove that the independent gradient-bandit updates applied by the players jointly form an unbiased stochastic gradient estimator of a hidden product-policy objective. This property then yields a universal finite-time first-order stationarity guarantee for every instance. Second, we show that stationarity cannot, in general, be strengthened to global optimality. To illustrate this, we identify a product-policy trap obstruction that rules out the guarantee of global optimality. Third, we show that global optimality can be recovered under structural assumptions on the reward table. To make global optimality achievable, we introduce finite-table certificates that separately characterize product-gradient dominance and self-bounding stochastic noise. We then establish certificate-conditioned bounds on the product-policy gap and pseudo-regret. Consequently, the standard $O(1/t)$ product-policy gap and $O(\log T)$ pseudo-regret rates hold when the certificates are uniform.

DriveMLP: When Training Strategy Outperforms Model Complexity in Autonomous Driving

Alessandro Canevaro, Julian Schmidt, Hang Yu, Ye Jin, Sajad Marvi, Georg Martius, Julian Jordan

cs.LG, cs.RO

Learning motion planning policies that remain reliable under closed-loop execution is a central challenge in Autonomous Driving (AD). Imitation Learning (IL) scales well to large AD datasets but suffers from distribution shift, causal confusion, and compounding errors under closed-loop execution. In contrast, Reinforcement Learning (RL) typically requires handcrafted rule-based rewards and massive simulation. We study a simple closed-loop IL framework that uses RL purely as an optimization tool, guided by a trajectory-distance reward derived from logged expert data. We refer to this training strategy as Expert-Guided RL (EGRL). Through a controlled empirical study we find evidence that the training procedure, rather than model size and complexity, accounts for a substantial part of the performance gains. We then introduce DriveMLP, a lightweight MLP policy trained with EGRL, as a simple instantiation of these findings. It achieves a score of 86.51 on the nuPlan Val14 Reactive benchmark, a +3.2 point improvement over the previous best IL planner (Flow Planner), with >15x faster inference. Code is available at: https://github.com/alessandro-canevaro/DriveMLP .

Structure-Aware Message Passing on Petri Nets through Directed Walks and Incidence Diffusion

Geri Skenderi

cs.LG, cs.AI

A common application of the process mining discipline is to connect observed event data with Petri nets, which are bipartite, directed graphical process models with rich formal underpinnings. Recent work learns vector representations of Petri nets to retrieve process models and relate them to event logs, but leaves implicit two key aspects hidden within the net's structure: directed cycles (process loops) and invariants recorded by the net's incidence matrix. We expose both of these to message-passing graph neural networks, through a positional encoding built from return probabilities of directed place-transition walks (PNRWPE), and through same-type messages gated by diffusion kernels of the incidence matrix (DA-PNGNN). Our constructions raise macro balanced accuracy in predicting the generation factors of the 96 Process Discovery Contest 2023 models from 0.751 to 0.796 with PNRWPE, which nearly doubles accuracy on process loops, and to 0.830 with DA-PNGNN, which, without retuning, also improves net-to-tree retrieval top-1 accuracy on the self-supervised ProcRosetta benchmark by 4.1 to 5.9 points.

Monitorability Is Trainable: Post-Training for Chain-of-Thought Monitorability

Usman Anwar, Tim Bakker, Christos Louizos, David Krueger

cs.LG

Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is one of the few practical tools available for overseeing capable AI systems, but it is fragile: a model's reasoning is only useful to a monitor if it remains coupled to what the model actually does. This coupling can be degraded during training through, for example, CoT compression, optimization against a monitor, and out-of-context generalization from synthetic documents. In this work, we propose an unsupervised post-training objective based on maximizing mutual information between CoT and the output for improving CoT monitorability. Across three model organisms with degraded CoT, it recovers most or all of the lost monitorability. Applied to undegraded policies, it further improves judged monitorability, improves prediction of unsafe behavior by CoT monitors and activation probes, and increases the prevalence of consequential reasoning. These results suggest that monitorability should be treated as a trainable attribute of the policy. A dedicated monitorability post-training stage can extend current practices that protect CoT from direct optimization pressure.

Asymmetric Likelihood-Free Hypothesis Testing
