Gunjan Kumar, Yash Pote, Jonathan Scarlett

cs.LG, cs.DS, cs.IT, math.IT, math.ST, stat.ML, stat.TH

In likelihood-free hypothesis testing (LFHT), given samples from two unknown distributions $P,Q$ on $[n]$ with $\mathrm{d_{TV}}(P,Q)\geq\varepsilon$, and from a third distribution $D\in\{P,Q\}$, the goal is to identify whether $D=P$ or $D=Q$. We study asymmetric likelihood-free hypothesis testing, where the sample budgets $s_P$ and $s_Q$ for $P$ and $Q$ may differ, and characterize the feasible triples $(s_P,s_Q,s_D)$ up to logarithmic factors. We establish that unequal reference budgets can give a polynomial saving. For instance, for constant separation, $n$ samples from $P$ and $n^{3/4}$ from $Q$ allow testing with $\widetilde O(n^{1/4})$ samples from $D$, whereas both existing baselines---symmetric LFHT and unequal-sample closeness testing---require $\Omega(\sqrt n)$ samples from $D$. We further apply our results to non-adaptive distribution 2-clustering, where the sample allocation among $k$ distributions must be fixed before observing any data. We obtain matching upper and lower bounds for this problem up to logarithmic factors in terms of the domain size, number of distributions, smaller cluster size, and separation, both with one cluster distribution known and with both unknown. Comparing with known bounds for adaptive sample allocation gives a worst-case adaptivity gap of $\widetilde\Theta(k^{1/4})$.

A Sharp Dynamic Haussler Lemma

Shihao Shao, Dacheng Tao

cs.LG, cs.DS

Haussler's covering lemma (JCTA 1995) gives the sharp $(C/\delta)^d$ metric-entropy bound for binary VC classes under normalized $\ell_1$ distance. We prove a sharp global dynamic analogue for bounded real-valued classes in normalized $\ell_p$, for every $1\le p<\infty$. Writing $d=\operatorname{sfat}_{\varepsilon/32}(\mathcal F)$, we obtain a global dynamic cover of size at most $(C/\varepsilon^p)^d$ for every horizon and $0<\varepsilon<1/2$. Here a single family of deterministic causal experts, fixed independently of the instance sequence, covers every target on every sequence. For $0<\varepsilon<1/4$, a matching worst-case lower bound $(c/\varepsilon)^{pd}$ holds already for binary classes on one fixed sequence, even with real-valued experts. The constants are universal, independent of $p$. For $p=2$, this resolves the horizon-independent sequential $\ell_2$ covering problem of Rakhlin, Sridharan, and Tewari (PTRF 2015) in a stronger global form. For $p=1$, it gives the sharp $(C/\delta)^{\operatorname{Ldim}(\mathcal H)}$ global dynamic covering bound for binary classes, resolving questions of Alon, Ben-Eliezer, Dagan, Moran, Naor, and Yogev (STOC 2021) and Malliaris and Moran (Trans. AMS 2024). Our proof combines rank-decreasing correction schedules and accurate sampled prefixes with exact continuations after target identification. Shared-parent routing distributes these continuations among eligible prefixes, eliminating the logarithmic overhead.

Preventing Model Extraction in Contextual Linear Optimization

Adam N. Elmachtoub, Tianyi Lin, Wenxuan Liu

cs.LG, math.OC

Consider a contextual linear optimization problem, where a predict-then-optimize (PTO) system returns optimal decisions without revealing the cost vectors used in its downstream linear program. Our focus is on how to prevent attackers, who can submit context queries, from extracting the underlying prediction model. Although the cost-to-decision map is many-to-one, observations across contexts can reveal the underlying prediction model. For linear models, we provide sufficient conditions where the attacker can learn the model up to positive scaling by only observing the decision. These conditions motivate an adaptive attack that alternates inverse optimization with boundary sampling, i.e., using context queries leading to decision boundaries of the current attacker model. To prevent extraction, we propose a mechanism that perturbs decisions only when the predicted cost vector is near a decision boundary. We establish a regret bound in terms of the perturbation radius. For the attackers that fit the defended responses as optimal decisions under the evaluation distribution, we also prove a lower bound on expected extraction error. Experiments on synthetic instances and real data quantify the security-performance trade-off of the defense mechanism and illustrate that our defense can increase the extraction error.

Privacy in Context Is Being Overlooked in the Age of General-Purpose LLMs

Prakhar Ganesh, Yash More, Marco Romanelli, Ferdinando Fioretto, Golnoosh Farnadi

cs.LG, cs.AI, Workshop, @, AAAI, 2026

As large language models (LLMs) continue to grow in scope and capability, privacy concerns are on the rise. Since users increasingly interact with a single general-purpose system across many distinct contexts, there is a natural tendency to pursue a one-size-fits-all solution to privacy. Unfortunately, this overlooks a crucial insight from decades of privacy research: privacy is inherently contextual. In this paper, we argue that existing work in prompt privacy is drifting towards attempts to design a unified framework, which is highly misleading and can foster a false sense of security. Furthermore, we argue that even future work in this direction needs to wrestle with fundamental tensions across various desirable properties of prompt privacy. Thus, such continued attempts towards a unified framework need to be reconsidered. We recommend three critical paths forward: application-specific deployment of LLMs, context-specific evaluation of privacy frameworks, and better privacy literacy.

Your Benchmark Is an Empirical Measure Over Difficulty

Yifan Sun, Naicheng Yu, Jiawen Gong, Jingyan Shen, Huan Zhang

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL

A benchmark is not merely a collection of test questions; it naturally induces an empirical measure over difficulty. In this work, we develop this measure-based view of benchmark evaluation. This perspective allows us to identify and formalize two central failure modes: measure mismatch, where the benchmark difficulty distribution differs from the intended difficulty distribution, and coverage gaps, where uncovered regions along the difficulty axis prevent benchmarks from detecting meaningful localized training progress. We propose two diagnostic metrics that capture these two failure modes and provide rigorous theoretical guarantees. Empirically, we analyze 25 popular benchmarks spanning 7 domains using carefully estimated item difficulties. Our results show that many benchmarks implicitly overweight certain difficulty regions, so their leaderboard conclusions should be interpreted with caution; others leave substantial portions of the difficulty axis uncovered, limiting their ability to monitor the model improvement process. Finally, we turn diagnosis into action by introducing BenchFill, a simple difficulty-targeted item rewriting algorithm that effectively reduces local coverage gaps.

FrontierCS 2: A Benchmark for Computer Science R&D

Qiuyang Mang, Wenhao Chai, Runyuan He, Dongruixuan Li, Zihan Zheng, Yujun He, Xuanyi Zhou, Hao Wang, Siddhant Ray, Bo Peng, Ruyi Ji, Shuo Yang, Haocheng Xi, Boru Chen, Xinyang David Han, Melissa Pan, Mayank Mishra, Boxi Yu, Sijia Liu, Kerem Akillioglu, Yi Pan, Zirui Wang, Mert Cemri, Diogo Antunes, Yichuan Wang, Aiwei Ivy Zhang, Zhenyang Chen, Qizheng Zhang, Kaiyuan Liu, Wenyuan Huang, Sherry Ruan, Shu Liu, Dacheng Li, Zerui Cheng, Zeyu Shen, Shang Zhou, Hanfei Chen, Yuzhong Zhang, Bohan Lyu, Qu Ao, Han Zheng, Chonghe Jiang, Jingzhuo Hu, Xiaokun Chen, Kunjun Li, Zhiqing Zhong, Haoye Tian, Tong Zhao, Yunzhong He, Hanchen Li, Ofir Press, Jiantao Jiao, Kunle Olukotun, Karthik R. Narasimhan, Jingbo Shang, Koushik Sen, Matei Zaharia, Alex Dimakis, Ion Stoica, Joseph E. Gonzalez, Alvin Cheung

cs.LG, https://github.com/FrontierCS/FrontierCS-2

Can LLM agents make contributions to frontier computer science R&D? We introduce FrontierCS 2, a benchmark of 48 open-ended tasks. We take a paper and the code its authors released and delete the paper's contribution. The deleted code becomes the human reference that sets the bar. Earlier open-ended benchmarks give each agent a problem statement and grade one program after the run. FrontierCS 2 places the agent inside a research environment. The agent inherits a working repository and may submit a patch at any point. For each patch, a judge returns a development-suite score but keeps the evaluator, the held-out final suite and the reference private, and grades the final submission on that suite against the reference. The tasks span 13 domains, from machine-learning systems and high-performance computing to databases, security and robotics. Beyond these 48 tasks, FrontierCS 2 keeps a private set of more than 100 tasks, to be announced in the future. We evaluate 12 agents. The best one is Claude Opus 5.5, passing 58% of its runs. No run passes on 16 tasks. A higher score is not the same as a pass: most runs that beat the reference on the combined final score still fall short on some metric or workload. On a shared capability scale for agents and tasks, the median task is roughly as difficult as what the strongest agent can handle.

CORD: Conflict-Aware Optimization for Joint RL and Distillation in Agents

Wenhao Yu, Zhengcheng Wang, Jiahong Liu, Zexuan Qiu, Minda Hu, Shaohang Wei, Jiafei Lyu, Junyan Lyu, Haobo Fu, Irwin King

cs.LG

Joint reinforcement learning and self-distillation combines task rewards with dense teacher guidance for agent training, but their gradients can partly cancel. A mixing coefficient controls relative scale without selectively removing opposing components. We present CORD, a general framework for conflict-aware optimization of two objectives. CORD separates the choice of protected objective from the condition that triggers projection, and combines the gradients once per optimizer mini-batch. A nearest-point formulation yields a closed-form update and characterizes its first-order effect on the protected objective before optimizer transformations. Gradient diagnostics connect conflict in agent training to lost local optimization progress and motivate selective correction. As a gradient-combination mechanism, CORD can be added to different joint training recipes while retaining their objectives. Across three such recipes, it improves ALFWorld success by 1.5-15.9 percentage points. On WebShop, it improves two of the three pairs, with SDAR rising from 60.7% to 64.6% success. Our analyses explain why protection direction and projection gate must be chosen together, and why the distillation objective and its mixing weight remain consequential after projection.

SCDM: Scalable Causal Discovery in Nonlinear Temporal Systems with Meta-Learning

Jingbo Wang, Kegeng Tang, Zihao Wang, Shaogang Ren

cs.LG

Causal discovery from nonlinear multivariate time series is challenging in high-dimensional systems, where the number of candidate directed relations grows quadratically with the number of variables. Existing methods are often limited by target-wise model fitting, repeated conditional testing, or expensive graph-search procedures. We propose SCDM, a shared-parameter meta-learning framework for scalable temporal causal discovery. SCDM treats each target variable as a task while learning a shared temporal predictor and a shared causal-strength matrix across tasks. Each meta-episode updates only a subset of target-variable tasks, allowing the model to accumulate structural evidence across the full system without exhaustive target-wise optimization. After training, a post-training graph readout converts the learned predictor into continuous directed causal scores for threshold-independent evaluation. We also provide a finite-error recovery principle showing that, under fully observed delayed-system assumptions and a positive risk-gap condition, thresholding SCDM scores recovers the graph when statistical, meta-optimization, and readout errors are below the population margin. Experiments on controlled synthetic, neuroimaging-inspired, high-dimensional, and real-world temporal benchmarks demonstrate competitive causal recovery and improved scalability in large temporal systems.

EPIC: Rethinking Trust Regions for Autoregressive LLM Policies via Exact-KL Prefix Importance-Ratio Clipping

Chengxi Li, Hanlin Wu, Zhicheng Cai, Huan-ang Gao, Wei-Ying Ma, Jingjing Liu, Hao Zhou

cs.LG

Reinforcement learning (RL) based on PPO-style clipping has become an effective approach to improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, standard token-level clipping measures policy change only through the conditional probability of the current token, while the prefix conditioning context is itself generated by the policy. This mismatch becomes especially consequential in long-response and off-policy training. We reformulate autoregressive generation as a finite-horizon Markov decision process and derive its performance-difference identity under behavior-policy sampling. The resulting change of measure identifies the cumulative likelihood ratio of the generated prefix as the exact importance weight for each stepwise policy-improvement term. Starting from the monotonic-improvement principle of trust-region policy optimization, we then construct a prefix-level trust region using the exact reverse-KL coordinate $r-1-\log r$, whose behavior-policy expectation equals the corresponding prefix-distribution KL divergence. This yields EPIC, a PPO-style sequence-clipping method that directly regulates prefix-level policy drift without relying on a local quadratic approximation or expert-routing replay. Experiments on off-policy reinforcement learning with a sparse mixture-of-experts language model show that EPIC consistently outperforms standard and sequence-level clipping baselines across mathematical-reasoning benchmarks, while remaining competitive with routing-replay-based alternatives. Code is available at https://github.com/LiChengxi666/EPIC.

Mitigating Model Collapse through Dreaming Learning

Matteo Benati, Alessandro Londei, Denise Lanzieri, Raffaello Mastromarino, Vittorio Loreto

cs.LG, cond-mat.dis-nn, stat.ML, Generative, Modeling, (PriGM),, NeurIPS, 2026

Training generative models on synthetic data causes loss of diversity and a decrease in performance, a phenomenon known as model collapse. We study this degeneration spectrally in a controlled Markov setting. The eigenvalue spectrum of the learned transition operator offers a direct view of the process, since the subdominant eigenvalues set how fast the chain mixes and therefore how much of the state space it continues to explore. We find that sampling at temperature $T_s < 1$ acts on the operator like repeated sharpening, pushing it toward a deterministic system: the subdominant eigenvalues approach the unit circle and the spectral gap narrows. When $T_s < 1$, even data accumulation does not appear to arrest this drift. We then analyze Dreaming Learning, a regulariser that, in parallel with regular training, adds a loss term on the model's own samples drawn at a raised temperature $T_d > 1$. Because high-temperature samples flatten the predicted distribution, training on them keeps the entries of the learned operator from becoming vanishingly small, which in turn keeps the eigenvalues bounded away from the unit circle. Measurements of the spectra over 5000 generations are consistent with this picture, and the mitigation effect persists in more complex settings such as LSTM language models and a 124M-parameter GPT-2.

Agent Behavior Maps: Locating Agents by What They Would Do, Without Running Them

Shota Fujikawa, Takuya Tamura, Kosuke Akimoto, Daichi Haraguchi, Masafumi Oyamada

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL

LLM agents with similar success rates can follow different action sequences. An Agent Behavior Map represents LLM-harness combinations using centered action log-likelihoods on shared reference trajectories, without requiring new environment rollouts from the target agent. We derive error bounds relating squared coordinate distances to trajectory KL under explicit assumptions, and across eight cross-scored models the distances track the directly measured trajectory KL in rank order. Across 100 LLMs and four environments, ALFWorld-based coordinates support performance prediction and identify behavioral similarities. In ALFWorld, these similarities persist after controlling for success rates and model size, including across model families. As an extension, harness-component displacements approximately compose, with accuracy varying across components and environments.

ExGrad: Beyond Suppression - Explicit Clean-Gradient Subtraction in Adversarial Training

Zhiyuan Ye, Xiangyu Zhou, Ji Qi, Hao Zhang, and Yi Zhou

cs.LG

Adversarial training derives updates from surrogate losses, but source-loss descent does not directly characterize an update's effect on adversarial margins. We introduce ExGrad (extrapolative gradient recomposition), which constructs clean-input, adversarial-input, and adversarial-weight-perturbed (AWP) gradients. Virtual steps measure their margin responses, and a globally solvable three-variable problem selects coefficients over a bounded affine domain without nonnegativity constraints. Across affine radii on CIFAR-10, negative clean coefficients rapidly predominate and increase in magnitude with the radius. Cross-zero interventions show that setting the clean coefficient to zero is insufficient: mild subtraction improves PGD-20, while stronger subtraction trades clean accuracy for further PGD-20 gains. In CIFAR-10 WRN-28-4 controls, none of the three candidate-removal variants matches full ExGrad. Projection diagnostics show that negative clean coefficients usually coexist with positive net clean-axis projections, although both decrease as the radius grows. Norm-matched controls indicate that update magnitude alone does not explain the moderate-radius gains. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet-100 show radius-dependent improvements over AWP. On CIFAR-100, ExGrad at $R=0.8$ improves all five reported metrics, including clean and AutoAttack accuracy by 3.84 and 1.75 percentage points. On ImageNet-100, the same radius improves all four robust metrics over both AWP and PGD-AT. These results identify clean-gradient subtraction as a response-revealed update preference whose strength and clean-robust operating point are controlled by the affine radius.

Loopholing All the Way: Language Modeling with pure Self-Conditioning

Adrien Deschenaux, Nadezhda Ilieva, Justin Deschenaux, Manuel Madeira, Dorina Thanou, Aur\'elien Lucchi, Antonio Orvieto, Caglar Gulcehre, Pascal Frossard

cs.LG

Recent Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) use Self-Conditioning (SC), or Loopholing, to improve their performance. While SC is traditionally seen as an auxiliary heuristic, its empirical success raises the question: can a continuous generative model be based purely on Self-Conditioning? We answer affirmatively with LAW (``Loopholing All the Way''), which propagates the Expected Embedding under the denoiser's prediction across steps, decoding the tokens once, at the end. On Sudoku, LAW outperforms previous Discrete and Continuous Diffusion Models, including Loopholing Discrete Diffusion. On Maze, LAW is the most accurate from 2 to 64 NFE. After training on TinyGSM and sampling at temperature 1 with 1024 NFE, LAW solves 42.2% of GSM8K problems against 34.5% for uniform Loopholing Discrete Diffusion. Additionally, fine-tuning a pre-trained Uniform Diffusion Model (Duo) with LAW increases its accuracy from 17.2% to 48.1%. Finally, on OpenWebText, LAW pushes the Pareto frontier and approaches Autoregressive Models (ARMs) with 64 NFE. Find our code at https://github.com/ilievanadezhda/law.

From Classifier Heads to Encoder Prototypes: Personalized Federated Learning with Head-Derived Clustering

Amirardalan Dehghanpour, Arian Noori, Ehsan Tanghatari, Naimeh Omidvar, Hamid R. Rabiee

cs.LG

Personalized federated learning must decide which clients should share representations. We introduce HDC-FL (Head-Derived Clustering for Personalized Federated Learning), which reads that decision off a signal shared-encoder training already produces: the client-specific classifi- er heads. At periodic refresh rounds the server projects the cached heads into a normalized low-rank descriptor space, groups the descriptors, builds one encoder prototype per group from the cached client encoders, and returns to each client a soft mixture of those prototypes paired with its own unchanged head. Client-specific heads are never averaged or distributed to oth- er clients; the shared-encoder trajectory is left intact; and no second optimization trajectory, additional local objective, or post-training fine-tuning stage is required. Across 11 CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 configurations covering label-subset, Dirichlet and rotation heterogeneity with ResNet-18 and MobileNetV2 encoders, HDC-FL is competitive with the strongest personalized and clustered baselines in macro-averaged mean accuracy and attains the numerically highest macro-averaged worst-client and bottom-20% accuracy among the evaluated methods. The tail results distinguish methods that look similar when only their means are compared. A controlled donor-swap diagnostic further finds positive donor-ranking associations between head similarity and encoder compatibility in all three settings tested, most clearly under covariate shift.

Circuits of LLM Classifiers

Hadi Khalaf, Sajani Vithana, Kunal Talreja, Haewon Jeong, Viveck R. Cadambe, Flavio Calmon

cs.LG

Language models make it easy to build classifiers by simply prompting a model with the sample to classify. However, their predictions are neither deterministic nor uniformly reliable across samples. How can we combine predictions from different models to improve reliability? We view LLM classifiers as noisy measurement instruments of an underlying target label. Our goal is to maximize average classification accuracy at a given average query cost. To this end, we introduce circuits that organize LLM classifiers into an adaptive sequence of queries and decisions. We then characterize the optimal circuit, obtain the fundamental limits of achievable accuracy, and provide practical insights on realizing the optimal circuit. Across seven classification tasks spanning safety, health, political science, human preference, agent evaluation, decision-making and reasoning, our method improves the cost-accuracy frontier over routing and cascading baselines.

Orbitol: Crystal Generation on Wyckoff Orbits

Ivan \v{Z}ugec, Joshua Rosenthal, Ivor Lon\v{c}ari\'c, Jan-Willem van de Meent, Tin Had\v{z}i Veljkovi\'c

cs.LG

Generative models propose crystalline materials by learning composition and geometry, but unconditional sampling gives no direct control over the space group, which constrains whether responses such as piezoelectricity are symmetry-allowed. We present Orbitol, a crystal generator built on the Crystalite diffusion Transformer that replaces atom tokens with Wyckoff-orbit tokens. Given a space group and a collection of occupied Wyckoff positions, Orbitol generates the atom type and free coordinates of each orbit together with a compatible lattice, so every valid structure is invariant under the operations of the requested group. It generates structures two to three orders of magnitude faster than the symmetry-aware baselines at the evaluated settings. On MP-20, 13.8% of Orbitol's attempts yielded stable or metastable, unique and novel structures that retained the requested space group after relaxation, compared with 10.8% for SGFM, the next best model. We also introduce SPARC, a score that combines retention of the raw detected space group with agreement between the retained-group and training distributions. Finally, we used Orbitol to generate candidates in piezoelectric crystal classes. Of the structures selected by internal screening, 89% passed our density functional theory checks, and 79% also matched no Materials Project or Alexandria entry with the same space group. Their median figure of merit was nine times that of the piezoelectric materials computed in the Materials Project, and 24% of the new candidates ranked in that set's top 10%.

Differentially Private Sparse Reward Estimation with Preference Feedback

Meng Ding, Mingxi Lei, Jie Zhang, Jinyan Liu, Di Wang

cs.LG, cs.AI

Reinforcement Learning with Human Feedback (RLHF) has become a central paradigm for aligning Large Language Models with human values. However, the reliance on sensitive preference data necessitates rigorous differential privacy (DP) guarantees to prevent user information leakage. Existing private alignment methods typically assume dense reward parameterizations, resulting in estimation error bounds that scale polynomially with the ambient {model size $d$}. Such dependence is prohibitive in modern {overparameterized models} where $d$ is vast. In this work, we study \emph{Private Sparse RLHF} problem to address this limitation, assuming that human preferences (reward model) are governed by a subset of $s^* \ll d$ relevant features. We provide the first comprehensive theoretical framework for differentially private sparse reward estimation from pairwise preference feedback under the Bradley-Terry-Luce model, and introduce efficient algorithms for both Sample-DP and Label-DP settings. Theoretically, under the squared $\ell_2$ parameter estimation error, we establish upper bounds of $\widetilde{O}(s^*/n + {s^*}^2/n^2\varepsilon^2)$ for $(\varepsilon,\delta)$-Sample-DP and $\widetilde{O}(s^*/n + s^*/n\varepsilon^2)$ for $\varepsilon$-Label-DP based on randomized response, where $n$ is the training data size. For the lower bounds, we show that the Sample-DP rate is nearly optimal, and that the guarantee for our Label-DP method cannot be further improved within the randomized-response framework. We also provide a general lower bound for the problem with Label-DP. Empirically, our framework demonstrates superior efficacy over dense baselines across sentiment generation and safety alignment benchmarks, offering a significantly more favorable privacy-utility trade-off.

BatchBench: Benchmarking AI Robustness to Batch Effects and Domain Shift in Biomedical Images - A JUMP Cell Painting Leaderboard

Sonja Topf, Ana Sanchez-Fernandez, G\"unter Klambauer

cs.LG, cs.AI

Despite recent advances, current AI systems remain highly susceptible to domain shifts, particularly in biomedical imaging, where images are usually acquired under substantially different experimental conditions. Although recent domain adaptation methods have made substantial progress, significant performance gaps under domain shift persist. To measure progress in this regard, we introduce BatchBench, a leaderboard that evaluates and compares the robustness of AI systems on biomedical images from unseen experimental batches and data sources. BatchBench is built on JUMP-CP, one of the largest publicly available biomedical imaging datasets. Generated using the Cell Painting assay, JUMP-CP exhibits pronounced batch effects and domain shifts, making it a challenging testbed for evaluating robustness to distributional changes.

Velocity Ambiguity Profiles: Time-Resolved Bayes-Risk Diagnostics for Flow Matching

Yunpeng Mei, Xiaowen Zhu, Chenyu Wang, Chenbo Xin, Hongjie Cao, Jiamin Wang, Jie Chen, Gang Wang

cs.LG

Flow matching trains a continuum of velocity regression problems but usually reports a loss averaged over interpolation time, obscuring which regions are limited by intrinsic ambiguity and which remain data-limited. We introduce the Velocity Ambiguity Profile (VAP), $\mathcal{A}_v(t)$, the time-local Bayes-risk floor of velocity prediction, and decompose time-resolved loss as $L_N(t)=\mathcal{A}_v(t)+R(t;N)$, where only the excess term $R(t;N)$ is reducible by more data or a different estimator. For $N$ training samples and a $K$-component mixture in the structured example, we prove two statistical benchmarks. In an intrinsic local-regression benchmark, $\mathcal{A}_v(t)$ enters the time-dependent noise factor of the standard nonparametric rate. In separated isotropic Gaussian mixtures, the Bayes velocity becomes locally affine, and a label-oracle residual-regression benchmark has parametric excess-risk scale $K\mathcal{A}_v(t)/N$, matched within the same separated residual Gaussian-location subproblem. Controlled Gaussian-mixture experiments validate this diagnostic through a dense transition sweep, calibrated oracle-to-blind and empirical-proxy audits, and a local controlled time-sampler stress test in which a frozen reducible-error proxy reduces reducible loss while cross-cell sensitivity delimits aggressive sampling. On CIFAR-100 DINOv2 feature flow, guided time sampling reduces uniformly evaluated velocity loss by $6.78\%$ and global feature Fr\'echet distance (FD) by $23.6\%$, while class-conditional kernel inception distance (KID) shows no improvement. VAP turns an averaged flow-matching objective into a time-resolved diagnostic for locating ambiguity-limited and data-limited regions.

The role of locality and weight sharing in convolutional neural networks

Francesco Mori, Melanie Weber

cs.LG, cond-mat.dis-nn, cond-mat.stat-mech, stat.ML

The success of convolutional neural networks is often attributed to locality and weight sharing. However, how these two properties separately shape learning, and how their effects depend on the spatial correlations of natural images, remain poorly understood. We address these questions in a teacher-student model with a convolutional teacher acting on $P$ input positions with correlation $c$ between positions. We compare convolutional and locally connected students trained by online stochastic gradient descent, and derive ordinary differential equations that describe their dynamics exactly in the limit of large feature dimension $D$. For uncorrelated positions ($c = 0$), the two students follow the same learning trajectory, with the convolutional student learning exactly $P$ times faster. We also find that locality affects the sample complexity: a student that matches the kernel size of the teacher recovers it from $\mathcal{O}_{D}(D)$ samples, whereas $\mathcal{O}_{D}(D\log(D))$ samples are needed with a larger kernel. For $c > 0$, spatial correlations can reduce the advantage of weight sharing. The asymptotic error of the convolutional student grows with $c$, whereas that of the locally connected student is independent of $c$ and overall lower when evaluated at the same learning rate. When the learning rate is instead tuned to the training horizon, however, the locally connected student wins at early times and the convolutional student at late times, with a transition that we predict analytically.

Self-normalized Bernstein Inequalities for Regenerative Processes and Applications to Learning Without Independence

Imon Banerjee, Pierre Alquier

cs.LG, math.PR, math.ST, stat.TH

We prove a self-normalized Bernstein inequality for bounded functions of a regenerative process with independent, identically distributed marked cycles of finite mean length. The bound uses observed cycle lengths and retains explicit terms for truncation and the cycle ending after the observation horizon. Selecting a tuning parameter from a finite deterministic collection gives a version of Bernstein inequality where all terms are empirical but one, related to the tails of the regeneration times. We then study this term in many regimes: it turns out that it is of second-order in many cases of interest. This is important for applications in machine learning: we prove generalization error bounds for classification with non-i.i.d., regenerative data. These bounds can be used to define learning objectives: by minimizing the first-order term only in the bound, we define agnostic classifiers that still satisfy oracle inequalities. We also show that the bound on the average deviation provided by our results is minimax-optimal in the sample size and in the expected regeneration time. Finally, we combine the Bernstein inequality with a regenerative strong approximation to obtain a Gaussian approximation using an observable block variance estimator.

JaxMARL-BC: Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Macroeconomic Modelling, in Seconds

Daniele Montagnani, Andrea Coletta, Antonietta Mira, Aldo Glielmo

cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.LG, econ.GN, q-fin.EC, Finance, (ICAIF, '26),, Milan,, Italy,, 2026

Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) offers a principled way of populating simulated economies with many heterogeneous decision makers. Its adoption, pioneered in finance, has nonetheless been slow in macroeconomics because of two main limitations: the lack of a shared frame of reference connecting MARL systems to dynamic stochastic general equilibrium (DSGE) models the profession already trusts, and the very long training times. The first limitation was recently addressed by the Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning Business Cycle (MARL-BC), which places deep RL households in a real business cycle economy and reduces to classic DSGE models in specific limits. Here we address the second limitation. We present JaxMARL-BC, a JAX-accelerated simulator that trains MARL-BC economies in seconds (between 75x and 724x faster than the reference implementation), and scales to 200,000 independent learning households. We verify its correctness against the known DSGE limits, characterise its scaling through a simple cost law, and demonstrate its value by a proof-of-concept calibration of an extended model with heterogeneous returns on capital, in a two-hour experiment that would previously have taken over a month. We release JaxMARL-BC as open source.

The Butterfly Effect in LLM Social Simulations: From Architecture Sensitivity to Robustness Audits

Jinyi Ye, Lei Cao, Ding Chen, Emilio Ferrara

cs.MA

A butterfly effect occurs when a local perturbation propagates through a system and produces divergence in its collective outcome. In LLM social simulations, this creates a particular challenge for scientific inference: sensitivity to implementation choices can enter agent interactions and alter the collective outcomes on which scientific claims are based. We study this problem through a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma and a social-media echo chamber, spanning representation- and design-level perturbations across four LLM families. We find that controlled perturbations can substantially alter collective outcomes, with sensitivity varying across both perturbations and models. In the Prisoner's Dilemma, changing only the representation of a matched persona shifts two-agent cooperation by 75.2 percentage points for GPT-5.2, while game framing and memory representation produce smaller or less consistent effects. Multi-agent interaction further reshapes representation sensitivity, producing amplification or attenuation depending on the model and perturbation. In the echo-chamber simulation, persona representation changes interaction targeting and is accompanied by divergence in collective interaction structure, extending representation sensitivity from agent behavior to network-level outcomes. These results show that robustness cannot be inferred from a single simulation configuration and that local sensitivity can change as it propagates through interaction. We introduce TRAILS (Taxonomy for Robustness Audits In LLM Simulations) and the Social Simulation Audit Card as a framework for systematically auditing these dependencies and calibrating the scope of scientific claims to the robustness evidence that supports them.

Implementation of Dynamic Microsegmentation for a Multi-Robot Network

Vishwam Raval, Jaewon Kim, Ravi Pattabhi, Sandip Roy

eess.SY, cs.MA, cs.NI, cs.RO, cs.SY

Parallel AI agents can produce useful local edits yet degrade a shared solution when their edits are combined. We model this coordination problem as discrete block optimization with an exactly evaluable objective and a known dependency graph. Independent sets certify compatible edits whose verified gains, measured against the same current solution, add exactly. A coordinator constructs a safe compatible candidate and can also evaluate interacting edits to capture favorable interactions. We prove that an optimal fractional coloring yields a merger that preserves a $1/\chi_f(G)$ fraction of the total verified local improvement, where $\chi_f(G)$ is the graph's fractional chromatic number. This guarantee is tight among mergers that select a subset of the same proposals. Under conditional local-improvement guarantees and a block error-bound condition, repeated collaboration converges geometrically in the objective gap. The rate separates task structure, agent allocation, and computation cost, yielding a linear program for agent assignment and an explicit time-cost trade-off. Code-repair experiments show that verified merging resolves conflicts between individually useful edits that cause naive merging to fail. On larger conflict-free tasks, parallel agents reduce modeled completion time at higher input-token-equivalent cost. On three public road networks, fractional integration retains 922 units of verified graph-cut improvement across 90 paired runs. We challenge this de facto design with two diagnostic findings. (1) Non-uniform computational need: items within the same sequence vary drastically in how much computation they require. Most converge after shallow processing while a critical minority only stabilizes at full depth. (2) Suboptimality of fixed allocation: given oracle access to the target item, a non-uniform depth assignment consistently outranks the fixed one, indicating that existing uniform computation both wastes capacity on already-converged items and sacrifices overall recommendation quality. These observations motivate a shift from fixed-depth computation to item-level adaptive reasoning. To this end, we propose LoopRec. LoopRec recursively applies a single weight-shared block and introduces a lightweight gate at each recursion boundary that makes a binary exit-or-continue decision for every item independently. Items whose representations have sufficiently matured exit early, while those requiring deeper reasoning continue to subsequent recursions. This design converts the fixed global depth into a learned, per-item property without auxiliary depth labels, and is readily adaptable to diverse backbone architectures. Extensive experiments on public benchmarks with four backbone families demonstrate that LoopRec consistently outperforms matched fixed-depth counterparts. We prove a positive result of a different shape. Fix an innate \emph{recursive contrast operator} $R$ mapping any string to a finite set of structurally bounded perturbations of it, and a discriminative scorer of bounded capacity trained by InfoNCE-style contrastive estimation \citep{vandenoord2018,macollins2018} on self-generated perturbations. Under a faithfulness condition relating operator, scorer, and target, the trained scorer achieves \emph{PAC conditional identification} from positive data alone: with high probability it ranks all but an $\varepsilon$-fraction of positives above every out-of-language perturbation in their own orbit, with sample complexity polynomial in the capacity, the orbit size, the score bound, and $1/\varepsilon$. The negatives are constructed by the learner, not supplied by an oracle, and no global normalizing constant over strings is ever computed. The misranking bound follows from the geometry of the contrastive loss---per-example loss floors plus a definite loss increment under misranking---yielding an $\varepsilon^{-2}$ rate, together with an explicit approximation gap that quantifies the cost of in-language (``colliding'') perturbations and vanishes for orbit-pure operators. The ranking guarantee lifts to a global membership classifier under a margin condition, and the same operator composed with the learned scorer is a normalization-free Metropolis--Hastings generator. Each selected tour must not exceed a prescribed multiple of the optimal tour length, while the maximum pairwise Jaccard similarity within the selected set is minimized. This formulation is important for applications requiring both solution quality and fault tolerance, such as logistics planning, robotics, and strategic patrolling. We propose a novel hybrid framework that decomposes D-TSP into a sampling stage and a selection stage. The selection stage is a max-min dispersion problem, for which any approximation better than a factor of two is NP-hard; we therefore focus on the sampling stage. Our contributions are both theoretical and empirical. Theoretically, we give two end-to-end guarantees for the optimal sampling distribution, from two perspectives: including an optimal set in the sampled pool, or having at least $k$ mutually distant tours in the pool. The second reduces the number of samples from exponential to linear in $k$, at the cost of weakening the benchmark from the best $k$ tours to the best $\beta k$ for some $\beta > 1$. For practical use, we prove that a pipeline that samples spanning trees and then converts to tours does not hurt the quality of the pool in principle. Kulasingham Categories: eess.AS cs.LG Comments: Supplementary results, audio files and the spectrograms are available at the following link: https://github.com/JulianSilva2001/NeuroCodec-TSE ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Autonomous mobile robots operating in dense crowds and cluttered sites require $360^\circ$ perception to react to surrounding hazards. While panoramic LiDAR offers full-surround coverage, learning representations within reinforcement learning (RL) is sample-inefficient under sparse rewards, and existing 3D pretrained point-cloud encoders are too memory-heavy for massively parallel RL simulation. We present RangeNav, a compact (183k parameters) LiDAR range-image encoder pretrained entirely on synthetic data. RangeNav uses anisotropic convolutions and circular padding to handle the extreme aspect ratio and azimuthal seam of range images. We pretrain the network using a navigation-aligned objective combining range reconstruction, semantic segmentation, and bird's-eye-view (BEV) prediction, then freeze it for policy learning. In simulation, adding semantic and BEV supervision increases success rates over a reconstruction-only baseline by an average of 32.03 and 23.73 percentage points (pp) in procedurally generated training and out-of-distribution realistic test environments, respectively. Zero-shot real-world deployments on a Unitree B2-W quadruped demonstrate robust navigation through dense crowds, low-light settings, and construction zones where limited-field-of-view depth policies struggle. Code and models will be open-sourced. arXiv:2610.29864 (*cross-listing*) Date: Wed, 21 Oct 2026 15:20:40 GMT (2367kb) Title: Silent Alignment in Two-Layer Linear Networks: A High-Dimensional Perspective Authors: Simon Martin (DI-ENS, LPENS, SIERRA), Francis Bach (DI-ENS, SIERRA), Giulio Biroli (LPENS) Categories: stat.ML cs.LG ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Machine-learning surrogates in computational science often require not only accurate predictions but also expectations, variances, conserved integrals, and normalization constants. Conventional neural networks, including Kolmogorov--Arnold networks (KANs), generally evaluate these quantities through Monte Carlo sampling or multidimensional quadrature, making repeated inference costly, particularly in high dimensions. We introduce KANs with Tractable Operators for Uncertainty and Constraints (TOUCKANs), whose finite-degree polynomial outer functions yield a decomposable sum--product representation. Under factorizing input measures, expectations and higher moments then reduce algebraically to deterministic recursions over one-dimensional leaf integrals. Independent and shared inner-block variants form a parameter-efficiency hierarchy, and a universal-approximation proof for its most restricted member extends to the complete class. The same deterministic operators allow conserved integrals and normalization to be imposed throughout training. Across uniform and Gaussian benchmarks through 50 dimensions, deterministic mean and variance errors remained below the median fixed-budget Monte Carlo errors in every case. TOUCKANs also conserved Cahn--Hilliard mass to machine precision and imposed normalization and orthogonality through deterministic moments, enabling joint computation of the lowest four Schr\"odinger eigenpairs in two, three, and five dimensions. TOUCKANs therefore connect KAN approximation with tractable probabilistic circuits, making statistical operators and integral constraints directly available within an expressive neural surrogate. arXiv:2610.29960 (*cross-listing*) Date: Wed, 21 Oct 2026 16:17:17 GMT (363kb) Title: Mastering Model Fit: Applications of the InterModel Vigorish for Interpretable Machine Learning Authors: Jiani Yan, Pengyun Wang, Klint Kanopka, Ben Domingue, Charles Rahal Categories: stat.ME cs.LG stat.ML ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Adaptive experiments use bandit algorithms to favor interventions with higher expected rewards. However, decision-makers also require reliable inference on current treatment effects: differences between intervention and reference-arm mean rewards at the present time, rather than historical averages. These objectives conflict: reward maximization favors better interventions, whereas current-effect inference requires continued exploration of potentially suboptimal interventions as outcomes change over time. We characterize this tradeoff for $K$-arm bandits with H\"older-smooth mean rewards of exponent $\beta\in(0,1]$ and constant $L$. Requiring high-probability simultaneous coverage of all current effects by confidence intervals of width at most $w$, we establish matching minimax bounds, up to logarithmic factors, on expected dynamic regret. Over horizon $T$, our algorithm TRACE (Tracking Rewards and Current Effects) achieves regret of order $\widetilde{O}_{\beta}\left(\sqrt{KT}+K^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}L^{1/(2\beta+1)}T^{(\beta+1)/(2\beta+1)}+Kw^{-2}(1+L^{1/\beta}w^{-1/\beta})\right)$, where the notation hides logarithms and constants depending only on $\beta$. This regret--precision tradeoff separates reward-learning regret from the cost of refreshing current-effect estimates, which remains unavoidable even when one arm is uniformly optimal. An empirical-Bernstein e-process extension adapts exploration to realized prediction errors while preserving worst-case guarantees. When within-block drift is negligible relative to sampling error, all observations, including reward-learning observations, can be reused for asymptotically normal pointwise inference and stronger local stationarity yields local asymptotic efficiency. Finally, we show that regret constraints can prevent honest adaptation to unknown smoothness and support our theory with extensive numerical experiments. arXiv:2610.30109 (*cross-listing*) Date: Wed, 21 Oct 2026 17:53:10 GMT (54kb) Title: Minimax Optimality of the NPMLE for Gaussian Mixtures in Hellinger Distance Authors: Shayan Hundrieser and Tudor Manole Categories: math.ST cs.IT cs.LG math.IT stat.TH ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ We give an algorithm for computing an $\eps$-approximate fixed point of any $d$-dimensional nonexpansion with respect to the $\ell_\infty$ norm in time $\log(1/\eps)^{O(\log^2 d)}$.
